abstract_pt: As abordagens atuais de Engenharia de Software não consideram o fator
  humano numa perspetiva individual (ou seja, programador, testador). O erro cognitivo
  humano e as taxonomias cognitivas recentes sobre as causas de erros humanos que
  originam defeitos de software sustentam a ideia intuitiva de que, por exemplo, a
  sobrecarga cognitiva, a fadiga e as distrações são causas humanas importantes de
  falhas no software.Esta tese de doutoramento teve como foco a avaliação da carga
  cognitiva dos programadores durante tarefas específicas de software, incluindo a
  compreensão de código e a inspeção de código para deteção de erros. Um dos principais
  focos foi relativamente à fase de engenharia de características como um passo crítico
  para o desenvolvimento de ferramentas de apoio à engenharia de software, como por
  exemplo, um modelo de avaliação da qualidade de revisão de código, utilizando configurações
  viáveis e compatíveis com ambientes de desenvolvimento de software.Para alcançar
  este objetivo, foram explorados três bio-sinais principais — Eletroencefalografia
  (EEG), Eletrocardiografia (ECG) e Eye-tracking com Pupillografia. O EEG foi investigado
  e proposto como uma possivel referência para medir a carga cognitiva dos programadores,
  oferecendo alta resolução temporal e precisão, sendo particularmente útil em cenários
  onde técnicas de imagem mais complexas, como a fMRI, são impraticáveis. Foram então
  propostos biomarcadores do EEG como referências para validar ou comparar o desempenho
  de medidas fisiológicas menos intrusivas, para suporte de confirmaçao de uma utilização
  mais confiável de sensores menos intrusivos para monitorizar a carga cognitiva durante
  tarefas de desenvolvimento de software.Além da análise do EEG, a variabilidade da
  frequência cardíaca (HRV) foi também explorada como uma abordagem menos intrusiva
  e prática para monitorizar a carga cognitiva. A utilização de características do
  HRV usando janelas de curta duração, particularmente durante tarefas cognitivamente
  exigentes, foi analisada em detalhe. Os resultados indicam que, embora as janela
  de maior duração ofereçam medições mais confiáveis, uma janela de 30 segundos é
  capaz de desempenhar resultados significativos para aplicações práticas. Além disso,
  a análise do movimento ocular com pupillografia foi também investigada para fornecer
  informações sobre a carga cognitiva dos programadores. Esta investigação abordou
  desafios relacionados com a falta de coerência na definição dos limites das bandas
  de frequência da pupila na literatura, propondo limites otimizados para este tipo
  de análise espectral, para uma maior confiança na avaliação da carga cognitiva em
  contextos de engenharia de software.Por fim, esta tese, através de uma análise compreensiva
  e sistemática, propôs e avaliou um modelo multimodal para avaliação da qualidade
  de revisão de código, combinando bio-sinais de diferentes níveis de intrusividade
  com métricas de complexidade de software e dados comportamentais do programador.
  O principal objetivo desta análise foi explorar soluções viáveis e de confiança
  que pudessem ser adotadas em ambientes de desenvolvimento de software. A análise
  dos diferentes modelos treinados permitiu discutir sobre a viabilidade da integração
  de sinais fisiológicos e comportamentais para prever a qualidade das revisões, identificando
  configurações viáveis e práticas adequadas para ambientes reais de desenvolvimento
  de software.Em suma, este trabalho estabelece uma ponte entre métodos de neurociência
  de alta precisão e abordagens menos intrusivas, criando uma base para ferramentas
  práticas de monitorização da carga cognitiva na Engenharia de Software. Estabelece
  pontos fundamentais sobre engenharia de características, fiabilidade de sinais e
  estratégias multimodais para melhorar a qualidade das revisões de código e abordar
  fatores humanos nas práticas de desenvolvimento de software.
abstract_en: Current Software Engineering approaches often overlook the human factor
  at an individual level (i.e., programmer, tester). Cognitive human errors and recent
  taxonomies on the causes of software defects support the intuitive idea that, for
  instance, cognitive overload, fatigue, and attention slips are critical human causes
  for software bugs. Understanding and addressing these cognitive dynamics can improve
  software quality and reliability.This PhD thesis focused on assessing the cognitive
  load of programmers during specific software tasks, including code comprehension
  and code inspection for bug detection. A strong emphasis was placed on the feature
  engineering phase as a critical step toward developing software development tools,
  such as a model for code review quality assessment using viable setups compatible
  with software development environments.To achieve this, three main biosignals were
  explored—Electroencephalography (EEG), Electrocardiography (ECG), and Eye-tracking
  with Pupillography. EEG was explored and proposed as a reliable reference for measuring
  programmers' cognitive load, offering high temporal resolution and precision, and
  being particularly useful in scenarios where more complex imaging techniques, such
  as fMRI, are impractical. EEG biomarkers were then proposed as references for validating
  and comparing the performance of less intrusive physiological measures, towards
  the way of a more confident use of less intrusive sensors, e.g. wearable devices,
  for assessing cognitive load during software development tasks.In addition to EEG
  analysis, heart rate variability (HRV) was also explored as a less intrusive and
  practical approach for monitoring cognitive load. The reliability of ultra-short-term
  HRV features, particularly during cognitively demanding software tasks, was thoroughly
  investigated. The results indicate that while longer time frames generally offer
  more reliable measurements, a 30-second window provided sufficient reliability for
  practical applications. Additionally, eye-tracking with pupillography was explored
  to provide insights into programmers' cognitive load. This research addressed challenges
  related to the absence of well-defined frequency bands in the literature, proposing
  optimized parameters for spectral analysis to enhance reliability in cognitive load
  assessment in software engineering contexts.Finally, this thesis, through a comprehensive
  and systematic analysis, proposed and evaluated a multimodal model for code review
  quality assessment, combining biosignals of varying levels of intrusiveness with
  software complexity metrics and behavioral data. The core goal of this analysis
  was to explore a viable and reliable setup solution that could be adopted in a software
  development environment. The analysis of the different trained models offers insights
  into the feasibility of integrating physiological and behavioral signals to predict
  review quality, identifying viable and practical setups suitable for real-world
  software development environments.In summary, this thesis bridges the gap between
  high-precision neuroscience methods, and less intrusive approaches, building a foundation
  for practical cognitive load monitoring tools in Software Engineering. It provides
  insights into feature engineering and signal reliability and multimodal strategies
  for improving code review quality and addressing human factors in software development
  practices.

Júlio	Cordeiro	Medeiros

PHYSIOLOGICAL	FEATURES

Abstract
C
urrent Software Engineering approaches often overlook the human fac-
tor at an individual level (i.e., programmer, tester). Cognitive human
errors and recent taxonomies on the causes of software defects support
the intuitive idea that, for instance, cognitive overload, fatigue, and attention slips
are critical human causes for software bugs. Understanding and addressing these
cognitive dynamics can improve software quality and reliability.
This PhD thesis focused on assessing the cognitive load of programmers dur-
ing specific software tasks, including code comprehension and code inspection for
bug detection. A strong emphasis was placed on the feature engineering phase as
a critical step toward developing software development tools using viable setups
compatible with software development environments.
To achieve this, three main biosignals were explored - Electroencephalography
(EEG),Electrocardiography(ECG),andEye-trackingwithPupillography. EEGwas
explored and proposed as a reliable reference for measuring programmers’ cognitive
load, offering high temporal resolution and precision, and being particularly useful
in scenarios where more complex imaging techniques, such as fMRI, are impractical.
EEG biomarkers were then proposed as references for validating and comparing the
performance of less intrusive physiological measures, towards the way of a more
confident use of less intrusive sensors, e.g., wearable devices, for assessing cognitive
load during software development tasks.
In addition to EEG analysis, heart rate variability (HRV) was also explored, but
as a less intrusive and practical approach for monitoring cognitive load. The relia-
bility of ultra-short-term HRV features, particularly during cognitively demanding
software tasks, was thoroughly investigated. The results indicate that, while longer
v

time frames generally offer more reliable measurements, a 30-second window pro-
vided sufficient reliability for practical applications. Additionally, eye-tracking with
pupillography was also explored to provide insights into programmers’ cognitive
load. This research addressed challenges related to the absence of well-defined fre-
quency bands in the literature, proposing optimized parameters for spectral analysis
to enhance reliability in cognitive load assessment in software engineering contexts.
Finally, this thesis, through a comprehensive and systematic analysis, proposed
and evaluated a multimodal model for code review quality assessment. This ap-
proach was used to compare the reliability of a comprehensive range of biosensors,
including fMRI, EEG, ECG/HRV, and Eye-tracking with Pupillography, with the
main goal of determining the viability of using low-intrusive biosensors in real setups
to accurately assess the quality of software development tasks.
In summary, this thesis bridges the gap between high-precision neuroscience
methods, and less intrusive approaches, building a foundation for practical cognitive
load monitoring tools in Software Engineering. It provides insights into feature
engineering, signal reliability, and a multimodal strategy for improving code review
quality while addressing human factors in software development practices.
Keywords: Bio-signal Processing, Data Fusion, Supervised Learning, Biofeedback,
Software Engineering, Human error, Software Faults, Code Review
vi

Resumo
A
s abordagens atuais de Engenharia de Software não consideram o fator
humano numa perspetiva individual (ou seja, programador, testador).
O erro cognitivo humano e as taxonomias cognitivas recentes sobre
as causas de erros humanos que originam defeitos de software sustentam a ideia
intuitiva de que, por exemplo, a sobrecarga cognitiva, a fadiga e as distrações são
causas humanas importantes de falhas no software.
Esta tese de doutoramento teve como foco a avaliação da carga cognitiva dos
programadores durante tarefas específicas de software, incluindo a compreensão de
código e a inspeção de código para deteção de erros. Um dos principais focos foi
relativamente à fase de engenharia de características como um passo crítico para
o desenvolvimento de ferramentas de apoio à engenharia de software, como por
exemplo, um modelo de avaliação da qualidade de revisão de código, utilizando
configurações viáveis e compatíveis com ambientes de desenvolvimento de software.
Paraalcançaresteobjetivo, foramexploradostrêsbio-sinaisprincipais-Eletroence-
falografia (EEG), Eletrocardiografia (ECG) e Eye-tracking com Pupillografia. O
EEG foi investigado e proposto como uma possivel referência para medir a carga
cognitiva dos programadores, oferecendo alta resolução temporal e precisão, sendo
particularmente útil em cenários onde técnicas de imagem mais complexas, como
a fMRI, são impraticáveis. Foram então propostos biomarcadores do EEG como
referências para validar ou comparar o desempenho de medidas fisiológicas menos
intrusivas, para suporte de confirmaçao de uma utilização mais confiável de sensores
menos intrusivos para monitorizar a carga cognitiva durante tarefas de desenvolvi-
mento de software.
Além da análise do EEG, a variabilidade da frequência cardíaca (HRV) foi tam-
vii

bém explorada como uma abordagem menos intrusiva e prática para monitorizar
a carga cognitiva. A utilização de características do HRV usando janelas de curta
duração, particularmente durante tarefas cognitivamente exigentes, foi analisada em
detalhe. Os resultados indicam que, embora as janela de maior duração ofereçam
medições mais confiáveis, uma janela de 30 segundos é capaz de desempenhar re-
sultados significativos para aplicações práticas. Além disso, a análise do movimento
ocular com pupillografia foi também investigada para fornecer informações sobre a
carga cognitiva dos programadores. Esta investigação abordou desafios relacionados
com a falta de coerência na definição dos limites das bandas de frequência da pupila
na literatura, propondo limites otimizados para este tipo de análise espectral, para
uma maior confiança na avaliação da carga cognitiva em contextos de engenharia de
software.
Por fim, esta tese, através de uma análise compreensiva e sistemática, propôs e
avaliou um modelo multimodal para avaliação da qualidade de revisão de código.
Esta abordagem foi realizada para comparar a fiabilidade de um amplo conjunto
de biossensores, incluindo fMRI, EEG, ECG/HRV e Eye-tracking com Pupilografia,
com o principal objetivo de determinar a viabilidade do uso de biossensores de baixa
intrusividade em ambientes reais para avaliar com precisão a qualidade das tarefas
de desenvolvimento de software.
Em suma, este trabalho estabelece uma ponte entre métodos de neurociência
de alta precisão e abordagens menos intrusivas, criando uma base para ferramentas
práticas de monitorização da carga cognitiva na Engenharia de Software. Estabelece
pontos fundamentais sobre engenharia de características, fiabilidade de sinais e es-
tratégias multimodais para melhorar a qualidade das revisões de código, abordando
os fatores humanos nas práticas de desenvolvimento de software.
Palavras-chave: Processamento de Biossinais, Fusão de Dados, Aprendizagem Su-
pervisionada, Biofeedback, Engenharia de Software, Erro Humano, Fal-
has de Software, Revisão de Código

xix

Chapter 1
Introduction
S
oftware has become an integral part of almost every aspect of our soci-
ety. From the highly visible consumer products by industry leaders like
Microsoft, Google, IBM, Apple, Amazon, and Meta, to the essential but
less obvious software used in sectors such as automotive and healthcare, software
development is now one of the largest industries in the world. The global software
market was valued at approximately USD 736.96 billion in 2024 and is projected
to reach around USD 2,248.33 billion by 2034, with a predicted compound annual
growth rate (CAGR) of 11.8% from 2024 to 2034 [1]. Ensuring software quality and
reliability has, therefore, become an even more critical concern.
Despite all research and advances in software engineering and software reliability,
every year there is a considerable financial loss in the software industry due to
software defects (i.e., bugs). These bugs represent the most enduring problem of
software quality, and the number of defects per 1000 lines of delivered code (KLoC)
remains extremely high. Steve McConnell’s seminal book [2] points to an industry
average of about 15 defects per KLoCs with a very large standard deviation. Even
when software is developed using highly mature processes, the deployed code still
has relatively high defect density, from 1 to 5 bugs per KLoC [3–6]. More recent
field data from several projects show defects densities from 1 to 6 bugs per KLoC [7],
suggesting that in real projects, often characterized by millions of lines of code, the
defect density remains quite significant and represents a huge cost for the software
industry and society in general.
Subsequently, reports on the financial impact of software defects and software
failures show dramatic figures. The global cost of debugging software defects was
estimated as $312 billion in 2013 [8]. In 2016, the cost of software failures for the
worldwide economy reached $1.1 trillion [9], while “software failures affected 3.6
billion people in 2017, causing US$1.7 trillion in financial losses” [10], reflecting a
clear and worrying upward trend. More recently, a 2022 report by the Consortium
for Information and Software Quality (CISQ) found that in the United States alone,
1

2 CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION
poor software quality led to $2.41 trillion in losses [11], highlighting that software
defects remain a massive financial burden for businesses and society.
An additional recent concern is the rapid growth of AI-generated code using
large language models (LLMs), such as GitHub Copilot and OpenAI’s ChatGPT,
which can further increase the amount of software defects. While LLMs may provide
accurate code for simple tasks, their accuracy tends to degrade with more complex
requirements (i.e., those that fall outside of the training space) [12]. This can lead
to a wide range of potential bugs, particularly since the code generated by LLMs
can be difficult for humans to understand, making this an additional challenge in
the process of debugging and fixing such code. This is a common perception among
developers, corroborated by empirical studies such as [13], where they report the
questionnaire results showing that 80% of the users often or always experience bugs
when using LLMs to generate code.
Given the high costs of software defects and the evolving nature of code gen-
eration, early bug detection is more crucial than ever. It is well known that the
cost of finding and solving/patching software bugs increases exponentially, depend-
ing on the phase of the software lifecycle when the bug is found, and reaches values
two orders of magnitude higher when the bug is found in the production phase
(i.e., in deployed software products), compared to bugs found in the requirements
phase [14]. This highlights the critical importance of identifying bugs as early as
possible in the development process to avoid the potentially huge impact of bugs in
deployed software.
One key strategy for early bug detection is related to the code review process.
Although code reviews address multiple aspects, one of the main goals remains the
detection of bugs. It is an essential checkpoint for finding software bugs before they
proceed to later stages of the software lifecycle, and therefore ensure the reliability
and functionality of the software.
However, not all software defects are detected during code reviews. A survey by
Shull et al. [15] on classic code reviews found that, on average, code reviews detect
about 60% of software defects, with effectiveness ranging from as low as 19% to
as high as 93%, depending on factors such as review methodology and developer
expertise [15]. Moreover, with the shift from formal, classic code reviews to more
informal modern code reviews, it is expected that the effectiveness of bug detection
will be lower than the reported average of 60% in the survey [15]. For example, a
study by Kononenko et al. [16] on Mozilla’s modern code review process found that
54% of reviews failed to detect bugs in approved commits.
Theselimitationshighlighttheneedforadeeperunderstandingofthehumanfac-
tors that can impact software quality throughout the development lifecycle. While
traditional software engineering research has focused extensively on process improve-
ments and tools to assist developers throughout the software lifecycle, there has been
less emphasis on the cognitive aspects of software developers’ tasks (cognitive over-

1.1. MOTIV ATION 3
load, fatigue, attention shifts, and stress), which can lead to human errors [17–19].
Recent studies have begun exploring how biosignal measures can be used to monitor
developers’ cognitive load during software development process tasks. These tools
offer a promising way to obtain real-time insights and use such information to im-
prove software quality by providing feedback to the developer on code regions that
may need a second look or require a more thorough software inspection or testing.
1.1 Motivation
The concept of using biosignal measures, such as Heart Rate Variability (HRV),
Respiratory Responses, Pupillary Response (Pupillography) or Electrodermal ac-
tivity (EDA), to gather information on cognitive load during specific tasks is not
new [20–22]. However, only in recent years the use of biosignal measures was pro-
posed for the context of software development. Foundational research works have
shown that biosignal measures can be linked to task difficulty or difficulty in com-
prehending code snippets [23], and that HRV, among other biosignals, can be a
potential tool to predict the quality of the code made by programmers, which is
useful to optimize software testing [24].
Recent studies proposed a broader idea named Biofeedback Augmented Software
Engineering [25] to integrate biosignal measures such as HRV and pupillography
[26] in the software development process through the use of cognitive state code
annotations [27]. The proposal consists of using eye tracking devices, particularly
non-intrusive desktop eye trackers, to provide accurate information on the code lines
where the programmer is looking at, and annotate such code lines/blocks with the
programmer’s cognitive state (expressing high levels of cognitive load, distractions,
fatigue, etc.) gathered using HRV and pupillography [27]. Such code annotations
are expected to allow predictions of code quality in order to provide online warnings
to the programmer or to guide code reviews or the software testing effort in general.
Importantly, the first studies [23–27] have shown that programmers’ cognitive
load during typical code development activities can be assessed using biosignal
measures obtained through low-intrusive devices compatible with standard soft-
ware development environments. Although quite encouraging, all these recent stud-
ies [23–27] have used minimally intrusive technology to infer programmer’s cognitive
load while carrying out software development tasks, and all of them share an inher-
ent limitation, i.e., the programmers’ cognitive load are inferred indirectly, through
peripheral physiological signals driven by the autonomic nervous system (ANS) (e.g.,
HRV, pupillography, EDA, among others. This raises the question on the accuracy
and precision of the cognitive load assessed in that way. Exploring and overcom-
ing these challenges is essential for understanding their viability as practical and
accurate tools for everyday use in software engineering.
These significant limitations of the initial proposals for using biosignal measures

4 CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION
to improve software quality could be mitigated by exploring and proposing a ground
truth to evaluate or compare the accuracy of low-intrusive devices that rely on
physiological manifestations driven by the ANS. Therefore, it is essential to first
explore more reliable and sensitive signals, such as Electroencephalography (EEG),
to establish potential ground truth biomarkers, which can serve as a reference for
evaluating and comparing the accuracy of other ANS-related sensors.
Therefore, to address this challenge, we propose a research trajectory that be-
gins by establishing the viability of EEG as a neuroscience reference for assessing
cognitive load during software development tasks. Building upon the foundational
study, we further explore the EEG and demonstrate how EEG can serve as a vi-
able surrogate for fMRI in scenarios where high-precision cognitive assessments are
needed but heavy imaging setups are impractical. These efforts include identifying
robust EEG biomarkers correlated with well-known brain regions activated during
high-cognitive-demand tasks, such as software inspection tasks.
Building on this, we also explore the feasibility of using heart rate variability
(HRV) as a less intrusive, real-time alternative for monitoring cognitive load, par-
ticularly under cognitively demanding scenarios where ultra-short-term HRV fea-
tures can present challenges regarding reliability. Moreover, we investigate the use
of pupillography to refine cognitive load assessment, identifying optimal frequency
bands for cognitive load monitoring that enhance precision while ensuring compati-
bility with real-world software development environments.
Finally, integrating insights from a multimodality study on code review quality
assessment, our research aims to consolidate EEG, fMRI, HRV, and Eye-tracking
with pupillography, combined with the programmers’ profile and software metrics,
into a comprehensive framework. This analysis focuses on finding a balance between
precision and practicality, by comparing high-intrusiveness sensors like fMRI and
EEG with cost-effective, industry-ready solutions like ECG and Eye-Tracking with
Pupillography. By comparing the results obtained from the different biosensors,
this analysis offers insights into the best alternatives for integrating low-intrusive
biosensors into real-world software development practices.
In summary, this thesis explores the following key research questions:
• "Can EEG be used to establish reliable biomarkers of cognitive load during soft-
ware development tasks? And, how does it compare to traditional metrics and
other physiological modalities in assessing cognitive load and perceived complex-
ity?"
• "How can physiological responses from the autonomic nervous system (ANS),
such as HRV and pupillography, be used to assess, in a reliable and precise way,
the cognitive load during software development tasks? For that purpose, what
are the optimal parameters for monitoring and biofeedback applications in this
context?"

1.1. INTRODUCTION 5
• "How can multimodal biosensor data, incorporating sensors with varying levels
of intrusiveness (EEG, fMRI, HRV, eye-tracking, and pupillography), be effec-
tively combined with programmer proficiency and software complexity metrics
to achieve an accurate and effective machine learning model for assessing code
review quality? Importantly, what is the best viable and feasible low-intrusive
setupfordeployingsuchtoolsinreal-worldindustrysettingstomaximizesoftware
quality and effectiveness?"
1.2 Contributions
This thesis contributes to the interdisciplinary fields of Neuroscience, Biomedical
Engineering and Software Engineering by addressing four fundamental aspects:
1. Through the use of EEG, this thesis explores the neural patterns as-
sociated with cognitive load during software tasks. This aspect focuses
on EEG as a reliable ground truth for assessing programmers’ cognitive load
in software engineering activities. The key contributions to this aspect are:
• Proposed the very first approach to use EEG as a reference to evaluate
and compare the accuracy of less intrusive devices that rely on ANS-driven
physiological manifestations for the software engineering context.
• Demonstrated the viability of using EEG-derived features to assess the cog-
nitive load of programmers during code comprehension with varying com-
plexities, showingconsistencywiththeprogrammers’subjectiveperceptions
of complexity and mental effort.
• Confirmed the limitations of traditional software complexity metrics, re-
vealing that programmers’ perceived code complexity saturates faster than
the metrics suggest, and emphasizes that, on existing software development
practices, the use of complexity metrics by the software industry (as a code
complexity indicator during development) must be revisited to include both
complexity metrics and programmers’ perceived code complexity measures.
• Proposed the EEG as a more practical alternative to fMRI for cognitive
load assessment, highlighting its cost-effectiveness, comfort, and ease of
participant recruitment for software engineering activities.
• Identified robust EEG features correlated with Insula activation (linked to
software development activities) and evaluates prior EEG features proposed
in the software domain, providing insights for developing more practical or
the use of wearable EEG solutions using fewer electrodes for this context.
• Addressed the hemodynamic delay challenge in the hemodynamic response
function (HRF) for multimodal EEG-fMRI studies, contributing to a more
accurate understanding of brain activity during software development tasks.

raphy, to assess cognitive load during software development tasks.
This aspect explores non-intrusive, real-life applicable sensors such as ECG
and eye-tracking with pupillography. The key contributions to this aspect are:
• Demonstrated the reliability of ultra-short-term HRV measurements dur-
ing cognitively demanding tasks, establishing valid time frames for HRV
analysis in software tasks such as code inspection and bug detection.
• Identified critical HRV features that remain stable within short time win-
dows, showing the potential for real-time biofeedback applications to mon-
itor cognitive load from software programmers.
• Addressed the lack of clear definitions for the low frequency (LF) and high
frequency (HF) bands in pupillography signal frequency domain analysis,
proposing new optimal frequency bands crucial for using eye-tracking with
pupillography in cognitive load assessment during software development.
• Designed, collected, and incorporated a secondary dataset focusing on cog-
nitive load assessment through mental arithmetic tasks, offering additional
insights for defining optimal frequency limits.

solution, combined with software complexity metrics and program-
mer proficiency, for assessing code review quality using shallow ma-

industry settings. The key contributions to this aspect are:
• Performed cross-inspection and evaluation of cognitive load features from
different biosensors. This evaluation assessed the trade-off between sensor

10 CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION
1.4 Thesis Structure
The structure of this document is organized as follows.
Chapter 2 provides a comprehensive overview of the background concepts essen-
tial for this research, including software engineering concepts, cognitive load defi-
nition and assessment, human nervous system, and neurophysiological monitoring
techniques.
Chapter 3 presents a literature review of studies that explore neurophysiological
signals in the software engineering context, namely functional imaging-based stud-
ies, brain bioelectrical activity-based studies, and ANS bioelectrical activity-based
studies.
Chapter 4 describes the datasets collected and introduces the ones explored in
this thesis.
Chapter 5 investigates central nervous system responses in software engineering
tasks, focusing on EEG-based analysis of cognitive load during code comprehension
and code inspection software development activities.
Chapter 6 explores autonomic nervous system-related signals, including HRV
and pupillography, to assess cognitive load during software inspection tasks.
Chapter 7 presents a multimodal biosensor framework to identify a viable and
effective biosensor setup solution for assessing the effectiveness of complex software
development tasks.
Chapter 8 concludes the thesis, by providing an overview of the main findings
and future directions.

Chapter 2
Background Concepts
This chapter provides a brief overview of the key concepts related to the research
presented in this thesis. It begins by exploring core software engineering princi-
ples, focusing on the software code complexity metrics, software quality and modern
code reviews, and human factors that influence development processes. Next, it
introduces the concept of cognitive load and the emerging field of NeuroSE, which
integrates neurophysiological measurements to assess cognitive load in software en-
gineering tasks. This chapter also addresses the fundamentals of the human ner-
vous system and the physiological basis for the biosignals explored in this work.
Finally, it highlights the core neurophysiological techniques of this research, such
as Electroencephalography (EEG), Electrocardiography (ECG), and Eye-Tracking
with Pupillography, highlighting their relevancy in analyzing cognitive load during
software-related activities.
2.1 Code Complexity Metrics and Software Quality
Software engineering has long relied on static software metrics to evaluate and im-
prove software quality. There are software metrics for almost every element and
phase of the software development process. However, in the context of this thesis,
the most relevant are the code complexity metrics, which focus on the structural
characteristics of the code itself. These metrics have been widely applied to tasks
such as guiding testing coverage, estimating defect density, and determining compo-
nent granularity in software architectures [28]. Among the many classic complexity
metrics available, two well-known examples are McCabe’s Cyclomatic Complexity
(V(G)) [29] and Halstead’s metrics [30]. McCabe’s Cyclomatic Complexity, for in-
stance, quantifies the number of linearly independent paths within a program. It is
calculated based on the control flow graph of the program, where decisions (e.g., if
and while statements) contribute to the complexity. This metric serves both as an
indicator of code complexity and as a guideline for testing, specifying the minimum
11

12 CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND
number of test cases needed to ensure full path coverage [29].
Halstead’s metrics, on the other hand, focus on the size and effort involved in
a program’s implementation. Halstead introduced measures such as the number
of unique operators (e.g., +, if, while) and operands (variables or constants) in a
program, along with derived measures such as program length, volume, difficulty,
and effort. These measures estimate the cognitive effort required to comprehend or
modify a piece of code, providing a valuable perspective beyond structural complex-
ity [30].
Other examples of code-related metrics include the average number of nested
block depths, the number of parameters per method, and total lines of code (LOC)
[31]. Beyond code-centric metrics, there are also documentation-related metrics,
useful to evaluate the quality and maintainability of software documentation. Ex-
amples include comments readability (CR) and comments and identifiers consistency
(CIC) [32], which ensure that comments align with the code structure, thereby sup-
porting code comprehension. In addition, there are also developer-related metrics
used to assess programmer expertise and experience, being often quantified by the
number of years spent coding or working with specific programming languages [33].
Although static metrics have been widely adopted in software industry, code
complexity has long been seen as a major cause of software bugs. Intuition and
experience suggest that humans inherently struggle with highly abstract and com-
plex tasks, which are prevalent in software development. Maybe this explains the
reason why software engineering, as a discipline, has had a strong focus on software
complexity metrics from the very beginning to mitigate errors and improve maintain-
ability. A classic example is the use of McCabe’s Cyclomatic Complexity (V(G)) in
testing and development processes. Many software companies adopt thresholds, such
as V(G) ≤ 10, to limit module complexity, assuming that highly complex modules
are more error-prone, harder to test, and challenging to maintain. These thresholds
are even recommended by standard organizations like NIST (National Institute of
Standards and Technology) [29]. However, it is known that complexity metrics may
deviate from human perceived complexity in code structures such as recursive or
multi-threading programming, as in these cases the complexity is not in the code
structures (usually very compact) but in the recursive and/or parallel nature of the
code [34]. Moreover, recent studies have been reporting that traditional complexity
metrics often deviate significantly from human-perceived complexity [25,35,36].
Alternative complexity metrics have also been explored to better align with hu-
man cognitive aspects in understanding code. One notable example is Cognitive
Complexity (CC Sonar), introduced by SonarSource and integrated into Sonar-
Qube [37,38]. To address V(G) limitations, CC Sonar was designed to reflect code
understandability by penalizing control flow disruptions instead of counting branch-
ing points. However, while CC Sonar has been explored and used in the software
industry, its effectiveness remains inconclusive. While some studies suggest that it

2.1. CODE COMPLEXITY METRICS AND SOFTW ARE QUALITY 13
offers potential advantages over traditional complexity metrics [39], other studies
report little or no improvement over V (G) in predicting human-perceived under-
standability [40,41].
In an interdisciplinary perspective, considering cognitive psychology on human
error [42], it may seem naive to assume that complexity is the single relevant cause
for human errors in software development. Human cognitive and physiological fac-
tors obviously play a critical role in software quality. More precisely, some studies
have shown that software bugs can frequently be caused by cognitive overload, stress,
distraction, and the inherent difficulty of comprehending complex code [17,18,43].
For example, Scalabrino et al. [33,44] demonstrated that traditional metrics fail to
capture the subjective understandability of code as perceived by developers. Com-
plementary research has shown that cognitive load, as assessed through physiological
measuressuchasEEG,HRV,andPupillography, alignsmorecloselywithdevelopers’
perceived complexity than static metrics alone [25,35]. These studies highlight the
importance of incorporating developer-centric data into software engineering strate-
gies to address the limitations of traditional approaches and better understand the
human element in software development.
Additionally, the emergence of AI-assisted tools, such as GitHub Copilot and
OpenAI’s ChatGPT, has further emphasized the importance of human cognitive
processes in software quality. While Large Language Models (LLMs) have demon-
strated the support and ability to generate software code for simple tasks, the perfor-
mance of these models deteriorates with more complex tasks [12]. Recent empirical
studies have revealed that AI-generated code often introduces distinct types of bugs
that differ from human-made errors. A study by Tambon et al. [13] reported that
80% of developers frequently encounter bugs when using AI tools, while another
study from Stanford University [45] observed that developers relying on AI code
generation tools may produce less secure software code compared to those who do
not use. These findings reinforce the additional need for rigorous human review
processes to ensure the reliability and functionality of AI-generated code. Thus, the
human in the loop, whether as a developer or a reviewer, becomes even more crucial
in ensuring the quality and security of the software.
These insights emphasize that static software metrics alone are insufficient to
address the multifaceted challenges of software quality. While such metrics provide
a foundational understanding of code complexity and defect-proneness, they fail to
capture the dynamic cognitive and physiological state of developers during soft-
ware tasks. Even code review, a widely adopted practice, partially addresses these
challenges by fostering collaboration, iterative feedback, and collective code owner-
ship. However, code review processes would also benefit from explicitly considering
human factors such as expertise, cognitive load, and attention shifts. Studies com-
bining cognitive psychology and physiological assessments have demonstrated the
value of integrating developer-centric data into software engineering processes. By

14 CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND
complementing software development tasks with insights from the cognitive load,
attention shifts, or perceived complexity, modern software engineering can achieve
a more holistic understanding of software quality along the software development
process.
2.2 Code Review and Modern Practices
Giventheimportanceofhumanoversight, codereviewsplayacriticalroleinensuring
software quality in the software development process, for example, by identifying
and fixing bugs before software deployment [46]. Typically, a reviewer examines
the code written by another developer to find errors, to ensure the requirements
to coding standards, and to improve code quality. This process involves reading
through the code, understanding its functionality, and identifying issues, such as
those that may lead to software failures, while also ensuring adherence to coding
standards, improving code readability, and enhancing overall maintainability [47].
The effectiveness of code reviews depends on several factors, including the clarity
and constructiveness of feedback, the expertise and domain knowledge of reviewers,
and effective communication between reviewers and authors [48]. Adherence to cod-
ing standards, attention to code readability and maintainability, and architectural
considerations also play significant roles. Social and personal factors, such as the
reviewer’s workload, experience, and participation in discussions, further influence
review quality [48].
Reviews can take various forms, from classic Fagan’s inspections [49], involv-
ing multiple reviewers and structured meetings, to the more recent and lightweight
Modern Code Reviews (MCR) [50,51], where a peer developer provides feedback.
Modern code review strategy has been largely adopted and become a key element
of software quality assurance, enabling developers to detect bugs, improve code
quality, and share knowledge within teams in a smoother way. Unlike traditional
reviews, which focused on formal inspections, MCR emphasizes lightweight and
continuous processes, supported by tools such as Gerrit and GitHub’s pull requests
[50]. These tools allow asynchronous communication, making code reviews more
scalable and accessible, especially in distributed teams. Companies like Google,
for example, use MCR extensively within their development processes. Sadowski
et al. [51] studied Google’s code review practices and highlighted their dual focus
on defect detection and fostering collaboration. MCR promotes a clear collective
code ownership and mentoring, enabling teams to share knowledge and maintain a
consistent understanding of the codebase.
Nevertheless, despite these advantages, MCR still presents challenges. Once
again, by being a human task, it heavily relies on human reviewers, whose perfor-
mance can vary depending on cognitive state shifts, and expertise. Therefore, there
is a need for tools and methods that account for human factors, such as integrating

2.3. COGNITIVE LOAD AND NEUROSE 15
cognitive load monitoring into MCR workflows.
In addition to these inherent challenges of software development, the rise of
AI-assisted development tools, such as GitHub Copilot, adds another dimension to
MCR. AI-generated code often requires careful review to ensure security, maintain-
ability, and correctness, as errors introduced by AI models can differ significantly
from those written by humans [12,45]. This further highlights the critical role of
the human reviewer as the final safeguard in the code review process, ensuring that
AI-generated artifacts meet the required quality standards.
One promising approach is the assessment of cognitive load by recording neu-
rophysiological signals, which can provide a deeper understanding of the mental
effort exerted by software programmers. By measuring cognitive load, researchers
and programmers in software engineering can identify and mitigate these cognitive
bottlenecks, thereby improving software quality. This forms the foundation of the
emerging NeuroSE field, which integrates neurophysiological methods and insights
into the software engineering field.
Integrating neurophysiological insights into MCR can further improve this pro-
cess. For instance, monitoring reviewers’ cognitive load and attention levels can help
identifying periods of decreased attention or performance, suggesting interventions
such as task redistribution or enforced breaks. By aligning MCR practices with hu-
man cognitive capabilities, these insights can offer software industry a more reliable,
effective, and efficient code reviews in real-world industry settings.
2.3 Cognitive Load and NeuroSE
The concept of assessing the cognitive condition of individuals is not new and has
been extensively explored across various fields over the past few decades [20–22,52].
However, the assessment of cognitive load in the context of software development
has only recently been introduced, focusing particularly on software programmers
as they perform development tasks.
When it comes to human information processing, two key components play a
central role, the working memory (WM) and the long-term memory (LTM). The
WM has a limited capacity, and people are purely conscious of the information that
is held in the WM [53]. On the other hand, LTM has unlimited storage and organizes
knowledge in a way that reduces the load on WM, helping to process information
more efficiently [54].
Building on these ideas, Cognitive Load Theory (CLT) was introduced by Sweller
in 1988 [55]. It explains how information flows between WM and LTM and how orga-
nizing and grouping information can influence WM’s capacity and improve learning.
It is when the WM capacity limit is approached that humans experience cogni-
tive overload, which can negatively impact their performance and lead to human
errors [56].

16 CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND
CLT defines cognitive load as the total mental effort needed to process infor-
mation and complete a task. According to CLT, cognitive load can be divided into
three types: intrinsic load (related to the inherent complexity of the task), extra-
neous load (related to how information is presented), and germane load (related to
learning and problem-solving) [55,57]. Together, these types form the total cognitive
load of a task. In Software Engineering, particularly within the emerging NeuroSE
research field, the term ’cognitive load’ is often broadly used to describe the overall
mental effort exerted by software programmers during task performance. It is some-
times referred to more generally as mental effort or cognitive stress [19,58,59]. In
this work, the broader definition of cognitive load is adopted, referring to the total
mental effort exerted during software-related tasks.
Understanding and assessing cognitive load can provide significant advantages
to the software engineering field by enhancing task performance and reducing po-
tential costs associated with errors and inefficiencies. Cognitive load can be assessed
through subjective measures, such as self-report questionnaires like the NASA-Task
Load Index (NASA-TLX) [60], performance metrics such as accuracy and response
times, and physiological measures such as autonomic nervous system (ANS) re-
sponses. Physiological measures, in particular, are the most promising, as they
enable reliable, non-intrusive, and real-time monitoring of a programmer’s cognitive
load, making them a key element in improving the software development process.
The advancements in the recent research field in software engineering, named
NeuroSE, have demonstrated the feasibility of applying neurophysiological meth-
ods to assess software developers’ cognitive conditions during software activities.
Techniques such as Electroencephalography (EEG), functional Magnetic Resonance
Imaging (fMRI), functional Near-Infrared spectroscopy (fNIRs), and autonomic ner-
vous system activity measurements, including heart rate variability (HRV), pupil
variation, respiration, temperature and skin conductance, can be explored to provide
objective and continuous insights into developers’ mental conditions while perform-
ing software engineering tasks [61]. These methods can offer a deeper understanding
of cognitive load, stress, and attention shifts that cannot be captured solely through
subjective measures like self-reports.
By combining cognitive load assessment with neurophysiological measurements,
NeuroSE field provides a foundation for improving software development processes.
This emerging field highlights the dynamic interplay between software processes and
developers’ cognitive processes, offering opportunities to design tools and workflows
that adapt to developers’ mental demands in real-time. Such advancements can
open the doors to the development and deployment of valuable tools for enhancing
software quality, reducing human error, and supporting developers in complex and
cognitively intensive tasks.
This ability to measure cognitive load relies on the human nervous system, which
generates and transmits the physiological signals captured by neurophysiological

2.4. HUMAN NER VOUS SYSTEM 17
techniques such as EEG, fMRI, HRV, and skin conductance. The next section
provides an overview of the human nervous system, followed by an overview of the
neurophysiological monitoring techniques.
2.4 Human Nervous System
The human nervous system forms the foundation for understanding the neurophys-
iological measurements used to assess cognitive load in software engineering tasks.
The nervous system is one of the most complex systems in the human body. This
system controls everything, physiologic needs, muscular responses and reflexes. It
works through the transmission of electrical signals, where stimuli received by the
body are processed into specific output responses.
The constant series of electrical signals generated by the neuronal cells (neurons)
is a product of changes in the neurons’s permeability to the ions Na+ and K+. When
a stimulus is applied, the depolarization of the neuron’s membrane, which was at
rest (with a resting potential of -70 mV), is triggered, initiating an action potential.
This electrical-chemical gradient propagates along neuron chains through a repeated
series of depolarizations, repolarizations, and hyperpolarizations, traveling from the
stimulus site to the brain. The brain then processes the signals and generates a
specific electrical or chemical response that produces an output [62].
The nervous system is divided into two parts: the central nervous system (CNS)
and the peripheral nervous system (PNS). The CNS, which includes the brain and
spinal cord, serves as the control center where stimuli are interpreted and responses
are generated. The PNS consists of the nerves branching from the CNS to the rest of
the body, carrying signals to and from the brain. This system ensures the execution
ofresponses, suchasthesecretionofspecificchemicalsormusclecontractions[62,63].
Regarding the brain, it is possible to divide it into Frontal, Temporal, Parietal,
and Occipital lobes. Each of these lobes, depicted in Fig. 2.1, has distinct func-
tions [64]. The frontal lobe is involved in motor actions, working memory, and con-
scious thought processes. The temporal lobe is primarily associated with auditory
processing and olfactory functions. The parietal lobe plays a key role in integrat-
ing sensory information from multiple senses, while the occipital lobe is primarily
responsible for visual processing [64,65].
The Peripheral System is divided into two parts, the Sensory division and the
Motor Division. As the names say, the first includes the afferent pathways and is
responsible for the acquisition of sensory stimuli, the second one, the Motor Division,
is composed by the efferent pathways and is responsible to send the response to the
final target, that can be for example a muscle or gland. The Motor division can also
be parted into the somatic nervous system (SNS) and the autonomic nervous system
(ANS). The SNS controls voluntary contractions, big muscle movements, and the
ANS is responsible for involuntary functions, such as breathing, heart beat, digestion

18 CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND
Figure 2.1: Division of the cortex into four lobes: frontal, temporal, parietal and occipital.
Extracted from [66].
or temperature control. At last, the ANS can be subdivided in two systems, the
Parasympathetic and the Sympathetic System [63], with opposite functions, the first
is responsible for alarming the body and the second one instigates body relaxation.
Together, these subsystems are responsible for sensations as stress, relaxation, fear
or panic [62,67]. In figure 2.2 we can see that both subsystems control two of the
muscles of major focus in this study, i.e., the heart muscle (HRV analysis) and the
muscles of the iris that control the constriction and dilation of the pupil (pupil size
analysis).
Building on the understanding of the human nervous system, a range of neu-
rophysiological techniques have been developed to measure the signals it gener-
ates, providing insights into the cognitive load of individuals. Techniques such as
Electroencephalography (EEG) directly capture electrical brain activity by measur-
ing neuronal voltage fluctuations, whereas functional Magnetic Resonance Imaging
(fMRI) and functional Near-Infrared Spectroscopy (fNIRS) measure hemodynamic
responses, providing indirect indicators of neural activation based on changes in
blood flow and oxygenation. In parallel, autonomic nervous system (ANS)-related
signals, including Heart Rate Variability (HRV), Electrodermal Activity (EDA), res-
piratory patterns, and pupillary responses, serve as indirect physiological markers
of cognitive load, reflecting variations in sympathetic and parasympathetic activity.
Within the context of NeuroSE, these neurophysiological monitoring techniques
arecrucialforassessingsoftwaredevelopers’cognitiveloadwhileperformingsoftware
tasks [23,69]. While this thesis primarily focuses on EEG, ECG, and Eye-Tracking
with Pupillography, it is important to highlight the broader spectrum of techniques
available in cognitive load research.
The following table (Table 2.1) provides a comparative overview of neurophysio-
logical techniques, summarizing their primary strengths and limitations for assessing
cognitive load in software engineering tasks [61,70–77].

2.5 Neurophysiological Monitoring Techniques
This section provides an overview of the key neurophysiological techniques explored
in this thesis to assess cognitive load during software tasks. The focus is on three
primary techniques: Electroencephalography (EEG) for direct measurement of brain
activity, Electrocardiography (ECG) for deriving Heart Rate Variability (HRV) as
an autonomic nervous system biomarker, and Eye-Tracking with Pupillography for
monitoring ocular and pupillary responses. These techniques allows a deeper under-
standing of the physiological mechanisms and patterns underlying cognitive load in
software engineering contexts, providing insights for developing solutions that can
enhance programmer performance, optimize software environments, and improve
code quality.
