2.5.1 Electroencephalography
Human EEG recording began in 1924 with the pioneering work of German psychi-
atrist Hans Berger [78]. Since then, EEG has become a crucial tool in neurology
and clinical neurophysiology [79]. Its ability to record the brain’s electrical activity
makes EEG particularly valuable in cognitive research, as it offers direct insights into
neural mechanisms associated with mental effort and complexity of the task [80].
EEG measures the electrical activity of the brain using electrodes that detect
voltage fluctuations caused by the summed activations of thousands of neurons [79].
This activity can be recorded minimally invasively using surface electrodes placed on
the scalp or invasively using intracranial electrodes. Surface EEG, the method used
in this work, is the most widely adopted due to its non-invasive nature, portability,
and cost-effectiveness, making it suitable for a variety of studies and applications
[81,82]. It provides a broad perspective on brain activity, though it has limitations,
such as reduced bandwidth due to signal filtering by the bone, and contamination
from electrical and muscular artifacts and noise, which can negatively impact the
analysis and, consequently, obscure valuable insights [83].
There are several standard configurations for the electrode’s location when using
surface EEG, e.g., the well-known International 10-10 or 10-20 System configura-

2.5. NEUROPHYSIOLOGICAL MONITORING TECHNIQUES 21
tions. The difference between the configurations is due to the distance between
adjacent electrodes. This distance corresponds to a percentage of the total distance
from front to back or left to right side of the head. For better visualization, Fig. 2.3
depicts an example of the International 10-20 System.
Figure 2.3: Configuration of the placements of the electrodes using the International 10-20 System
(adapted from Malmivuo et al. [84]).
The EEG electrodes are labeled based on their position on the scalp, with the
first letter indicating the associated brain region (e.g., "F" for Frontal, "T" for Tem-
poral, "P" for Parietal, "O" for Occipital, "C" for Central). Additional labels specify
whether the electrode is located on the midline sagittal plane ("z") or in the left
(odd numbers) or right (even numbers) hemispheres. The EEG signals recorded are
relative measurements, representing the potential difference between the recording
electrode and a reference electrode, typically placed near Cz or Pz.
For data interpretation, EEG signals are commonly analyzed by decomposing
them into five main frequency bands: Delta (<4 HZ), Theta (4-8 Hz), Alpha (8-13
Hz), Beta (13-30 Hz) and Gamma (>30 Hz) [85]. In the literature, These frequency
bands are widely used for feature extraction in studies assessing mental states, in-
cluding cognitive load [65].
EEG’s ability to directly measure brain activity makes it a highly versatile tool
for cognitive research, particularly in controlled environments. EEG is versatile and
accessible for cognitive research. While fMRI is often restricted to highly controlled
environments due to its high costs, lack of portability, and intrusive setup require-
ments, EEG is less intrusive and provides a practical alternative for a variety of
experimental designs [86].
Additionally, compared to other techniques like fMRI, which offers superior spa-
tial resolution and detailed insights into localized brain activity, EEG’s high tem-
poral resolution is another relevant advantage for capturing rapid cognitive load
changes, making it a valuable tool for analyzing rapid and dynamic task demands.
Furthermore, advances in technology have also introduced wearable and portable

22 CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND
EEG systems, which expand their potential use beyond controlled experiments. De-
spite limitations such as signal contamination and reduced spatial resolution, EEG
is a powerful tool to investigate the neural patterns associated with cognitively
demanding tasks. Its balance between feasibility and data richness makes it a fun-
damental tool for understanding cognitive processes in diverse research contexts,
including the analysis of cognitive load in the context of this research.
2.5.2 Electrocardiography
The first human ECG recording was published in 1887 by British physiologist Augus-
tus Waller [87]. Subsequent refinements, particularly by Dutch physiologist Willem
Einthoven, who introduced the string galvanometer [88], established ECG as a cru-
cial diagnostic tool in cardiology. Beyond clinical applications, ECG has gained
significant attention in biomedical research, particularly in fields such as emotion
recognition, stress assessment, and cognitive load evaluation.
ECG records the heart’s electrical activity by detecting the voltage changes that
occur during each cardiac cycle. These changes are generated by the depolarization
and repolarization of cardiac muscle cells. Standard ECG systems vary in complex-
ity, ranging from single-lead configurations to the commonly used 12-lead system,
which involves 10 electrodes placed on the limbs and chest [89]. In cognitive load
research, simplified ECG configurations are often preferred for their ease of deploy-
ment and reduced intrusiveness.
The cardiac cycle, recorded as a repeating PQRST waveform, provides detailed
information about heart function (Fig. 2.4). The P wave represents atrial depo-
larization, the QRS complex corresponds to ventricular depolarization, and the T
wave reflects ventricular repolarization [90,91]. Analyzing the properties of these
waveforms, including their amplitude, duration, and variance, can reveal valuable
insights into autonomic nervous system (ANS) activity.
One of the most significant measures derived from ECG is Heart Rate Vari-
ability (HRV), defined as the variance in time between consecutive R peaks (R-
R intervals) [92]. HRV analysis is a powerful tool for evaluating the balance be-
tween the sympathetic and parasympathetic branches of the ANS. Features ex-
tracted from HRV include time-domain metrics, such as the standard deviation of
R-R intervals (SDNN), and frequency-domain metrics, such as Very Low Frequency
(VLF: 0.0033–0.04 Hz), Low Frequency (LF: 0.04–0.15 Hz), and High Frequency
(HF: 0.15–0.4 Hz) bands. The LF/HF ratio, in particular, is a widely used metric
for assessing ANS dominance, providing valuable insights into stress and cognitive
load [93,94].
The use of ECG to derive HRV features is widely recognized for its ability to
provide insights into the balance between the sympathetic and parasympathetic
branches of the ANS. While techniques like EEG and fMRI target brain monitoring,

2.5. NEUROPHYSIOLOGICAL MONITORING TECHNIQUES 23
Figure 2.4: Illustration of the cardiac cycle and respective action potential depolarization (green)
and repolarization (red) of the heart cavities. Adapted from [91].
ECG offers an indirect yet valuable perspective on cognitive load by monitoring
the body’s autonomic responses to cognitively demanding tasks. Compared to eye-
tracking or EEG, ECG setups can be simpler and more robust to external artifacts,
making them easier to integrate into studies where developers may move or shift
during tasks.
However, inferring programmers’ cognitive load through peripheral physiological
signals like HRV introduces certain challenges. ANS-driven responses are influenced
byvariousexternalstimuliandinternalfactors, whichcanintroducenoiseorspurious
reactions unrelated to the software development task at hand. Additionally, the
response time of HRV and other ANS signals is often slower than the brain activity
captured by EEG, which can be a challenge for applications that require precise
real-time temporal resolution. Despite these limitations, HRV remains a practical
measure that offers a broader view of cognitive load. Moreover, HRV data can be
obtained using non-intrusive, cost-effective sensors, making it a valuable tool for
assessing cognitive load in software engineering tasks.
2.5.3 Eye-tracking and Pupillography
In the late 19th century, eye movement data was obtained through introspection or
simple observation of the subject’s eyes by a physician or researcher [95]. The first
precise and non-invasive eye-tracking device was developed in 1901 by Dodge and
Cline, capable of recording horizontal eye movements during specific tasks [96]. Over
time, advancesintechnologyhavesignificantlyimprovedthetemporalresolutionand

24 CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND
spatial scanning capabilities of eye-tracking devices [97].
Modern eye-tracking systems typically use near-infrared light directed at the
pupil, with an infrared camera capturing reflections from the pupil and cornea.
These reflections allow for precise tracking of eye movements and positions [98,99].
Recorded eye movements are commonly categorized into three types: (i) Fixations,
where the eye stabilizes on a specific area for a short period (e.g., 200–300 ms);
(ii) Saccades, which are rapid movements between fixations; and (iii) Scan-paths,
representing the sequence of fixations and saccades over time [98].
Additionally, many modern eye-tracking devices can record pupil size and reac-
tivity, enabling pupillography analysis [100]. The pupil’s size is controlled by the
autonomic nervous system (ANS), with both the sympathetic and parasympathetic
divisions playing a role in pupil dilation and constriction [101]. As a result, pupil-
related metrics, such as pupil diameter, pupil-size variability (PSV), and blink rate,
are increasingly used as indicators of mental workload and cognitive states [102–104].
In recent years, eye-tracking and pupillography have gained significant interest
due to their ease of use, non-intrusive nature, and versatility across various envi-
ronments and tasks. These tools are particularly valuable in software engineering
research, where features like fixation duration, blink rate, and pupil dilation provide
insights into developers’ cognitive load during complex tasks [105,106].
From a practical perspective, eye-tracking stands out among neurophysiological
techniques due to its seamless integration into typical software development envi-
ronments. Unlike other sensors that need to be worn, which may affect comfort or
lead to distractions, eye-tracking can be mounted on monitors or used as lightweight
glasses, enablingcontinuous, real-timemonitoringwithoutdisruptingthedeveloper’s
workflow [107]. This compatibility, along with its ability to capture both gaze loca-
tion and physiological data such as pupil size, makes eye-tracking a cost-effective and
practical solution for assessing cognitive load in software development contexts [108].
Furthermore, eye-tracking offers a unique advantage over other techniques, such
as wearable devices like smartwatches, which typically rely on Photoplethysmog-
raphy (PPG) to measure Pulse Rate Variability (PRV). PPG-based methods may
compromise the High-Frequency (HF) component which can be relevant for monitor-
ing cognitive load via the autonomic nervous system [74]. Another important aspect
is the fact that Eye-tracking provides an additional layer of analysis. It not only
captures physiological indicators, such for example gaze behaviour features or pupil
size features, but also spatial metrics such as gaze location, enabling a fine-grained
analysis of where developers focus their attention during tasks which is a must for
spotting specific regions of code or broader areas that require revision or other sug-
gested actions. This dual capability of assessing both physiological and spatial data
allows for a comprehensive understanding of cognitive load, making eye-tracking an
indispensable tool for software engineering research [108].
However, eye-tracking does come with certain limitations. Developers may move,

2.6. CONCLUDING REMARKS 25
takebreaks, orshiftpositionsduringtasks, whichcandisruptcontinuousmonitoring.
Complementing eye-tracking with other methods can be useful to provide additional
validation and ensure continuity of monitoring when eye-tracking data is incomplete
or disrupted.
2.6 Concluding Remarks
This chapter has provided the basic concepts for understanding the connection be-
tween software engineering and neurophysiology, which is the core of the research
of this thesis. The discussion of the human nervous system and biosignals also es-
tablishes the physiological basis for assessing cognitive load, the key focus of this
research. Finally, the chapter introduces the neurophysiological techniques used to
monitor and assess cognitive load, providing the basic concepts to understand the
research work detailed in the following chapters.

Chapter 3
Literature Review
T
his chapter provides a literature review of existing research on the neu-
ral mechanisms underlying software development tasks and the cognitive
load in software engineering contexts. Specifically, it reviews the stud-
ies exploring functional imaging, brain bioelectrical activity, and ANS bioelectrical
activity for understanding or evaluating cognitive processes during software-related
activities. For a detailed summary of the studies covered in this chapter, refer to
Appendix A, Table A.1.
3.1 Neurophysiological Studies in Software Engineering
Understanding variations in cognitive load, particularly during code comprehension,
is a key aspect for improving software engineering practices. Programmers spend up
to 70% of their time comprehending code during development activities [109]. This
has motivated a growing interest in understanding the cognitive and neural patterns
underlying programming tasks. Research in this domain has been exploring tech-
niques and sensors, such for example, from functional magnetic resonance imaging
(fMRI), functional near-infrared spectroscopy (fNIRS) and electroencephalography
(EEG) to autonomic nervous system (ANS)-based sensors such as electrocardiogra-
phy (ECG) for heart rate variability (HRV), Electrodermal Activity (EDA), Eye-
trackingwithPupillography. Thissectionprovidesasystematicreviewofthecurrent
literature on this domain, organized into subsections on functional imaging studies,
EEG-based studies, and ANS-related biosignal research.
3.1.1 Functional Imaging-Based Studies
Functional imaging tools, such as fMRI and fNIRS, have been pivotal in the under-
standing of the neural mechanisms underlying programming tasks. Studies in this
domain have focused on different programming tasks, including code comprehension,
code inspection and bug detection, and code programming.
27

28 CHAPTER 3. LITERATURE REVIEW
In 2014, Siegmund et al. [110] conducted one of the first fMRI studies identifying
specific brain regions associated with code comprehension, particularly areas related
to language processing, working memory, and attention. In a subsequent replication
study with a larger sample size, Peitek et al. [111] confirmed these findings and re-
inforced the role of these cognitive regions in programming tasks. Building on this
foundational work, other studies from Siegmund et al.’s research group investigated
code comprehension from complementary angles. For example, Siegmund et al. [59]
explored how semantic chunking (organizing code into meaningful groups) and the
use of beacons (recognizable elements or patterns in the code that act as helpful
hints for navigating or understanding it) influenced comprehension efficiency. The
results highlighted how semantic cues reduced neural activation, thereby easing com-
prehension. Later, to enhance the granularity of fMRI data, Peitek et al. [112,113]
integrated eye-tracking into their fMRI experiments. These studies demonstrated
how specific gaze patterns mapped to distinct phases of code comprehension, pro-
viding more detailed insights into programmers’ cognitive processes. The addition of
eye-tracking enabled a finer-grained analysis of brain activity, helping to understand
better how programmers navigate and comprehend code.
Floyd et al. [114] expanded this scope by comparing the neural activity during
code comprehension, code review, and natural language processing. Their study
achieved 79% accuracy in distinguishing the tasks based on brain activity and also
revealed that the higher the expertise and skill of the software programmers, the
smaller the difference in neural activation level between the tasks.
Peitek et al. [115] further investigated program comprehension but focusing on
the code complexity metrics. Their findings linked higher complexity, particularly
in size and vocabulary-based metrics, with increased activation in brain regions
associated with language processing and working memory. A follow-up replication
study by the same authors [116] reinforced these results, highlighting the limitations
of traditional complexity metrics in fully capturing the cognitive demands of code
comprehension.
Additional to code comprehension, parallel research has focused on bug detec-
tion and its associated neural mechanisms using fMRI. Early studies in this context,
by Durães et al. [117] and Castelhano et al. [118], highlighted the insula’s role in
bug detection, with its activation levels correlating with the accuracy of bug identi-
fication. Expanding on this, more recently, Castelhano et al. [119] investigated the
neural mechanisms underlying bug detection during code inspection. Using fMRI,
they identified the insula as a pivotal role within the error-monitoring network,
exhibiting increased activation during bug detection tasks. Their findings further
revealed a shift in effective connectivity, transitioning from bottom-up influences in
simpler tasks to top-down regulation by frontal regions, particularly the anterior
cingulate cortex, during bug detection. This study further highlights the insula’s
role in error-monitoring processes and decision-making, particularly during complex

3.1. NEUROPHYSIOLOGICAL STUDIES IN SOFTW ARE ENGINEERING 29
code inspections tasks.
For programming tasks, despite the inherent complexity of performing these type
of studies inside a fMRI, Krueger et al. [120] carried a study using fMRI to ana-
lyze neural mechanisms during code programming. The authors found increased
activation in the brain’s right hemisphere, particularly in regions linked to working
memory, planning, and spatial cognition, underscoring the distinct cognitive de-
mands of code programming compared to comprehension. Later, in the same line,
Hishikawa et al. [121] used also fMRI to study how the brain changes during pro-
gramming skill learning in beginners. In this study, over a 5-month training period,
participants showed improved accuracy, which was linked to increased activity in
the right inferior frontal gyrus, a region associated with learning and adaptation.
The findings highlighted how the brain adapts and plays a key role in programming
skill development.
While fMRI has provided valuable insights into the neural mechanisms underly-
ing software tasks, its high cost, immobility, and complexity limit broader adoption
for larger studies, particularly in dynamic programming activities like code writing
and debugging, where staying still for long durations inside a scanner is impractical.
In parallel to the previous works, some researchers have also explored fNIRS as a
less restrictive alternative to explore the neural mechanisms and cognitive processes
of programmers associated with software development activities.
ThefirststudyusingfNIRSinthis context, in2014, wascarriedout byNakagawa
et al. [122], where the authors demonstrated that fNIRS could capture increases in
cerebral blood flow when programmers faced complex code comprehension tasks, as-
sociating these changes with increased cognitive load. In the same year, Ikutani et
al. [123] published a study using fNIRS to investigate the effects of variable memo-
rization on mental workload. Their study found significant brain activity differences
between tasks requiring variable memorization and simpler code comprehension, fur-
ther supporting fNIRS as a reliable modality for capturing cognitive load variations
in programming contexts.
In the work from Fakhoury et al. [124], the authors have combined fNIRS along-
sideeye-trackingtoinvestigatetheimpactofpoorsourcelexiconandcodereadability
on cognitive load. Their findings revealed that linguistic antipatterns significantly
increased programmers’ mental effort. In a follow-up study with more participants,
Fakhoury et al. [125] confirmed these results, emphasizing the importance of source
code readability in reducing cognitive load.
Together, these functional imaging studies have provided valuable insights into
the neural mechanisms underlying software development tasks, these techniques face
challenges related to high costs, experimental complexity, participant discomfort, or
difficulties in recruiting volunteers. Despite revealing significant findings about brain
regions activations and connectivity patterns involved in code comprehension, code
inspection and bug detection, and code programming, further research is needed to

30 CHAPTER 3. LITERATURE REVIEW
translatetheseinsightsintopracticalapplicationsclosertorealsoftwaredevelopment
environments.
Motivated by those insights, this thesis shifts the focus toward a less intrusive
technique, such as EEG, and investigates the use of EEG as a neuroscience reference
to bridge the gap between high-precision imaging methods, with their detailed spa-
tial resolution for understanding neural mechanisms, and more practical approaches
(HRV, Eye-tracking with Pupillography) suited for integration into software devel-
opment tools. By exploring potential EEG biomarkers associated with software
development tasks and validating them against insights from fMRI, this work in-
vestigates the use of EEG as a ground truth for assessing cognitive load in this
context. Therefore, it can then be used to support the evaluation and comparison
of other less intrusive and scalable sensors, facilitating future research studies and
larger data collections in software engineering contexts that better reflect real-world
software development environments.
3.1.2 Brain Bioelectrical Activity-Based Studies
The EEG has been widely employed to study cognitive load, attention, and task en-
gagement across various domains, including psychology, neuroscience, and human-
computer interaction [19,126–130]. Numerous studies have established strong corre-
lations between EEG frequency bands and cognitive load dynamics, highlighting its
utilityinmeasuringmentaleffortwithhightemporalresolution[65,127,128,131,132].
In the context of software development, EEG provides a less intrusive and cost-
effective alternative to imaging techniques like fMRI and fNIRS, enabling researchers
to explore the neural patterns and programmer’s cognitive load during tasks such
as code comprehension, code inspection and debugging, and code programming ac-
tivities. Its ability to capture rapid neural responses also makes EEG a particularly
effective tool for exploring the dynamics of cognitive load in software engineering
contexts.
Initial studies focused on code comprehension tasks. Fritz et al. [23] pioneered
the use of EEG combined with eye-tracking and EDA to classify code difficulty,
achieving up to 67.71% accuracy. This work highlighted the potential of multi-
modal approaches for cognitive load assessment but emphasized the need for larger
datasets and improved feature engineering. Later, Müller and Fritz [133] also ex-
plored biosignals such as EEG, EDA, and eye-tracking to classify developers’ emo-
tional valence (positive vs. negative) and perceived task progress (high vs. low)
during code comprehension tasks. The positive emotions reflected states like sat-
isfaction and flow, while negative emotions corresponded to frustration and stress.
Similarly, high progress indicated productive advancement, whereas low progress re-
flected obstacles or lack of advancement. Their results showed 71.36% accuracy for
emotions (positive vs. negative) and 67.70% for progress (high vs. low), highlighting

3.1. NEUROPHYSIOLOGICAL STUDIES IN SOFTW ARE ENGINEERING 31
the potential of multimodal biosignals to assess affective and performance states in
software development.
Other studies shifted focus toward analyzing programmer expertise and cognitive
patterns during code comprehension tasks. From a different perspective, Crk et
al. [134,135] explored EEG features to distinguish novice and expert programmers,
identifying Alpha and Theta bands as key indicators of expertise. Later, Crk et al.
[136] refined these findings using individual alpha frequency (IAF), offering further
insights into expertise-related neural patterns.
Similarly, in the context of task difficulty and programmer expertise, Lee et
al. [137] found that Beta and Gamma bands correlated with task difficulty and
expertise, particularly in frontal and parietal regions. Expanding on this, Lee et
al. [138] extended this line of research by integrating EEG with eye-tracking data
to predict both task difficulty and programmer expertise. Their approach achieved
F-measures of 66.6% for task difficulty and 97% for expertise, offering insights about
the benefits of multimodal strategies for improving classification accuracy and cap-
turing cognitive patterns in software tasks.
Building on the focus of identifying cognitive load variations in code comprehen-
sion tasks, Yeh et al. [139] explored EEG signals to compare cognitive load between
confusing and non-confusing code snippets, finding significant differences in frontal
Theta and Alpha activity across the two conditions.
On the same topic of code comprehension tasks, Gonçales et al. [140] explored
different machine learning models based on EEG features to classify the program-
mer’s difficulty while performing code comprehension tasks. Among the different
models trained, k-Nearest Neighbor (kNN) achieved the highest F-measure of 86%,
reinforcing the potential of EEG as a biomarker of software programmers’ cognitive
load for distinguishing task difficulty levels in software contexts.
Expanding beyond studies on standard code comprehension tasks, Kosti et al.
[58] analyzed EEG activity during code comprehension and syntax error detection
tasks. The authors found stronger Theta, Beta, and Gamma activation during code
comprehension tasks, correlating with increased cognitive effort. Despite promising
results, the authors also emphasized the need for larger sample sizes to improve gen-
eralizability. Similarly, Fucci et al. [141] replicated the fMRI-based study by Floyd et
al. [114], comparing code comprehension and natural language processing, using less
intrusive sensors, including EEG, EDA, and HRV. Their machine-learning models
achieved a balanced accuracy of 87%, surpassing the 79.1% reported in the original
study. This study highlights the feasibility of less intrusive sensors for distinguishing
code comprehension from prose comprehension tasks. However, unlike the original
study, this work found no significant correlation between expertise and classifier
performance, suggesting limitations in capturing expertise-related differences with
simpler physiological signals.
Exploring other software development activities than code comprehension tasks,

32 CHAPTER 3. LITERATURE REVIEW
Calcagno et al. [142] investigated EEG patterns during code programming tasks.
The authors identified increased Delta, Theta, and Beta activity in frontal and
parieto-occipital regions, reflecting mental effort and attention, when compared to
control conditions. Extending this work, Calcagno et al. [143] explored Theta band
connectivity, revealing dynamic fronto-parietal networks supporting working mem-
ory during code programming, code reading, and language tasks. Together, these
studies reinforced the idea of EEG as a potential tool for analyzing neural pat-
terns and cognitive processes involved in software development tasks, extending its
application beyond code comprehension.
In the domain of ocular behavioral patterns, Peitek et al. [144] combined EEG
with eye-tracking to assess programmer efficacy, showing that higher-performing
programmers had targeted reading behaviors (shorter fixations and fewer revisits)
and lower cognitive load. Interestingly, traditional programming experience metrics
showed weak correlations with performance, whereas self-evaluation skill levels and
eagerness to learn presented stronger correlations. These findings emphasize the
role of behavioral and neural patterns in distinguishing programming performance,
offering insights beyond conventional assessments.
Following the work developed in this thesis, which will be detailed in the next
chapters, recent efforts have focused on the use of EEG to enhance code complexity
metrics. Hao et al. [34] explored the use of proposed EEG biomarkers of cogni-
tive load, combined with behavioral features from eye-tracking, to investigate their
potential as benchmarks for assessing programmers’ cognitive load during code com-
prehension tasks. Specifically, the authors proposed a set of guidelines to enhance
existing code complexity metrics, particularly state-of-the-art metrics such as cog-
nitive complexity from SonarSource tools, highlighting EEG’s potential to refine
complexity evaluation methods in software engineering.
Although EEG has demonstrated its potential to capture neural activity and
assess cognitive load with high temporal precision, its application often depends on
controlled environments and still requires moderately complex equipment compared
to less intrusive sensors and wearable devices. Building on these findings, early
and parallel research has explored less intrusive approaches, particularly ANS-based
signals such as HRV, EDA, respiration, and eye-tracking with pupillography. How-
ever, the precision and accuracy of these ANS signals remain a concern, as they are
susceptible to other physiological variations, external influences, and noise. This
highlights the role of EEG as a key intermediary step, bridging high-intrusiveness
techniques, such as fMRI, and more practical, less intrusive approaches. In this role,
EEG findings can validate or benchmark the performance of ANS-based indicators
of cognitive load variations, ensuring their reliability. The next section reviews stud-
ies that explored ANS signals for cognitive load monitoring in software engineering
tasks.

3.1. NEUROPHYSIOLOGICAL STUDIES IN SOFTW ARE ENGINEERING 33
3.1.3 ANS Bioelectrical Activity-Based Studies
In parallel, other researchers have explored ANS-related signals as less intrusive al-
ternatives for monitoring cognitive load in software engineering tasks. This subsec-
tion reviews studies that evaluated ANS signals in tasks such as code comprehension,
code programming, and code reviews, focusing on their feasibility and limitations
for cognitive load monitoring.
Muller et al. [24] conducted the first study linking HRV features with task diffi-
culty and code quality, achieving accuracies between 57.4% and 72.8%. The study
also showed that the biosignal data results were higher than the software metrics re-
sults, but combining both information led to improved performance. However, the
authors also highlighted challenges such as noisy data and spurious physiological
reactions unrelated to the software tasks.
Expanding this line of research, Couceiro et al. [25] assessed HRV features for
cognitive load classification during code comprehension tasks. Their model achieved
high sensitivity (96.00%) and specificity (81.00%) for distinguishing easy versus com-
plex code but struggled to differentiate between more complex levels, suggesting
alignment with subjective evaluations rather than traditional software metrics. In
parallel, Couceiroetal.[145]analyzedpupillographyfeatures, findinghighervariance
in low-frequency bands during more complex tasks, indicating increased cognitive
load. Subsequent studies explored the integration of these signals with eye-tracking
to identify critical regions in the code that demanded higher cognitive load [27].
Inadifferentperspective, Hijazietal.[146]proposedapproachesleveragingANS-
based biosignals, such as HRV and eye-tracking, to monitor cognitive load during
code review tasks. Their first study introduced the iReview tool, which combined
physiological data with behavioral metrics like the number of revisits, achieving
prediction accuracies between 75% and 87% using machine learning classifiers, pro-
viding valuable insights into biofeedback applications for code review evaluation.
Expanding on this, a later study by Hijazi et al. [108] adopted a similar approach,
but now using HRV, pupillography, and eye-tracking data to train machine learning
models for predicting code review quality, achieving 87.77% accuracy. Both these
works presented valuable insights regarding the potential of biofeedback applications
for code review evaluation.
Steyde et al. [147] explored HRV and respiration signals of software programmers
while performing code comprehension and code programming tasks. Their findings
revealed significant increases in heart rate and respiratory rate, and reductions in
HRV total power, particularly during programming tasks, suggesting higher cogni-
tive effort and sympathetic activation during such tasks. The study also highlights
the potential of these signals for real-time cognitive load monitoring and emphasizes
the need for adaptive models to handle physiological data variability.
Laudato et al. [148] investigated the use of HRV and EDA signals, along with

34 CHAPTER 3. LITERATURE REVIEW
behavioral data from mouse and keyboard interactions, to predict bug introduction
during programming tasks. Their approach achieved 76% accuracy using physiolog-
ical and behavioral features, comparable to traditional code-based metrics (79%).
Furthermore, combining both approaches improved performance to 84% accuracy,
highlighting the potential of integrating human-centric physiological and behavioral
data with code metrics to enhance bug prediction capabilities.
While studies using ANS-related signals, such as HRV, EDA, Respiration, and
Eye-tracking with pupillography, have shown promise for cognitive load monitor-
ing and task evaluation in software development, challenges persist regarding their
precision and accuracy. Additionally, concerns emerge about the reliability of ultra-
short-term features in HRV analysis and the absence of well-defined frequency limits
in the spectral analysis of pupillography to be used in software contexts. These gaps
are further addressed and explored in the following chapters, including also research
on the performance of different biosignals of different levels of intrusiveness in code
quality review assessment, to identify a potential solution as a viable setup for prac-
tical applications in software development environments.
3.2 Concluding Remarks
Thischapterreviewedexistingresearchonfunctionalimaging, EEG-basedapproaches,
and ANS biosignals for assessing cognitive load and understanding neural mecha-
nisms during software engineering tasks. Although current research studies have
demonstrated the potential of these techniques, there are still limitations regard-
ing their intrusiveness, practicability, precision, and accuracy, motivating further
research. The following chapters address these gaps by evaluating EEG as a neuro-
science reference and exploring ANS-based signals to investigate optimal parameters
and practical solutions for real-world software development environments. For a de-
tailed summary of the studies covered in this chapter, refer to Appendix A, Table
A.1.

Chapter 4
Datasets
T
his chapter provides an overview of the experimental studies explored, or
conducted as part of this thesis, and underBiofeedback Augmented Soft-
ware Engineering (BASE)project. These studies were designed to inves-
tigate the neural mechanisms and physiological responses of software programmers
performing different software tasks, including code comprehension, code inspection
and bug detection, and code programming. The collected datasets incorporated a
rich and wide range of modalities, including Electroencephalography (EEG), Func-
tional Magnetic Resonance Imaging (fMRI), Functional Near-Infrared Spectroscopy
(fNIRS), Electrocardiography (ECG), Electrodermal Activity (EDA), Photoplethys-
mography (PPG), Blood Pressure (BP), Eye-Tracking with Pupillography, and oth-
ers, depending on the experimental study.
The results and contributions of this thesis primarily focused on the data col-
lected from two main experimental studies. The first experimental study, referred
to as Study 0, focused on code comprehension tasks, while the second experimental
study, referred to as Study 1, was on code inspection tasks. In addition to these ex-
periments, further data collection efforts were conducted, including Study 2, which
focused on code programming tasks, and more recently an extended version of Study
0 (Study 0 Extension) with additional code comprehension data.
The following sections summarize these studies. Additional detailed descrip-
tions of the experimental protocols and data collection setups are presented in the
appendices B, C, D and E.
4.1 Overview of Experimental Studies
4.1.1 Study 0: Mental Effort Monitoring During Code Compre-
hension Tasks
This study aimed to assess developers’ mental effort during code comprehension
tasks involving Java code snippets of varying complexity. Data were collected from
35

36 CHAPTER 4. DATASETS
30 participants, including students, university professors, and professional software
developers with experience in Java programming. The group consisted of 24 males
and 6 females, aged 19 to 42 years (average: 24.00± 6.18 years). Participants were
classified into three proficiency levels - 13 intermediate, 12 advanced, and 5 expert
- based on years of practice and the volume of Java code written.
Participants completed three runs, each corresponding to one of three code com-
prehension trials using Java snippets of increasing complexity, as determined by
software complexity metrics (Code 1: simplest, Code 3: most complex). Each trial
included a control task (natural language text reading, 60 seconds max) and a code
comprehension task (10 minutes max). Between tasks, a 30-second baseline interval
was introduced with a cross displayed on the screen to establish resting-state cog-
nitive activity (see Figure 4.1). Trials followed a fixed sequence (Code 1, Code 2,
Code 3) to ensure consistency, with participants unaware of the complexity levels to
prevent bias.
After each trial, participants completed two questionnaires. The first assessed
their understanding of the code, incentivizing engagement. The second, based on
NASA-TLX [60], evaluated subjective mental effort, task fulfillment, time pressure,
and frustration on a six-point scale.
The experiment captured fully synchronized data, including EEG, ECG, EDA,
PPG,andEye-trackingwithpupillographysignals, forcomprehensiveanalysis. Each
session lasted approximately 1.5 hours per participant, comprising 45 minutes for
setup and 45 minutes for the trials (see Figure 4.1). For more detailed information
about the protocol, see Appendix B.

4.1.1.1 Study 0 Extension: Validation Study
This study replicated the original Study 0 with a new group of 35 participants
and a fresh set of Java code snippets of varying complexity, designed to validate

4.1. OVER VIEW OF EXPERIMENTAL STUDIES 37
the initial findings. Participants alternated between tasks of natural language text
reading and Java code comprehension under the same experimental conditions as
the original study.
The participant group consisted of 19 MSc students, 6 PhD students, 5 re-
searchers, and 5 software professionals, all with significant programming experi-
ence, particularly in Java. Eligibility was determined through interviews assessing
years of experience and the amount of code written. These interviews ensured that
participants met the minimum requirements for inclusion in the study. Of the 35
participants, 28 were male and 7 female, with ages ranging from 20 to 48 years
(average: 26.50 ± 6.88 years).
The multimodal biosensor setup used in the original study was employed here
as well, capturing synchronized data from EEG, ECG, PPG, and eye-tracking with
pupillography signals.
For more detailed information about the protocol, refer to Appendix C.
4.1.2 Study 1: Brain Activity and Physiological Responses During
Software Inspection
This study aimed to assess developers’ mental effort and performance during code
inspection and bug inspection tasks using C code snippets. Data were collected
from 21 participants with C programming experience, all male, aged 19 to 40 years
(average: 25.56 ± 6.85 years). Participants were selected through a screening pro-
cess involving two questionnaires: one evaluating programming experience based on
recent coding activity and another assessing technical skills through a 10-question
test. Of 49 candidates, only those scoring 3 or higher were eligible, resulting in 16
intermediate (scores 4–7) and 5 expert participants (scores 8–10).
Each participant completed four runs, each comprising three randomized condi-
tions: a control task of natural language text reading (60 seconds max), a simple
code comprehension task (5 minutes max), and a code inspection and bug detec-
tion task (10 minutes max). Baseline intervals of 30 seconds, with a fixation cross
displayed, were introduced before and after each condition to capture resting-state
activity (see Fig. 4.2).
After each run, participants answered two questionnaires. The first question-
naire aimed to encourage participants to stay engaged and focused on the task by
informing them beforehand that they would need to recall and describe the bugs in
the code snippets at the end of each run. The second, an adapted NASA-TLX [60]
survey, collected subjective ratings on mental effort, task fulfillment, time pressure,
and frustration on a six-point scale.
Fully synchronized data, including EEG, ECG, EDA, PPG, BP, Eye-tracking
with Pupillography, and fMRI signals, were collected throughout the experiment.
The total duration for each participant was under two hours, comprising 30–45

38 CHAPTER 4. DATASETS
minutes for setup and a maximum of 74 minutes for task execution (see Figure 4.2).
For a detailed description of the protocol, refer to Appendix D.

4.1.3 Study 2: Brain Activity and Physiological Responses During
Software Programming
Thisstudyaimedtoevaluatedevelopers’mentaleffortandcognitiveprocessesduring
tasks involving natural text reading, code comprehension, and programming activi-
ties. Data were collected from 11 participants with programming experience, includ-
ing researchers and students from Politecnico di Milano and Università Statale di
Milano. Participants were selected based on a screening questionnaire that assessed
their understanding of C code snippets. The questionnaire featured multiple-choice
questions about the functionality of 10 code snippets, with participants required to
answer at least 4 questions correctly to qualify. The final group included 8 males
and 3 females, with a mean age of 27.72± 6.06 years.
Participants completed two runs, each featuring three randomized tasks-natural
text reading (60 seconds max), code comprehension (5 minutes max), and program-
ming (minimum 5 minutes, max 20 minutes). Baseline intervals of 30 seconds,
displaying a fixation cross, were added before and after each task to capture resting-
state activity (see Fig. 4.3).
In the programming task, participants implemented a simple algorithm based on
given instructions. After each run, they rated their experiences using a questionnaire
assessing mental effort, task fulfillment, time pressure, and discomfort.
During the experiment, EEG, fNIRS, ECG, EDA, Eye-tracking with pupillogra-
phy, and Respiratory signals were recorded in a fully synchronized manner. Addi-
tionally, keyboard and mouse activity, as well as facial video recordings, were cap-
tured to provide comprehensive behavioral data for analysis. The total experiment

lasted approximately 1.5 hours, including setup and tasks. Figure 4.3 illustrates the
experimental protocol. For more detailed information, refer to Appendix E.

4.2 Other Contributions
The datasets collected and analyzed during this research are also intended to support
further research works by making them fully available to the scientific community.
A manuscript is currently being prepared that provides a detailed overview of the
experimental setups, protocols, data collected, methodologies, and validation pro-
cesses used in all these studies. The goal is to make these datasets available as useful
resources for future research in software engineering and cognitive load monitoring.

Chapter 5
Central Nervous System and Software
Engineering
T
his chapter presents two research studies that collectively explore the use
of EEG as a tool for assessing cognitive load during software development
tasks. Each study is introduced with its context and key findings, followed
by the full text of the respective papers.
The first study,Can EEG Be Adopted as a Neuroscience Reference for Assessing
Software Programmers’ Cognitive Load?, investigates whether EEG can effectively
identify programmers’ cognitive load during code comprehension. By analyzing data
from 26 programmers, the study reports that EEG features, particularly those re-
lated to Theta, Alpha, and Beta brain waves, are powerful discriminants of cognitive
load. This finding suggests that EEG, when combined with eye-tracking, can pin-
point specific code lines that cause higher cognitive demand, providing insights to
improve software quality.
Medeiros, J., Couceiro, R., Duarte, G., Durães, J., Castelhano, J., Duarte, C.,
Castelo-Branco, M., Madeira, H., de Carvalho, P. & Teixeira, C. (2021). Can
EEG be adopted as a neuroscience reference for assessing software programmers’
cognitive load?. Sensors, 21(7), 2338.Impact Factor: 4.43 (2021); Q1.
Building on this foundation, the second study from this thesis,EEG as a Po-
tential Ground Truth for the Assessment of Cognitive State in Software Develop-
ment Activities: A Multimodal Imaging Study, explores the correlation between EEG
biomarkers and fMRI data. Through a controlled experiment with 14 programmers,
the work identifies robust EEG features that correlate with brain activity in regions
such as the Insula, which is associated with software debugging tasks. The reported
findings support the use of EEG as a ground truth for assessing cognitive states,
41

serving as a useful source for evaluating non-intrusive, wearable tools that could
monitor programmers’ cognitive states in real-time.

Abstract: An emergent research area in software engineering and software reliability is the use of
wearable biosensors to monitor the cognitive state of software developers during software devel-
opment tasks. The goal is to gather physiologic manifestations that can be linked to error-prone
scenarios related to programmers’ cognitive states. In this paper we investigate whether electroen-
cephalography (EEG) can be applied to accurately identify programmers’ cognitive load associated
with the comprehension of code with different complexity levels. Therefore, a controlled experiment
involving 26 programmers was carried. We found that features related to Theta, Alpha, and Beta
brain waves have the highest discriminative power, allowing the identiﬁcation of code lines and
demanding higher mental effort. The EEG results reveal evidence of mental effort saturation as code
complexity increases. Conversely, the classic software complexity metrics do not accurately represent
the mental effort involved in code comprehension. Finally, EEG is proposed as a reference, in par-
ticular, the combination of EEG with eye tracking information allows for an accurate identiﬁcation
of code lines that correspond to peaks of cognitive load, providing a reference to help in the future
evaluation of the space and time accuracy of programmers’ cognitive state monitored using wearable
devices compatible with software development activities.
Keywords: software engineering; bio-signal processing; electroencephalogram; biofeedback; human error
1. Introduction
Software defects (i.e., bugs) represent the most enduring problem of software quality.
In spite of decades of research and advances in software engineering and software reliability,
the number of defects per 1000 lines of delivered code (KLoC) remains astonishingly high.
Steve McConnell’s seminal book [1] points to an industry average of about 15 defects per
KLoCs, which is a very large standard deviation. Even when software is developed using
highly mature processes, the deployed code still has a relatively high defect density, from
1 to 5 bugs per KLoC [ 2–5]. More recent ﬁeld data from several projects show defects
densities from 1 to 6 bugs per KLoC [6], suggesting that in real projects, often characterized
by millions of lines of code, the defect density remains quite signiﬁcant and represents
a huge cost for the software industry and society in general. Subsequently, reports on
the ﬁnance impact of software defects and software failures show dramatic ﬁgures. The
global cost of debugging software defects was estimated as $312 billion in 2013 [7], and the
Sensors 2021, 21, 2338. https://doi.org/10.3390/s21072338 https://www.mdpi.com/journal/sensors

Sensors 2021, 21, 2338 2 of 26
cost of software failures for the worldwide economy reached $1.1 trillion in 2016 [8] while
“software failures affected 3.6 billion people in 2017, causing US$1.7 trillion in ﬁnancial
losses” [9].
Known causes of human error from a software developer perspective such as fatigue,
cognitive overload, attention slips, and stress have not been much addressed in software
engineering research. All these cognitive states (high mental effort, stress level, attention
shifts, cognitive overload, mental fatigue) have been associated with error-prone scenarios,
as established by human error models [10] and their adaptation to software development
tasks [11,12], but so far none of the available software development methodologies uses
any form of direct information from the cognitive state of the software developer.
The idea of using biometric measures such as heart rate variability (HRV), respiratory
responses, pupillary response (pupillometry), electrodermal activity (EDA), or electromyo-
gram (EMG) to gather information on the cognitive load while carrying out speciﬁc tasks
(and inherently infer the difﬁculty associated with such tasks) is not new [13–16]. Further-
more, speciﬁc biometric measures such as EDA have also been proposed to discriminate
stress from cognitive load [17,18]. However, only in recent years has the use of biometric
measures been proposed for the context of software development. First works have shown
that biometric measures can be linked to task difﬁculty or difﬁculty in comprehending
code snippets [19], and that HRV can be used to predict the quality of the code made by
programmers, which is useful to optimizing software testing [20].
Recent studies proposed a broader idea named Biofeedback Augmented Software
Engineering [21] to integrate biometric measures such as HRV and pupillometry [22] in the
software development process through the use of cognitive state code annotations [23].
The proposal consists of using eye tracking devices, particularly non-intrusive desktop
eye trackers, to provide accurate information on the code lines where the programmer is
looking, and annotate such code lines with the programmer’s cognitive state (expressing
cognitive load, distractions, fatigue, etc.) gathered using HRV and pupillometry [ 23].
Cognitive state code annotations are expected to allow predictions of code quality in order
to provide online warnings to the programmer, or to guide code inspections or the software
testing effort in general. The idea of such predictions is intuitively simple and can be
illustrated by straightforward examples (just three examples among many others):
a. If the annotations associated with a given set of code lines say that the programmer is
under high cognitive overload while producing/reviewing such code lines, then the
chance of having software bugs in such code lines is higher than normal;
b. If the code is complex (measured by classic complexity metrics) and the annotations
show that the programmer is distracted while dealing with such code lines, then the
probability of software bugs in the annotated code lines is higher than normal;
c. If the code is simple and the annotations show that the programmer is under high
mental effort, then the probability of software bugs in the annotated code lines is
higher than normal.
Importantly , these first studies [19–23] have shown that programmers’ cognitive load
during typical code development activities can be assessed using biometric measures ob-
tained through wearable and low intrusive devices compatible with standard software
development environments. Although quite encouraging, all these recent studies [19–23]
have used wearable technology to infer programmer’s cognitive states while carrying out
software development tasks, and all of them share an inherent limitation: The programmers’
cognitive states are inferred indirectly , through peripheral physiological signals driven by
the autonomic nervous system (ANS) (e.g., HRV , pupillometry , EDA, EMG, among others),
which raises the question of the accuracy and precision of the cognitive states assessed in
that way. Furthermore, the response time of the ANS driven signals captured by wearable
technology is another important concern, as the whole idea of cognitive code line annotation
requires a timely response (or at least the delay must be known) to achieve accurate annota-
tions. And, finally , physiological responses driven by the ANS is influenced by many other

Sensors 2021, 21, 2338 3 of 26
stimuli, which means that the signals gathered by the wearable devices will include noise
and spurious physiological reactions not related to the software development activities.
There are other alternative technologies that do not focus on the recordings of ANS-
related biosignals to assess the cognitive states of a subject, such as face analysis or gaze
analysis. However, to the best of our knowledge, most studies exploring face analysis to
assess cognitive states use it alongside other biosignasl [24]. Furthermore, for face analysis,
the recording of video or images of the software programmers, while they are performing
software tasks, can be psychologically intrusive. Regarding gaze analysis, there are a lot
of research on this topic in software engineering [ 25], but most of the works focus on
the afterwards analysis, i.e., based on the ﬁxations, saccades, and scan-paths, needing
some time to obtain the overall analysis of the gaze points. Consequently, it presents a
low response time to gather information about the complexity of the task and inherent
cognitive load. Therefore these type of approach might be useful as an complementary
analysis, but not to validate the accuracy and precision of the cognitive states assessed
based on physiological signals driven by the ANS.
These important limitations of all the existing proposals towards the use of biometric
measures to improve the software quality can only be addressed through the creation of a
ground truth to evaluate the accuracy of wearable devices that rely on physiologic manifes-
tations driven by the ANS. This paper evaluates existing and new electroencephalography
(EEG) biomarkers related to the central nervous system (CNS), sensitive to different levels
of cognitive load in the context of software development.
EEG can be a powerful tool to this end, since it records the electrical activity of the
brain, which is one of the most relevant physiological areas analyzed when assessing
cognitive states [ 26]. From this signal we can explore and extract different and useful
information to infer directly the cognitive states of the subject from the brain activity
recorded, and still offers a less intrusive approach than other imaging techniques such
as functional magnetic resonance imaging (fMRI) and near ﬁeld infrared spectroscopy
(fNIRS). Therefore, with our experiment, we intend to investigate and respond to the
following research goals: (i) The cognitive load measured using EEG is representative of
the subjective perception of code complexity; (ii) the complexity measured using classic
software complexity metrics do not translate the EEG and subjective perception results
from the code snippets; and (iii) EEG biomarkers combined with eye-tracking data can
differentiate code regions that cause a higher cognitive load. Based on those hypotheses,
this paper proposes the use of EEG to create a ground truth to be used as a reference in
the evaluation of the accuracy of wearable devices. Although EEG is turning into a low
cost and portable solution with advances in technology [27,28], it still cannot be used in
normal software development environments, because it is too intrusive (in most practical
realizations, it needs a cap to apply the large number of electrodes to the subject).
In short, the contributions of this paper are the following:
• Proposes the very ﬁrst approach to use EEG as a reference to evaluate and improve
the accuracy of wearable devices that rely on physiologic manifestations driven by
the ANS, as indicators of programmers’ cognitive load. This contribution includes the
online availability of a package including the proposed method, protocols, datasets,
and EEG results;
• Demonstrates the viability of using EEG-derived features for assessing the cognitive
load of programmers in comprehending program snippets of different complexities;
• Shows that cognitive load of programmers in code comprehension is consistent with
the subjective perception of the programmers on the complexity and mental effort
spent on comprehending a given program snippet;
• Conﬁrms that software complexity metrics have clear limitations as indicators of
the code complexity as perceived by (human) programmers, and shows that pro-
grammers’ perceived code complexity saturates very quickly as complexity metrics
increase. This contribution has a relevant impact on existing software development
practices, as it shows that the use of complexity metrics by the software industry (as

Sensors 2021, 21, 2338 4 of 26
a code complexity indicator during development) must be revisited to include both
complexity metrics and programmers’ perceived code complexity measures.
The next section presents additional background and related work, particularly on
cognitive state assessment using EEG. Section 3 describes the controlled experiment, the
data, the acquisition protocol, and also details the methods used for preprocessing EEG
data, feature engineering, and classiﬁcation. Section 4 presents the main results and
respective discussion, and proposes the use of the EEG as a reference to calibrate wearable
devices that collect physiological signals driven by the ANS. Section 5 presents the threats
to the validity of this work and Section 6 concludes the paper.
2. Background and Related Work
The root causes of software defects are buried deep in human error manifestations
and must be addressed in an interdisciplinary perspective involving software engineering,
cognitive psychology, neuroscience, and even biomedical ﬁelds. In fact, this interdisci-
plinary research perspective on software defects has gained ground and is now an emergent
research line. The next subsections brieﬂy overview the key aspects of this interdisciplinary
research effort.
2.1. Software Complexity Metrics and Software Quality
Software complexity metrics are used worldwide in software engineering to guide
software testing coverage, to estimate software defect density, and even to determine
the most adequate component granularity in software architectures. In general, it is
assumed that complexity metrics also portray the inherent complexity of software artefacts,
as perceived by (human) software developers. However, it is known that complexity
metrics deviate considerably from human perceived complexity in code structures such
as recursive or multi-threading programming, as in these cases the complexity is not in
the code structures (usually very compact) but in the recursive and/or parallel nature of
the code. In addition, recent work has exposed the limits of complexity metrics as a tool to
express software complexity in the human/programmers sense [21]. The present paper
reinforces this conclusion using the much more reliable measurement provided by the EEG.
In fact, as shown in Section 4, subjective complexity felt by participants (measured by the
NASA TLX tool [29]) is inline with the cognitive load measured by EEG results. However,
both measurements (i.e., EEG and subjective complexity results) deviate considerably from
software complexity metrics, showing that the use of complexity metrics as predictors of a
programmer/tester’s cognitive effort (and, consequently, predictor of error prone code)
could be misleading.
Metrics can be relative to the code, documentation, or programmer subject [30]. Classic
code-related metrics (i.e., McCabe [31] and Halstead’s [32] metrics) focus mainly on code
and data structures. Well-known examples of code metrics include McCabe Cyclomatic
Complexity (V(G)) [31], average Number of Nested Block Depth, Number of Parameters
and Lines of codes, among others [33]. Documentation-related metrics are used to measure
the quality of the code documentation, while the developer-related metrics are used to
quantify the programmer’s experience, such as the time (in years) spent programming in
general or/and in a speciﬁc programming language. A brief description of the metrics used
for code complexity and comprehensibility assessment [34], that are mentioned throughout
this paper, are presented in Table 1.

Sensors 2021, 21, 2338 5 of 26
Table 1. Brief description of the code complexity software metrics used in the present work.
Code Complexity Metrics Description
Lines of codes Measures the number of lines in the code
Number of Parameters Measures the number of parameters in the code
Weighted Method Count Measures the sum of the complexity of the code methods
McCabe Cyclomatic Complexity Measures the number of paths that are linear independent in the code
Nested Block Depth Measures the maximum nested block depth in the code, i.e., the depth
level of the block (e.g., condition) that is deeper in the code
2.2. Electroencephalography
Code comprehension has a key role in the software development process. An extensive
experimental study [35] shows that programmers spent about 70% time in code compre-
hension tasks, while writing software code. This central role of code comprehension maybe
explains the recent interest of the neuroscience community in researching the brain and
neural mechanisms related to programming tasks, especially code comprehension. Most
of these neuroscience studies rely on complex and intrusive imaging equipment such as
functional magnetic resonance imaging (fMRI) [36–43] and near field infrared spectroscopy
(fNIRS) [44,45]. In this section we will provide a brief background of EEG and discuss related
work on EEG during software development task, due to its relevance for this paper.
EEG can be a powerful tool to help understanding the brain mechanisms behind
code comprehension and subsequently the complexity associated with software codes
and human error while coding. The scalp-EEG is the most frequently used, since it is
non-invasive and it provides a general perspective of the brain to understand the neural
mechanisms [46]. At the scalp level, the signals recorded with EEG are the result of
the activity of populations of pyramidal neurons that when ﬁred in a synchronized way
produce potential differences that cross bone and skin and can be sensed externally in the
microvolts (µV) range. For interpretation, the EEG signal is commonly divided into 5 main
frequency bands (Delta, Theta, Alpha, Beta, and Gamma) [ 47], presented in Table 2. It
should be noted that this is just a simpliﬁed idea because there are subtypes of Alpha and
Theta bands, for example.
Table 2. Electroencephalography (EEG) frequency brain bands and associated brain state.
Name Frequency Range Associated State of Brain
Delta <4 Hz Unconscious/Deep sleep
Theta 4–8 Hz Conscious/Imagination/Memory
Alpha 8–13 Hz Conscious/Relaxed mental activity
Beta 13–30 Hz Conscious/Emotional/Focused
Gamma >30 Hz Conscious/High mental activity
Several research studies reporting the analysis of general task engagement and mental
workload through the acquisition of EEG have been published in the last 20 years and in an
extensive range of ﬁelds [48–50]. In many of them, strong correlations were found between
the cognitive states of the subjects and the EEG frequency bands. The ﬁndings pointed out
mainly to the strength of Theta and Alpha waves to represent these mental states [49–52].
Fritz et al. [19] accomplished the ﬁrst automated approach for the assessment of a
mental workload during code comprehension using psycho-physiological information
(eye-tracker, EEG, and EDA). With data recorded from 15 software programmers, the
authors achieved a F-measure (harmonic mean of the precision and recall) of only 56.73%

Sensors 2021, 21, 2338 6 of 26
using EEG information, which increased up to 67.71% by fusing the information from the
three sensors.
Igor Crk and Timotthy Kluthe [53] performed a study reporting a binary qualiﬁcation
of a programmer’s expertise through code comprehension using recorded EEG signals
from 14 electrodes in 34 participants. By using information of Theta and Alpha bands,
the authors could distinguish the expertise with an accuracy from 53% to 63% depending
on the task. Later, Lee et al. [ 54] also recorded EEG signals, using 13 electrodes, from
18 subjects, to analyze neurophysiological processes occurring during code comprehension
tasks and the possibility to distinguish between expert programmers from beginners. The
results showed that high frequencies are dominant features, namely Beta and Gamma
bands, and the most signiﬁcant channels were from frontal and parietal regions.
Lee et al. [55] conducted a study involving 38 participants with the aim of predicting
their expertise level (novice or expert) or the difﬁculty (easy/difﬁcult) of the comprehension
tasks they performed, through the use of EEG and eye tracking data. The best result
obtained for the prediction study of the difﬁculty of the task was a F-Measure of 66.6%,
while for the study of prediction of the participants’ level of expertise, a F-Measure of 97%
was achieved. Although striking results have been obtained, the authors did not specify
what features were extracted from the recorded EEG, keeping unknown the EEG features
that led to these surprising results.
Yeh et al. [56] carried out a controlled experiment in order to investigate the brain activ-
ity of 8 participants while they were performing code comprehension tasks of 12 confusing
or non-confusing code snippets. Using only eight EEG electrodes from the frontal region of
the brain, the authors computed the power of the Theta and Alpha bands of the signals,
and found for all eight participants, there were signiﬁcant statistical differences between
the two categories of code snippets. Nevertheless, there were no signiﬁcant statistical
differences between the codes of the same category using such EEG features.
The most recent study on this topic was conducted by Kosti et al. [ 57] in 2018 and
had the purpose of investigating the brain activity during two different programming
tasks: Comprehension and inspection of syntax errors in C code. Using only 14 electrodes,
they recorded EEG signals from 10 participants. The authors found that Theta, Beta, and
Gamma waves during comprehension tasks correlate with cognitive effort, with stronger
correlations observed for higher frequency waves. Another surprising ﬁnding was the
detection of a much higher activation of Theta and Beta waves in comprehension tasks
than in tasks of inspection of syntax errors. Authors argued that this was due to the fact
of inspection tasks being considered easier tasks that do not require as much effort in the
imagination of the program output as it is demanded for comprehension tasks.
Despite the encouraging results, most of the authors are aware of the limitations
of such results, either because of the low number of participants or given that there
are few studies in this area, making it difﬁcult to make a comparison of results and
conclusions. Furthermore, the studies that presented high performance did not indicate
the most discriminant features for further investigation of their feasibility as potential
biomarkers, which is a signiﬁcant challenge for reproducing these results. However, such
achievements prompt a further investigation of biosignals such as EEG, which, given
the current technological advances and the existence of low-cost off-the-shell acquisition
devices, are becoming more user friendly [ 27,58]. Furthermore, it can also encourage
the investigation of correlations between EEG biomarkers with the ANS responses, in
order to explore the possibility of replacing EEG by more comfortable and non-intrusive
measurement modalities that allow monitoring of such responses in daily life conditions.
The limited number of studies and the small number of participants make it difﬁcult
to establish measures regarding cognitive load in Software Engineering. Our study differs
from previous ones by providing a systematic analysis using EEG information with a
considerable number of participants and having control elements such as the use of the
NASA TLX tool to assess the subjective complexity of the code, as perceived by participants.
The main aim is to understand the brain activity involved during different types of tasks

Sensors 2021, 21, 2338 7 of 26
and propose the use of EEG as a reference to attest the accuracy of the programmers’
cognitive load code annotation using simple, non-intrusive, and wearable biosensors that
rely only on physiologic manifestations driven by the ANS (and not on direct brain signals
such as EEG).
3. Materials and Methods
3.1. Protocol
Subjects were submitted to three different trials of code comprehension tasks using
three code snippets written in Java. The three code snippets, named as Code 1, Code 2, and
Code 3, have different complexity levels, being Code 1 the simplest code and Code 3 the
most complex one. Each trial consisted of a Control task of text reading in natural language
(60 s maximum) and a task of code comprehension (10 min maximum). Before and after
each task, a screen with a cross in the middle was shown for 30 s to the subject, acting as a
baseline interval for the next task (see Figure 1).
After each trial, the subjects answered two questionnaires. In the first one, the subject
had to explain the purpose of the code and the general idea of the algorithm/code structure
with the goal of knowing whether the participant has understood the code or not. In reality ,
a second, and very important goal of the first questionnaire was to create an incentive for the
participant to really try to understand the code, as the participant was informed beforehand
that he/she would be asked about the code at the end of the trial. In the second questionnaire,
the subject filled a survey based on NASA-TLX (Task-Load Index) survey [59] with four
questions, rating it from 1 to 6, in order to assess the subjective mental effort, task fulfilment,
pressure over time, and frustration of the subject while doing the code comprehension task.
The acquisition protocol is represented in Figure 1 with an estimated experience time
of about one and a half hours for each subject—around 45 min for the preparation of
experimental setup and then 45 min for the trial procedures.

The order of the trials is always the same (i.e., no randomization) to assure that all the
code comprehension tasks were executed in the same conditions by all the participants.
The participants were not informed about the complexity of each program to avoid bias.
The order used was Code 1→ Code 2→ Code 3, being Code 1 the less complex and Code 3
the most complex, at least according to software complexity metrics (as we will see in the
results, participants considered Code 2 and Code 3 as having similar complexity). Figure 2
shows the code complexity metrics of the three programs used in the study. According to
the metrics, differences between the three different codes complexity are visible, being the

Our dataset, experiment protocol and the sample programs (Code 1, Code 2, and
Code3) are publicly available in the repository of the H2020 project AI4EI (A European AI
On Demand Platform and Ecosystem) in the following link: https://ai4eu.dei.uc.pt/base-
mental-effort-monitoring-dataset (accessed on 15 March 2021).
3.2. Participants, Acquisition Setup, and Quality Control
EEG signals were recorded from 30 subjects that were selected after a series of inter-
views. Subjects were students, university professors, and professional software developers,
with experience in Java programming language. Speciﬁcally, from the 30 participants,
24 were male and 6 female, with ages ranging from 19 to 42, and average age of 24 years
old. In addition, through the interview and based on years of experience in Java program-
ming or in the number of lines of code programmed in Java in the last months or years,
the participants were classiﬁed into three levels of proﬁciency: Intermediate, advanced, or
expert, including 13 intermediate, 12 advanced, and 5 expert participants.
EEG signals were acquired using the Neuroscan SynAmps 2 ampliﬁer, from Com-
pumedics, at a sampling frequency of 1000 Hz, with 64 channels placed according to the
International 10-10 system. Neuroscan also included four integrated bipolar leads for EMG,
ECG, and the ocular-movement references VEOG (vertical electrooculogram) and HEOG
(horizontal electrooculogram).
In the acquisition setup designed to record the EEG data, the EEG quick-cap was
connected to the ampliﬁer through the EEG HeadBox, which was connected to the acqui-
sition computer that controls the whole experience, communicating synchronously with
all sensors and to a second computer, used to present the stimuli to the participant and,
additionally, sends the trigger to the acquisition computer (see Figure 3).
