After exploring the frequency band combination across the main dataset, validation dataset, and lastly, the 
combined datasets to further refine the proposed frequency bands, it is important to bridge these results of the 
refined proposed optimal frequency band (0.06-0.29 Hz for LF and 0.29-0.49 Hz for HF). To further validate the 
relevance of the proposed pupil frequency bands, we further analyzed, for example, the feature LF/HF values 
derived from the power of LF and HF bands for both HRV and the optimized pupil frequency bands in relation 
to different code section complexities conditions across all runs and subjects. Code section complexities were 
determined based on McCabe’s Cyclomatic Complexity software metric (V(g)) 78, with sections categorized 
as low complexity when V(g) ≤ 3 and high complexity when V(g) > 3. Comprehensive details regarding the 
examples of code snippets, code sections, and complexity descriptions are available in the online repository.
This additional analysis revealed that HRV-derived LF/HF values increased significantly between low and 
high cognitive load conditions, with a median increase from 3.04 to 3.86 (p = 3.25e-12, α = 0.05). Similarly, 
pupil-derived LF/HF values, based on the optimized frequency bands (0.06-0.29 Hz for LF and 0.29-0.49 Hz for 
HF), showed a comparable trend, with median values increasing from 1.87 to 2.44 (p = 8.99e-4, α = 0.05). Given 
the non-normality of the data and the presence of two independent groups, the Mann-Whitney U test was used 
for statistical comparisons, ensuring robustness and appropriateness of the analysis. These results demonstrate 
that the proposed pupil frequency bands are capable of capturing cognitive load variations in a manner consistent 
with HRV , further supporting their potential as possible surrogate biomarkers for HRV in software tasks.
Although there are promising results reported in this paper, there are still limitations that should be 
discussed as the main threats to the validity of the presented study. First of all, given that the data of this study 
was acquired in a very controlled environment, we will always face limitations in terms of the made-up setup and 
the simulation of a natural software development environment. The complexity of the experiments on studies 
designed like the presented one is inherent. As much as the participants are informed about all the procedures 
and task to be performed, in order to keep them calm and comfortable during the experiment, it is impossible to 
be close to a simulation as close as possible to a real software environment scenario. Nevertheless, by performing 
an additional experiment, in a different environment and under different tasks, to investigate our methodology 
and findings, we tried to minimize the threats to the validation of our study and enhance the robustness of our 
findings.
Regarding the code snippets used in the controlled experiment of the main study, although they were 
carefully chosen during the design of this experiment, in order to represent different characteristics concerning 
complexity (simple/complex) and algorithm type (recursive/iterative), we are aware that the code snippets could 
be larger and with more software bugs, being more closer to real-world software. Nevertheless, for practical 
reasons, we could not use extensive programs as the participants would require a considerable amount of time 
to inspect the codes and detect the software bugs, which would make the experiments unfeasible, especially with 
participants lying down for a long time inside the MRI scanner. Moreover, in the additional experiment, which 
involved mental arithmetic tasks conducted outside the MRI scanner, we had the flexibility to incorporate more 
extensive tasks in terms of duration and complexity, and therefore, we believe it made our analysis more robust 
when comparing and also when grouping both datasets.
Another limitation concerns the dataset, particularly the lack of gender diversity. Despite our effort to gather 
a balanced group of participants during the screening of participants, unfortunately, the percentage of female 
software developers (among both master’ s students and the software industry) is relatively small when compared 
to the male percentage, and the group of participants resulted in not being evenly balanced in gender (21 male 
participants). In future larger datasets, the influence of gender-related factors should also be considered in the 
analysis. Nevertheless, by adding the auxiliary dataset (17 male and 13 female participants), we were able to 
improve the gender balance in the global dataset.
Regarding our approach, our preprocessing steps, including artifact removal and SSA reconstruction, were 
designed to minimize the influence of gaze-related artifacts, ensuring that the key findings remain robust. 
Nevertheless, given some residual influence may remain, future work could explore more advanced methods to 
further refine the accuracy of pupil size measurements and enhance the precision of cognitive load monitoring.

Scientific Reports |         (2025) 15:3361 13| https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-025-85663-2
www.nature.com/scientificreports/

Although this study focused on identifying generalizable frequency bands for assessing cognitive load at the 
group level, individual differences, such as age or other conditions, may lead to slight variations in frequency 
band limits, similar to what occurs in other physiological signals during frequency analysis79,80. Future research 
could explore personalized frequency band analyses to enhance cognitive load monitoring in more tailored 
applications.
In addition, future research could explore the potential of using the present findings to predict software 
development outcomes, such as code review performance and bug detection. Integrating pupillography with 
other physiological and behavioral measures may provide deeper insights into how cognitive load influences 
task performance in real-world software development environments.
Another promising application involves using physiological measurements to identify sections of code with 
high perceived complexity, indicating the need for refactoring. Future work could explore the combination of 
physiological data with other metrics to evaluate code review quality and predict bug detection performance. 
Ultimately, the goal is to improve overall software quality rather than closely monitoring individual developers’ 
performance.
Conclusion
The goal of this study was, through a data-driven approach, to identify the optimal frequency bands of 
pupillography that can approximate the variations observed in the HRV when monitoring the ANS, expanding, 
even more, the adoption and applicability of eye-tracking with pupillography for the assessment of cognitive 
load. Particularly relevant in software development scenarios, where other suggested sensors and setups may 
be impractical or less effective, this sensor not only facilitates cognitive load assessment but also provides gaze 
points of where the programmers are looking during programming tasks. This dual functionality offers a unique 
and comprehensive spatial-temporal resolution for assessing cognitive load during software development 
processes, enabling accurate annotation of code lines or blocks with information related to the cognitive 
load of programmers. This opens new possibilities to predict and avoid software bugs, among other potential 
applications.
From a controlled experiment with 21 programmers, our study provides evidence for the optimal frequency 
bands to be used during pupillography data analysis in the context of software debugging. Our findings highlight 
the LF (0.06-0.29 Hz) and HF (0.29-0.49 Hz) bands as the optimal limit bands, yielding a geometric mean 
correlation of 0.238 of the three features. LF and HF correlations were 0.279 and 0.168, respectively, while LF/
HF ratio correlation was 0.286. In the broader analysis with both datasets, the LF (0.06-0.29 Hz) and HF (0.29-
0.49 Hz) bands maintained significance, with a geometric mean of 0.236. LF and HF correlations were 0.270 and 
0.182, and LF/HF ratio correlation was 0.270. These values are overall higher compared to the ones using the 
limits of the frequency bands reported in the literature of the pupillography, and therefore, those features are the 
best to be used for the assessment of the programmer’s cognitive load in the context of dynamic and complex 
tasks such as software development tasks or similar tasks.

Chapter 7
Enhancing Code Review Quality and
Effectiveness
T
his chapter presents the final study of this work on the use of multimodal
approach to assess code review quality. It explores both high- and low-
intrusiveness techniques, identifying practical solutions for real-world ap-
plications. The study is introduced with its context and key findings, followed by
the full text of the respective paper.
The study,Benchmarking the Accuracy of Biosensors in Predicting Bug Finding
Effectiveness, evaluates the accuracy and reliability of cognitive load measures from
different modalities (from high-intrusiveness techniques like fMRI and EEG to lower-
intrusiveness sensors such as ECG and Eye-Tracking with Pupillography) by com-
paring their performance on code review quality prediction. The research highlights
that sophisticated intrusive biosensors such as fMRI and EEG do not outperform
non-intrusive biosensors in predicting bug-finding effectiveness, and suggests that
Pupillography or HRV combined with Eye-tracking are viable solutions for industry
settings.
Medeiros, J., Hijazi, H., Durães, J., Couceiro, R., Castelhano, J., Madeira, H.,
Teixeira, C. & de Carvalho, P. (Under Review). Benchmarking the Accuracy of
Biosensors in Predicting Bug Finding Effectiveness. IEEE Transactions on Software
Engineering. Impact Factor: 6.5 (2024); Q1.
141

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON SOFTW ARE ENGINEERING 1
Benchmarking the Accuracy of Biosensors in
Predicting Bug Finding Effectiveness
J´ulio Medeiros, Haytham Hijazi, Jo ˜ao Dur ˜aes, Ricardo Couceiro, Jo ˜ao Castelhano, C ´esar Teixeira,
Henrique Madeira, and Paulo de Carvalho
Abstract—Despite substantial research progress in using neu-
rophysiological biosensors to track developers’ cognitive states
(e.g., cognitive load, engagement, stress, and fatigue), there are
still no tangible examples of these techniques being adopted in the
software industry. The prevailing view is that reliable biosensors,
such as EEG, are too intrusive for practical use, while low or
non-intrusive biosensors lack sufficient reliability to be effectively
applied in real-world settings. This paper compares the reliability
of a comprehensive range of biosensors, including fMRI, EEG,
ECG/HRV , and Eye-tracking with Pupillography, with the main
goal of determine the viability of using low or non-intrusive
biosensors in real setups to accurately assess/predict the success
rate of relevant software development tasks. We used code review
as a benchmark task to compare biosensors due to the cognitive
complexity of bug finding. The experiment evaluates the accuracy
of bug-finding prediction models trained with features from
different biosensors, providing concrete insights into the practical
applications of non-intrusive biosensors. Our results show that
sophisticated intrusive biosensors such as fMRI and EEG do not
outperform simple non-intrusive biosensors in predicting bug-
finding effectiveness. In contrast, non-intrusive biosensors such
as HRV combined with code reading behavior captured by eye
tracking achieve the best results, detecting correctly at least 85%
of the bad reviews, representing more than 81% of undetected
bugs.
Index Terms —Software Engineering, Code Review Quality,
Bug Finding, Error mitigation, Biofeedback, Biosignals, fMRI,
ECG, EEG, Eye-tracking, Pupillography
I. I NTRODUCTION
The integration of biosensors in software engineering is an
emerging field focusing on enhancing software development
efficiency and improving software reliability by monitoring
programmers’ cognitive states during tasks like programming,
code reviewing, and testing. Cognitive states such as program-
mers’ mental overload, attention shifts, stress, and fatigue are
critical during high-demand intellectual tasks and have been
linked to error-prone scenarios [1], [2].
This work was supported in part through national funds by FCT - Fundac ¸˜ao
para a Ci ˆencia e a Tecnologia, I.P., under the projects UIDB/00326/2025 and
UIDP/00326/2025; and in part by FCT through the project BASE (POCI-01-
0145-FEDER-031581) and the Ph.D. grant 2020.07906.BD. (Corresponding
authors: J ´ulio Medeiros.)
J´ulio Medeiros, Haytham Hijazi, Ricardo Couceiro, C ´esar Teixeira,
Henrique Madeira, and Paulo de Carvalho are with University of Coimbra,
CISUC/LASI, DEI (e-mail: juliomedeiros@dei.uc.pt; haytham@dei.uc.pt;
rcouceir@dei.uc.pt; cteixei@dei.uc.pt; henrique@dei.uc.pt; car-
valho@dei.uc.pt).
Jo˜ao Dur˜aes is with Polytechnic University of Coimbra, and with University
of Coimbra, CISUC/LASI, DEI (e-mail: jduraes@isec.pt).
Jo˜ao Castelhano is with University of Coimbra, ICNAS (e-mail: joaocastel-
hano@uc.pt).
Physiological data such as brain activity captured through
brain imaging tools, heart rate variability (HRV), and eye
movement patterns, among others, have been used by re-
searchers to assess programmers’ cognitive states and help
understand basic cognitive mechanisms related to code devel-
opment, leading to a variety of proposals to improve testing
effectiveness, evaluate code review efficacy, or identify prob-
lematic code snippets in terms of code comprehension, among
many others [3]–[6]. This emerging and highly active research
area was termed NeuroSE in a 2021 survey [7]. However,
despite the large number of works published in recent years
on such topics, none of these proposals is actually used in
real-world software development settings.
The main reason behind the slow adoption by the software
industry of new methods and tools resulting from inter-
disciplinary research combining neuroscience and software
engineering is the high intrusiveness of most biosensors
used to collect neurophysiological data from developers during
software development tasks. For example, functional magnetic
resonance imaging (fMRI), electroencephalography (EEG),
and functional near-infrared spectroscopy (fNIRS) are simply
impossible to be used in real software development setups, not
only because of their high intrusiveness but also due to their
complexity and high cost.
An alternative to complex techniques that measure brain
activity (fMRI, EEG, fNIRS), is to use methods that gather
physiologic data driven by the autonomic nervous system
(ANS) [6]–[8], such as heart rate variability (HRV), electro-
dermal activity (EDA), pupillometry, and even code reading
patterns captured by eye trackers, as these signals reflect
underlying cognitive states and brain activity. These indirect
methods to assess cognitive states can be used in real software
development setups, especially using modern non-intrusive
devices to collect the ANS signals, such as bracelets and
smartwatches, but are considered less reliable than the methods
based on signals directly collected from the brain.
The main goal of this paper is to answer the following ques-
tion: Are low-intrusive sensors, compatible with existing
software development setups, accurate enough to assess
programmers’ cognitive load and other cognitive states?
We designed a comprehensive and sophisticated experi-
ment including a wide range of multimodal biosensors such
as fMRI, EEG, ECG/HRV , pupillography, and code reading
behavior captured by eye-tracking. Given the complexity of
this setup, aggravated by the restrictions imposed by the use
of highly expensive tools such as fMRI and EEG, which
are shared with other projects and clinical applications, the

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON SOFTW ARE ENGINEERING 2
entire experiment, from design to data collection, lasted 15
months. The result is a unique dataset that integrates the
physiological data collected from the 21 programmers who
participated in the experiments while performing a complex
software development task. The dataset is publicly avail-
able (https://github.com/Julio-CMedeiros/TSE-Benchmarking-
the-accuracy-of-biosensors-bug-finding-effectiveness) to pro-
mote reproducibility and encourage other researchers and
practitioners to conduct further analyses on the results.
In addition to the key research question mentioned above,
the general goal of the dataset is to provide a benchmarking
baseline for comparing and assessing accuracy in evaluating
cognitive states when transitioning from highly sophisticated
biosensors used in research, such as fMRI, to much simpler
ones such as HRV , which can be measured by a wristband
or smartwatch fully compatible with software development
environments. Since the dataset contains synchronized physi-
ological signals from all biosensors recorded for each partic-
ipant while executing the same task, it establishes a reliable
baseline for benchmarking, enabling a credible assessment of
whether simple and non-intrusive devices can assess program-
mers’ cognitive states with accuracy comparable to that of
sophisticated (and intrusive) biosensors.
An important aspect of the benchmarking enabled by our
experimental dataset is the task performed by the participants
(i.e., the cognitive workload). We selected code reviews as
the target task for our study because code reviews are cog-
nitively demanding, requiring code comprehension, bug de-
tection, and decision-making. Notably, code reviews allow for
the challenging objective of assessing and predicting review
quality in terms of bug-finding effectiveness, which is a more
comprehensive goal than simply comparing the accuracy of
cognitive load measurements obtained from different biosen-
sors. The key challenge is to infer the quality of a given code
review using neurophysiological data collected directly from
the reviewer during the review process.
Modern code reviews [9], [10], often associated with pull
requests in distributed version control systems like Git, are an
essential practice in software development. Unlike traditional
Fagan inspections [11], which required a group of inspectors
and were costly, modern code reviews typically involve just
one reviewer and are conducted asynchronously (i.e., without
a review meeting). This approach allows for reviewing most
of the code of a given project, but its main weakness is the
reliance on a single reviewer. Therefore, showing that the
accuracy achievable in predicting the quality of a code review
based on physiological data collected from the reviewers is
crucial for assessing the feasibility of enhancing code reviews
with biosensor data in real-world settings.
Code comprehension activities, often in the context of code
reviewing, represent around 58% of the average time spent by
developers in code development [12]. Furthermore, emerging
trends in software development, such as the rapidly increasing
use of machine-generated code, support our choice of code
review as the target task for our study.
In fact, the rapid growth of automatically generated code
using Large Language Models (LLMs), such as Copilot and
ChatGPT, has a promising potential to improve code develop-
ment efficiency but also poses a significant risk of increasing
software defects. AI-generated code still requires considerable
human intervention before deployment, emphasizing the im-
portance of code understanding and making code reviews more
demanding, as LLMs can introduce unique types of bugs that
differ from those typically created by humans [13].
LLMs may provide accurate code for simple tasks but their
accuracy tends to deteriorate with more complex requirements
(i.e., those that fall outside of the training space) [14]. This
can lead to a wide range of potential bugs, particularly since
the code generated by LLMs can be difficult for humans to
understand, complicating the process of debugging and fixing
such code. This is a common perception among developers,
corroborated by empirical studies such as [13] reporting ques-
tionnaire results showing that 80% of the users often or always
experience bugs when using LLMs to generate code. A recent
study from Stanford University [15] suggests that developers
who use AI-generated code might produce less secure code
than those who do not. These findings highlight the growing
importance of assessing programmers’ cognitive states and
monitoring program comprehension challenges, particularly
when reviewing code generated with the assistance of AI-
powered tools. This aligns fully with the target task used in
our experiments.
The paper has the following contributions:
‚ Systematically evaluates the performance of biosensors
in assessing code review quality, starting with highly
intrusive techniques and progressively evaluating more
practical and less intrusive methods. This approach high-
lights the trade-offs between accuracy, intrusiveness, cost,
and practicality, providing insights into which setups are
viable for real-world software engineering tasks.
‚ Provides clear guidance for adopting non-intrusive so-
lutions such as Eye-Tracking and HRV in industrial
settings. Our results demonstrate how such tools can
enhance software quality by improving code review
processes while remaining practical and accessible for
everyday use.
‚ Explores, as an additional analysis layer, the complemen-
tary role of different software complexity metrics when
combined with the biosensors data for code review quality
assessment.
‚ Offers a comprehensive experimental study comprising
an extensive and complex multimodal dataset collected
during code review software tasks that require code
comprehension, bug detection, and decision-making. This
contribution includes the online availability of a package
including the protocols, dataset collected, proposed meth-
ods, and results.
The paper is structured as follows: Section II provides an
overview of the motivation behind our experimental design
and the broader context within the NeuroSE research field.
Section III describes the experimental protocol and data col-
lection. Section IV presents the methodologies concerning the
preprocessing and feature engineering for all biosignals of
interest and the respective machine learning approaches for
code review evaluation assessment. Section V presents the key

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON SOFTW ARE ENGINEERING 3
findings and the implications for software engineering. Section
VI presents a discussion of the threats to the validity of the
study. Finally, Section VII concludes the paper.
II. V ISION , MOTIVATION AND GOAL OF THE PROPOSED
LARGE SCALE EXPERIMENT
A. Current State on NeuroSE
The assessment of cognitive load has been explored in
different fields for decades [16]–[18], but its application in
software engineering has only recently begun to receive sig-
nificant attention. Cognitive load, defined as the total mental
effort needed to process information and complete a task, can
be divided into three types: intrinsic (related to task complex-
ity), extraneous (related to how information is presented), and
germane (related to learning and problem-solving) cognitive
load [19]. These three types together form the total cognitive
load of a task. In Software Engineering, particularly in the
NeuroSE research field [7], the term ’cognitive load’ is often
broadly used to describe the overall mental effort exerted
by software programmers during task performance. For the
purposes of this paper, we adopt this broader definition of
cognitive load to refer to the total mental effort exerted by
participants during software-related tasks. Understanding and
assessing the cognitive load is a fundamental key to improving
guidance in task performance and to reducing potential costs
in the software industry.
There are mainly three primary ways to assess directly or
indirectly the cognitive load of subjects: subjective self-rated
evaluations like the NASA Task Load Index [20], performance
measures such as task performance accuracy and response
time, and physiological measures such as autonomic and/or
central nervous system activity. The latter is the most promis-
ing one that can be deeply explored and used for reliable,
non-intrusively and real-time monitoring of the programmer’s
cognitive load as a key element during the software develop-
ment process.
The early research in this area often relied on heavy
and highly intrusive sensors, such as electroencephalography
(EEG), functional magnetic resonance imaging (fMRI), and
functional near-infrared spectroscopy (fNIRS), to explore brain
mechanisms and extract valuable information about brain
activity, contribution to the general understanding of the brain
mechanisms involved in software development tasks [3], [4],
[21]–[40].
Another research direction has focused on evaluating pro-
grammers’ cognitive load using indirect approaches based on
sensors that capture ANS-driven signals [6], [41]–[44]. These
studies have predominantly focused on electrocardiography
(ECG), electrodermal activity (EDA), and eye-tracking with
pupillography. All these sensors are currently available as low
intrusive devices such as wristbands, bracelets, smartwatches,
and simple desktop eye trackers. These studies have used such
variety of sensors (and associated pipeline of techniques) either
to classify task difficulty, assess programmer expertise, and
evaluate code comprehension and code programming difficulty
[6], [29], [41], [42], [45].
An emerging research direction, has investigated the use
of low-intrusive biosensors and wearables to enhance code
review quality by assessing programmers’ cognitive load. For
example, Hijazi et al. [5], [46] explored the use of features
extracted from heart rate variability (HRV) and from the Eye-
tracking with pupillography in this context [6], [47], [48].
HRV and pupillography were used to monitor the autonomic
nervous system (ANS) and indirectly assess the cognitive
load of the programmers, while eye-tracking provided data on
the behavior of the programmers’ gaze patterns, such as the
number of revisits. These studies have shown promising results
with low-intrusive sensors, but challenges remain regarding the
accuracy and reliability of the inferred cognitive load derived
from ANS-based signals, which are generally less precise than
those obtained from direct brain activity monitoring.
Furthermore, despite those promising insights and even
some concrete proposals of tools [46], the reality is that none
of these NeuroSE studies have led to methods or tools that
managed to be adopted by the software industry. While low-
intrusive sensors raise concerns about accuracy and reliability,
there is also a major challenge in the impracticality of high-
intrusive sensors. In addition, there is a clear absence of a
standardized way to choose the right biosensor modalities. On
top of that, there is also a lack of a standard pipeline package
for preprocessing, feature engineering, and robust machine
learning model design, making it difficult to reproduce and
obtain the results consistently. Addressing these challenges is
essential for making NeuroSE approaches viable for real-world
applications.
B. Proposed Experiment, Justification and Discussion
The key idea behind our study is to design and implement
a controlled experiment in which each participant (developer)
performs a representative and relevant software development
task (code reviews) while being monitored using a wide
range of biosensors. These include sensors that capture signals
directly from the brain (EEG and fMRI) and biosensors
that collect signals driven by the autonomic nervous system
(ECG/HRV and pupillography). Additionally, the participant’s
code reading/reviewing behavior is captured by an eye tracker.
All these signals are synchronized and globally provide a
unique and comprehensive dataset to evaluate how non-
intrusive biosensors compare with sophisticated neurophysi-
ological tools such as EEG and neuroimaging tools such as
fMRI.
These sensors vary in their degree of intrusiveness and
provide different types of cognitive insights. Biosensors that
measure neural activity associated with the central nervous
system, such as EEG and fMRI, can only be used in re-
search experiments due to their high level of intrusiveness.
For example, most EEG devices, particularly those used in
research, require a cap with electrodes, which is impractical for
real-world software development settings, while fMRI is even
more restrictive due to the strong magnetic fields of the fMRI
scanner. However, since these tools measure brain activity,
they are generally considered to provide the most accurate
assessment of a participant’s cognitive load while performing
a task. Furthermore, the use of both EEG and fMRI allows us
to combine these two biosensors to obtain the best contribution

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON SOFTW ARE ENGINEERING 4
of their different features to assess participant’s cognitive load.
It is well known that fMRI provides high spatial resolution,
offering neuroscience insights into the activation of relevant
brain regions [3], [40], while EEG captures better rapid
temporal fluctuations in cognitive load with high precision
[36], which justify the simultaneous use of both tools for a
more comprehensive analysis of cognitive load in software
engineering tasks.
The use of low-intrusive biosensors (ECG/HRV , pupillog-
raphy, and eye trackers) in conjunction with EEG and fMRI
allows us to compare the results obtained from the different
biosensors, as well as all possible combinations, to identify the
best alternatives for integrating low-intrusive biosensors into
real-world software development practices. Specifically, our
goal is to establish the real feasibility of transitioning from
potentially accurate but highly intrusive sensors, such as EEG
and fMRI, to much simpler and nearly non-intrusive sensors,
such as HRV and pupillography, supported by robust machine-
learning models.
To the best of our knowledge, the dataset obtained from
the proposed experiment is the only known dataset that com-
bines such a comprehensive set of biosensors (EEG, fMRI,
ECG/HRV , pupillography, and eye tracking) in a controlled
experiment using real code reviews as the target task. In
addition to the biosignals collected during the experiment,
the dataset also includes other relevant inputs, such as a
complete characterization of the complexity of the different
code snippets under review (measured using a variety of
software complexity metrics) and data on the programmers’
expertise.
A key aspect is the selected type of software engineering
task used in the experiment. Most of the NeuroSE studies [7]
use code comprehension tasks and focus on assessing partic-
ipants’ cognitive load or understanding the brain mechanisms
involved in code reading. We chose code reviews as the target
task for two main reasons. First, it is a more complex task
that involves code reading, comprehension, bug detection, and
decision-making processes. Second, participants’ performance
in bug detection provides a meaningful ground truth for the
evaluation of the effectiveness of the code reviews, measured
by the number of found bugs (true positives), missed bugs
(false negatives), and wrongly identified bugs (false positives).
In practice, the different code snippets under review were
previously seeded with realistic bugs, allowing the easy as-
sessment of the effectiveness of each code review performed
in the experiment since we know the exact number of bugs
existing in each code snippet. The realism of the bugs inserted
is assured by the use of state of the art software fault injection
methods, as established by Dur ˜aes et al. [49], [50].
The fact that we measured the effectiveness of each code
review performed in the experiment allows for a more com-
prehensive comparison of the different biosensors. Instead of
comparing the accuracy of cognitive load assessments based
on signals from different biosensors (assuming EEG and fMRI
as reference measures) we can evaluate biosensors based
on their ability to predict code review quality in terms of
bug detection effectiveness. Specifically, for each biosensor
(or combination of biosensors), we trained a set of machine
learning models to predict code review quality. We then tested
these models using biosignals from participants who were not
included in the training set to assess each biosensor’s ability
to predict code review effectiveness. This approach provides
a more effective comparison of the different biosensors, as
it eliminates the assumption that cognitive load measured by
EEG and fMRI serves as the definitive ground truth for review-
ers’ cognitive load, which is an assumption that is inherently
difficult to prove. In summary, we compare biosensors based
on their ability to predict bug-finding effectiveness, using
each participant’s measured performance in each review as
the ground truth.
III. D ATASET AND PROTOCOL
In this section, we describe the characteristics of the par-
ticipants, the experimental protocol, and the data acquisition
setup used in our controlled experimental study.
A. Participants
For this study, 21 participants with experience in C pro-
gramming language and code inspection were selected from
a pool of 49 candidates. The participants were all male,
with ages ranging from 19 to 40 years old, and an average
age of 25.56 ˘ 6.85 years old. The screening process was
done through a series of interviews, and the participants were
selected based on their responses to two questionnaires: one
assessing their programming experience over the past three
years and another evaluating their coding skills through a
series of 10 questions. Candidates with a score lower than 3
out of 10 points on the technical questionnaire were considered
ineligible. Consequently, we categorized the 21 programmers
into two proficiency levels: 16 participants were classified as
intermediate (scoring between 4 and 7 points in the technical
questionnaire), while 5 participants were identified as experts
(scoring between 8 and 10 points).
B. Experiment Protocol
Participants were provided with four distinct runs of code
comprehension and bug inspection tasks using different C
programming language code snippets. Each run comprised a
control condition of natural language text reading (up to 60
seconds), a condition of basic code comprehension (up to 5
minutes), and a task of code inspection for bug detection (up
to 10 minutes). Before and after each phase, participants had
a condition of a centered cross displayed on the screen for
30 seconds, establishing a baseline period for the subsequent
conditions. To avoid any measurement bias, the sequence of
the three main conditions and code snippets examples were
randomized independently for each participant and across all
four runs.
After finishing each run, the participant answers two ques-
tionnaires: the first one was related to questions about the
inspected code snippets to assess whether the participant has
understood the code or not (and also to induce focus on the
tasks, as the participants were informed beforehand about
the questionnaire); the second was to record the subjective

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON SOFTW ARE ENGINEERING 5
evaluation of the participant over the whole experiment - the
participant filled a questionnaire based on NASA-TLX survey,
rating his/her subjective mental effort, task fulfillment, pres-
sure over time, and frustration felt during the code inspection
and bug finding task.

Fig. 1. Representative schematics of the experiment protocol with an

The experiment protocol is illustrated in Fig 1, with the
total estimated time for each subject being less than two hours.
This includes approximately 30-45 minutes for setting up the
experimental equipment and up to 74 minutes for completing
all task procedures.
The four code snippets used for the code inspection and
bug detection condition tasks - Bucket Sort, Fibonacci, Hondt
Method, and Matrix Determinant — were chosen and designed
with the goal of representing different code characteristics
regarding complexity (simple / complex) and algorithm type
(recursive / iterative) (see Table I). These code snippets were
further divided into smaller regions defined according to the
language code constructs (see section IV-C for more details).
Traditional software quality metrics - McCabe’s Cyclomatic
Complexity V(g), SonarQube (CC Sonar), Lines of Code
(LoC), Halstead Effort (HEff), and Halstead Difficulty (HDiff)
- were computed for the entire code snippets as well as for
the code regions. More details about the rules and conditions
used to identify the code regions (invisible for the partici-
pants and used only for results analysis) are provided in the
section Methods (see Regions Structure & Software Metrics
Section IV-C).

The Bucket Sort snippet, an iterative sorting algorithm,
is medium-sized and complex, with four injected bugs. In
contrast, the Fibonacci snippet, a small and simple recur-
sive function, contains only one bug. The Hondt Method,
an iterative algorithm used for seat allocation in elections,
despite its small size, exhibits medium-complex code with
four bugs. Lastly, the Matrix Determinant snippet, a recursive
algorithm for computing the determinant of square matrices,
is a medium-sized and complex code snippet, also containing
four bugs. The injected bugs across these programs represent
different real-world fault types, such as checking, assignment,
and algorithmic errors, following the methodology proposed
in [49], [50].
The order in which these snippets were presented to partic-
ipants was randomized and varied independently for each par-
ticipant. Regarding the bugs, the bugs injected in our study’s
code snippets were designed to reflect realistic scenarios,
avoiding syntax errors or uncommon language/library issues.
All relevant data, including i) the experimental pro-
tocol, ii) screening and experimental questionnaires, iii)
NASA-TLX and bug detection evaluation data, iv) code
snippets with bug locations and complexity, as well as
EEG, fMRI, ECG, and eye-tracking with pupillography data
(with all participant information fully anonymized), is pub-
licly available in the online repository at the following
link: https://github.com/Julio-CMedeiros/TSE-Benchmarking-
the-accuracy-of-biosensors-bug-finding-effectiveness.
C. Acquisition Setup and Quality Control
During the experiment, we collected simultaneous and syn-
chronized recordings of EEG, fMRI, ECG, eye-tracking, and
pupillography data from the software programmers’ partici-
pants. Regarding the acquisition setup, the recordings were
carried out with participants lying inside the MRI scanner in
a comfortable position. EEG signals were recorded using the
Neuroscan SynAmps 2 amplifier with 64 channels, including
four for EMG, ECG, and eye movement references [51]. For
ECG, electrodes were placed at V1 and V2 positions [52],
and both EEG and ECG were sampled at 10 kHz. fMRI data
were collected with a 3T Prisma MRI scanner using a 20-
channel head coil [53]. Eye movements and pupillography
were captured with the EyeLink 1000 Plus Eye Tracker at
500 Hz [54]. During the experiments, the triggers were also
recorded when participants marked or confirmed suspicious
code lines as containing bugs.
After the experiments, the analysis and evaluation of the
collected data resulted in the exclusion of some participants
to maintain data integrity. Consequently, in order to ensure
that only high-quality (in all four biosensors, with no excep-
tion) were retained for the comprehensive analysis using all
biosignals, the dataset was reduced from 21 to 13 subjects.
The data collection was authorized by all the participants
involved by informed written consent, and the study was ap-
proved by the Ethics Committee of the Faculty of Medicine of
the University of Coimbra, in accordance with the Declaration
of Helsinki.
IV. M ETHODS
This section describes the methodologies used in this study.

A. Preprocessing & Feature Extraction
The preprocessing step is mandatory for cleaning the
recorded data as much as possible and guaranteeing a reli-
able analysis of the post-processed signals. In the following
subsections, we describe the preprocessing of the biosensors
used in our study.
1) fMRI: The functional magnetic resonance imaging
(fMRI) analysis was conducted using BrainV oyager QX 2.8
(Brain Innovation). Preprocessing of the functional data was
performed with default parameters, which included cubic
spline interpolation for slice scan time correction, trilinear 3D
motion correction, and high-pass filtering implemented using
a Fourier basis set with two cycles. Structural and functional
datasets were coregistered and normalized to Talairach stan-
dard space. Detailed processing steps are described in previous
works [3].
To extract cognitive load features, brain activity asso-
ciated with source-code review was identified through a
random-effects General Linear Model (GLM) multi-study
analysis [55]. This analysis contrasted source-code review
blocks against baseline conditions, generating a statistical
map of brain activation. Subsequently, the blood-oxygen-level-
dependent (BOLD) signal was extracted from the identified
brain network, specifically targeting the right parahippocampal
gyrus (Brodmann Area 36), which is associated with episodic
memory, novel association formation, and spatial navigation,
as well as the premotor area. These extracted signals were
used as physiological inputs representing cognitive load for
the code review evaluation model. The temporal resolution
of the extracted features was 3 seconds, consistent with the
repetition time (TR) of the fMRI acquisition.
2) EEG: As a first step of EEG preprocessing, an MR-
induced artifact correction was performed using the FMRIB
plug-in for EEGLAB, provided by the University of Oxford
Centre for Functional MRI of the Brain (FMRIB) [56], [57].
Here, in this context, the ”Artifacts” term refers to unwanted
electrical signals or noise that can contaminate, in this case,
EEG recordings. These can arise from external factors, such as
interference from the MRI scanner (environmental artifacts),
or from physiological sources, such as participant movements,
muscle and cardiac activity, eye movements and eye blinks,
among others (physiological artifacts). Regardless of their
source, these artifacts must be identified and removed through
software processing to ensure the integrity of the data.
First, we addressed the gradient artifact (GA) using an
average artifact subtraction (AAS) approach based on the algo-

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON SOFTW ARE ENGINEERING 7
rithm from Niazy et al. [56]. This method involves creating an
artifact template considering the number of MRI acquisition
volumes and applying a temporal principal component analysis
(PCA) on each channel to form an optimal basis set (OBS) to
estimate and subtract residual artifacts. After GA reduction,
the EEG signals were downsampled to 1000Hz. Next, to
remove the ballistocardiogram (BCG) artifact, we applied an
AAS approach with PCA and OBS, also proposed by Niazy
et al. [56]. This required an R-peak detection on the ECG
signal to obtain QRS complex events for constructing the BCG
template in the AAS method.
Following the correction of MR-induced artifacts, FIR filters
with Hamming sinc window were applied to EEG signals.
Firstly, a high-pass filter with a cut-off frequency at 1Hz and
then a low-pass filter with a cut-off frequency at 45Hz. The
filter orders used were estimated heuristically by the default
filter order mode (transition bandwidth set to 25% of the lower
passband edge, with a minimum threshold of 2 Hz).
Afterwards, a visual inspection of the EEG data was per-
formed in the time domain, and bad channels were identified,
removed, and interpolated using the spherical spline interpo-
lation algorithm from Perrin et al. [58]. The data was then re-
referenced using an average reference approach by averaging
all 60 channels and performing a linear transformation.
To remove remaining artifacts (ocular, muscle, cardiac,
or residual MR-induced signal that are called ”artifacts”
in biomedical terminology), independent component analysis
(ICA) was applied for blind source separation (BSS). EEG
epoching with 1.5-second epochs was performed, rejecting
epochs with large muscular activity or non-stationary data
[59]. The Extended Infomax algorithm [60] was used to
compute ICA components. The artifact-related components
were removed, and the data was back-reconstructed to the
original space without the artifacts present in the independent
components.
Then a hand-crafted feature engineering approach was fol-
lowed, incorporating features commonly reported in cognitive
load assessment studies related to code comprehension or
inspection tasks [27], [29], [36], [61]. These features were
selected to bridge cognitive load with code review quality,
providing a basis for evaluating how cognitive load, as one of
the primary inputs, impacts code review effectiveness.
Frequency content has been correlated with cognitive load
and is commonly used when assessing cognitive load via
EEG signals. A 1-second window with 80% overlap was
used to compute the power spectrum density (PSD), and two
power-related features that have been showing potential in
assessing cognitive load were extracted. The frequency ranges
considered for the three relevant frequency bands of interest
were Theta (4–8Hz), Alpha (8–13Hz), and Beta (13–30Hz).
The cognitive load index “Brainbeat” was showed by Holm
et al. [62] and recent studies [27], [29] to be a powerful
feature in estimating cognitive load during tasks through the
ratio between powers of two frequency bands from frontal and
parietal brain locations:
”Brainbeat” Index“ θPowerpFzq
αPowerpPzq (1)
Task engagement indexes, first reported by Pope et al.
[63] and recently being used in recent studies, are ratios
between Theta, Alpha, and Beta power bands, and were being
widely used for two possible representative indexes of the
participants’ engagement during tasks [27], [29], [64], [65]:

βPower `αPower (2)
3) ECG: Like EEG, initial preprocessing was essential to
address the gradient artifact (GA) induced by the MRI scanner
on the ECG signals. To this end, an average artifact subtraction
(AAS) technique based on the algorithm from Niazy et al.
[56] was also performed to reduce this artifact on ECG data.
Afterwards, the ECG was downsampled to 1000Hz. Besides
the GA correction, the ECG presents some changes in its
morphology due to the magnetic field produced by the MRI
machine. Specifically, the ECG signal tends to exhibit a larger
T-wave than the QRS complex and an R-wave with reduced
amplitude. Consequently, traditional QRS detection algorithms
tend to fail and lead to incorrect R-R intervals calculation.
To address this issue, we performed the R-peak detection
algorithm proposed by Christov et al. [66], which is widely
used due to its robustness and high performance in scenarios
when ECG signals are recorded within an MRI scanner.
Following the detection of R-peaks, we computed the R-R
intervals to obtain the HRV time series [67].
After the computation of HRV , we implemented additional
processing steps to enhance the quality of the HRV time
series. If any outliers were present, they were identified using
a boxplot analysis technique, as described in [68], and further
removed. Subsequently, we performed linear interpolation and
resampling to 8Hz to ensure data uniformity [6]. To concen-
trate on essential information relevant to the study, specifically
the low-frequency and high-frequency bands of HRV , a low-
pass FIR filter with Hamming sinc window was applied with
a cut-off frequency at 1Hz (the filter order was also estimated
heuristically by the default filter order mode).
For the feature extraction, a 30-second sliding window with
a step of 1 second was used to estimate the PSD for the
HRV series and compute the frequency features. For the HRV
time series, for each sliding window, we extracted the power
in the well-established low-frequency (LF: 0.04–0.15Hz) and
high-frequency (HF: 0.15–0.40Hz) bands and computed the
LF/HF ratio, well-known as a relevant feature when assessing
cognitive load [5], [6].
4) Eye-tracker and Pupillography : The first step in pre-
processing involved discarding all the pupil diameter (PD)
samples identified as inaccurate, which included those marked
as invalid by the eye-tracking device. Additionally, we also
excluded samples occurring 100 milliseconds before the onset
and 100 milliseconds after the offset of the flagged invalid
samples [44]. Subsequent to this initial step, we noticed
certain pupil diameter (PD) values in the pupillography signal
that indicated quick or abnormal pupil dilations, deviating
significantly from the expected trend. To address this issue, we
performed an outlier detection step using a boxplot analysis
technique, as described in [68], to detect and remove these

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON SOFTW ARE ENGINEERING 8
irregular readings. After removing all outliers and inaccurate
readings, we applied a shape-preserving piecewise cubic in-
terpolation to fill in the excluded values, followed by down-
sampling the resulting pupillography signal to 8 Hz [44].
Additionally, to further mitigate the impact of artifacts,
especially those introduced by eye blinks and external factors,
on the pupillography time series, we performed an algorithm
based on Singular Spectrum Analysis (iterative SSA) [44],
[69]–[71]. This algorithm effectively removes and fills in the
data affected by these artifacts, as highlighted by Nakayama
et al. [72]. Using the iterative SSA approach, we aim to
reduce the influence of artifacts, enhancing the reliability of the
frequency domain features extracted from the pupillography
signal [72].
Finally, the pupillography signal was filtered using a high-
pass FIR filter with a Hamming sinc window, set to a cut-off
frequency of 4ˆ 10´4 Hz. This was done to mitigate the
influence of medium-term nonstationary components within
the analyzed time interval, as suggested by Eleuteri et al. [44],
[73].
In feature extraction, similar to the HRV features, a 30-
second sliding window with a step of 1 second was also
applied to estimate the PSD of the pupillography signal and to
compute the frequency features. For each sliding window, we
extracted the power in the well-established low-frequency (LF:
0.04-0.15Hz) and high-frequency (HF: 0.15-0.40Hz) bands,
and computed also the the LF/HF ratio as our main feature
of Pupillography. Additionally, based on a recent research
study [74], we also computed the LF/HF ratio based on new
proposed optimal frequency bands for LF (0.06-0.29Hz) and
HF (0.29-0.49 Hz) bands when performing frequency domain
analysis of pupillography signals.
Still regarding the eye-tracking modality, but now specifi-
cally to the gaze data, we computed the fixations, saccades,
their respective durations, as well as saccades velocity. Then,
we computed second-order features to capture the dynamics
of such features for each inspected region (further detailed in
section IV-C), including mean, standard deviation, duration,
total count, and maximum, minimum lengths and velocity for
saccades. In addition to these features, we also computed the
number of times that the programmer spent at least one second
or more inside a region of the code snippet, i.e., the number
of visits/revisits for each region of the code snippets, and also
computed the total time spent in each region. This allows us
to extract information about the programmer’s review gaze
behavior during the review task.
B. F eature Normalization and Transformation
After preprocessing and feature extraction, for EEG, fMRI,
HRV and Pupillography, each feature value of these signals
was normalized to reduce inter-subject variability and intra-
subject variability throughout the experiments [27], [75]. More
specifically, regarding intra-subject variability, this normaliza-
tion step is crucial, even in small-duration experiments, to try
to minimize the influence of eventual external factors such as
fatigue. Given the design of our experimental study, where
there are neutral load events (e.g., the event of the cross),
it is possible to normalize the features values with respect
to that baseline event. Thus, each cross-event was used for
the normalization of the baseline of the next event, i.e., the
first cross normalized the reading task while the second cross
normalized the code inspection task. The final value of each
feature becomes a variation with respect to the reading text
task [75]:
∆FeatureC,rpkq“ FeatureC,rpkq´ ĞTextReadingrpkq
ĞTextReadingrpkq
(3)
where FeatureC,r(k) corresponds to a vector with the values of
the feature k from the code review task C in the run r, being
normalized by TextReadingrpkq, which is the average of the
feature k during resting state of text reading, from the same
run r.
Following feature normalization, second-order features were
computed from the normalized features for each segment of
those tasks. The second-order features computed were the
following: Maximum, minimum, mean, standard deviation,
quantiles, and peaks. This was performed in order to capture
and enhance the state of the subject for each sections for code
review quality assessment.
C. Regions Structure & Software Metrics
We divided the code under review into smaller code re-
gions to evaluate code review at a finer granularity of code
constructs. This structured approach reflects real-world code
review scenarios, where developers read and comprehend
code using small snippets related to language constructs. The
average size of the code regions defined in the programs used
in the experiment is 8.9 lines of code, with a maximum length
of 14 lines, meaning that the code regions are well-scaled for
readability and comprehension. The division of the programs
into regions (only for analysis purposes), was based on the
following:
1) Preserving Logical Code Blocks : Code regions are
defined as top-level constructs in a program, ensuring
that logical units of code remain intact and not split.
‚ Each function or method is considered an indepen-
dent code region if a program contains multiple
functions. If the program consists of a single func-
tion, the entire function is treated as a single unit.
‚ Inside a function or a single-unit program, loop con-
structs (e.g., for, while) act as natural separators.
Code before the loop, inside the loop, and after the
loop are considered distinct regions.
‚ If a block of variable declarations spans at least
two lines at the start of a function or a program,
it is treated as an independent region, separating
declarations from the core logic.
2) Maintaining Syntactic Consistency : To preserve the
readability and logical flow of code, regions are struc-
tured to align with syntactic constructs.
‚ Control structures such as loops (e.g., for, while)
and conditionals (e.g., if, else) must be fully con-

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON SOFTW ARE ENGINEERING 9
tained within a single region, ensuring that logical
statements remain intact.
‚ Functions and methods are never split across mul-
tiple regions; their body remains within a single
defined unit.
3) Avoiding Overlaps: Code regions are non-overlapping,
meaning that a single line of code cannot belong to
multiple regions.
In practice, the division of the code into regions was done
with a parser to construct an abstract syntax tree during
syntactic analysis and split it into sub-trees at the top-level
constructs of the language. Since code regions are consistent
syntactic blocks of code, it is possible to calculate static
complexity metrics for each region. These metrics, including
cyclomatic complexity (Vg), lines of code (LoC), CCSonar,
and Halstead Effort and Difficulty, are shown in Table 1 for
all the regions considered in the programs. The metrics are
then used to augment the feature space when classifying each
code region as ”well” or ”poorly” reviewed (as detailed in the
subsequent section IV-D).
D. Quantifying Code Review Quality and Targets Labeling
In this work, instead of comparing the accuracy of cog-
nitive load measured by the different biosensors assuming
that EEG or fRMI represent the ground truth (which could
be questionable), we evaluate all biosensors based on their
ability to predict code review quality in terms of bug detection
effectiveness. We trained a set of machine learning models on
the features described earlier to predict code review quality
at the code region levels. The model training followed the
supervised paradigm and is based on the existence of a
ground truth, which is the actual performance of the reviewers
(i.e., participants) obtained during the controlled experiment
sessions. Since we know the location of the injected bugs in
different code regions, we can quantify the ground truth at
each code region, i.e., our target labeling. The ground truth is
characterized by the number of found bugs (true positives), the
number of missed bugs (false negatives), and the number of
wrongly identified bugs (false positives). In other words, these
numbers can be represented by precision and recall. To achieve
harmony between precision and recall, we use a special version
of the f-measure, called f-beta, which indexes the essence of
the truly identified bugs, wrongly identified bugs, and missed
bugs. Equation 1 shows the f-beta formula:
Fβ“p 1`β2q¨ precision¨ recall
pβ2¨ precisionq` recall (4)
The selection of beta is a design choice. When false negatives
are more critical to avoid than false positives, we should
choose a β greater than 1. This will make the Fβ score more
sensitive to recall, thereby giving more importance to reducing
false negatives. In our technique design, we used β “ 2.
We propose that missed or wrongly identified bugs could
be attributed to difficulties in code comprehension, stress, or
distractions, which impact the quality of code reviews and
the reviewer’s engagement. Given that our problem is formed
as a binary classification (Bad Reviews and Good Reviews),
we experimented with various thresholds to binarize the Fβ
values as either 0 or 1. Through empirical testing and data
inspection, a threshold of 0.70 was found to be optimal.
This threshold effectively separated participants into good and
poor performers. Specifically, reviewers who scored above
0.70 on the Fβ metric demonstrated reasonably acceptable
performance, with acceptable rates of false negatives and false
positives. Thus, we consider a code region to be well
reviewed if Fβą 0.70 and poorly reviewed otherwise. It is
important to note that this threshold is not a fixed value and
it could be different depending on design specific needs and
business requirements of a tool or implementation. However,
for the purposes of our study, this threshold was used primarily
just as a reference point. The key focus remains on comparing
the different biosignals under the same conditions, rather than
optimizing the threshold itself.
E. Code Review Quality Evaluation: Optimal Machine Learn-
ing Model
To assess the code review quality using machine learn-
ing models, we systematically tested different cognitive load
modalities and their impact on the model’s performance.
Figure 3 illustrates the workflow of our approach, offering an
overview of how fixed inputs, such as programmer proficiency
and software metrics, are consistently included in the machine
learning model, while we vary cognitive load modalities (e.g.,
HRV , EEG, fMRI, Eye-Tracking, Pupillography) and evaluated
one at a time. The transformed and normalized features
from each modality undergo further feature selection and are
used as inputs to the classification model. This approach
enables a controlled comparison of the modalities’ impacts
on key overall performance metrics. Given that our dataset
was imbalanced, accuracy alone would not provide a reliable
measure of model performance. To address this, we used the
F1-score metric separately for each class (Bad Review and
Good Review regions). The F1-score for bad reviews tell us
how well the model identifies bad reviewed code regions,
considering both how many it finds (recall) and how accurate
those predictions are (precision). Similarly, the F1-score for
good reviews measures the model’s performance regarding
good review code regions. We also computed the macro F1-
score, as our overall performance metric for the grid search.
This approach prevents the model’s performance from being
biased toward the more frequent class and provides a fair
assessment of both classes. Finally, the macro F1-score was
also used as the primary metric for the comparison of the
modalities, and respective statistical analysis. This way, the
results highlight the practical and optimal setups suitable for
real-world software engineering applications.
For the code review quality assessment approach, we
performed a 70-fold Leave-3-Subjects-Out Cross-Validation
(L3SO-CV) strategy. This approach ensures that in each itera-
tion, three subjects are left out for testing while the remaining
data is used for training the machine learning model. With
70-fold L3SO-CV , it strikes a balance between robustness and
computational cost, and we ensure that each subject’s data
is used for both training or testing in different iterations,

Fig. 3. Workflow for evaluating code review quality models with fixed inputs
(programmer proficiency and software metrics) and varying cognitive load
modalities. The diagram highlights feature selection, machine learning, and
performance evaluation to determine optimal setups for practical applications.
allowing for a thorough evaluation of the model’s performance.
This extensive approach helps in capturing the variability of
data and randomization across different folds, providing a
more reliable assessment by mitigating the risk of overfitting
and ensuring that the model’s performance is evaluated under
diverse conditions.
Before training, we performed preprocessing steps to ad-
dress potential missing values and ensure consistency in the
dataset. Following this, during the training phase, a Z-score
normalization was applied to standardize the features. The nor-
malization parameters (mean and standard deviation) derived
from the training set were subsequently used to transform the
test set. This ensures that the test data is normalized in the
same way as the training data, maintaining consistency and
avoiding data leakage.
Within each training set of the 70-L3SO-CV , we further
performed a 30-fold Leave-2-Subjects-Out Cross-Validation
(L2SO-CV) for feature selection specifically for cognitive load
(CL) assessment [27]. For this purpose, a wrapper method
using a bag of trees ensemble with 5-fold cross-validation
was used. This feature selection process is performed for
each biosensor modality under evaluation for the code review
quality assessment model. From the initial set of cognitive
load features extracted from that modality, this method selects
a subset of the most relevant features that improve the perfor-
mance of the cognitive load classification model. This ensures
that only the most discriminant cognitive load biomarkers are
retained as cognitive load input for the final model, the code
review quality assessment classification model.
After the feature selection process, the top five most recur-
rent features across the 30-L2SO-CV were identified. These
features were then used as input biomarkers for the cognitive
load aspect. This ensures that only the most discriminant
cognitive load biomarkers are retained as cognitive load in-
put for the final model, the code review quality assessment
classification model.
Subsequently, the cognitive load biomarkers were also com-
bined with programmer proficiency and software complexity
metrics to provide a more complete evaluation of code review
quality (see Figure 3). In this approach, programmer profi-
ciency and software complexity metrics were kept constant,
while cognitive load modalities, as cognitive load biomarkers,
were varied to evaluate performance. This comparison allows
us to observe how different modalities perform, considering
their varying levels of intrusiveness, and to assess their prac-
ticality and potential use for real-world software engineering
setups.
For our code review quality evaluation model, various clas-
sifiers were tested, including Support Vector Machine (SVM)
with a linear kernel, k-Nearest Neighbors (KNN), Fisher’s
Linear Discriminant Analysis (FLDA), Naive Bayes, Decision
Tree and Ensemble Bagged Trees. The parameters for each
classifier were tuned using 10-fold cross-validation on the
training set. This step ensures optimal model performance by
identifying the best parameter settings for each classifier.
Finally, the best-trained models were tested on the indepen-
dent test dataset, and their performance was evaluated to obtain
the final metrics for statistical analysis. This evaluation process
focused on ensuring reliability and robustness in assessing
code review quality across the diverse subjects and conditions.
Additionally, statistical comparisons were conducted be-
tween each proposed optimal model and a random predictor
model, as well as among the optimal models themselves. The
random predictor, which does not incorporate biosensors or
other metrics, served as a baseline. Statistical significance
was assessed using a significance level of 1% (alpha = 0.01),
ensuring robust evaluation of performance differences.

This section evaluates the performance of the features from
the different biosensors (modalities). More precisely, we evalu-
ate the impact of features from the different modalities (fMRI,
EEG, HRV , and Pupilography (Pupil)), on the performance of

the machine learning models predicting the quality of code
reviews. Each model was also compared against a random
predictor to assess statistical significance.
Table 2 shows the performance of the different models and
the random predictor across different modalities (i.e., different
biosensors). As discussed previously in Section IV-E, we use
the F1-score of Bad Review (%), F1-score of Good Review
(%), and the Macro F1-score (%) to quantify the models’
predictive performance for both the Proposed Model and the
Random Predictor Model.
The findings suggest that modality-specific sensors within
the CNS, i.e., fMRI and EEG (macro F1-scores higher than
71,38%), perform slightly better compared to ANS-related
sensors (macro F1-scores from 69,90%), showing their value
as cognitive load biomarkers as one of the inputs integrated
with the proficiency and software metrics when classifying
code review quality. Nevertheless, it is important to refer to
how HRV and pupillography performed similarly to the CNS-
related sensors (presenting no significant statistical differences
in the performances, as shown in Figure 4), showing to be a
prominent and viable low-intrusive solution for potential de-
ployment in industry settings, particularly pupillography, given
nowadays low-cost and effective eye-tracking technology.
When combining both CNS and ANS sensors (All Mod.) to
select features as cognitive load input for the machine learn-
ing model, the trained models using all modalities revealed
an interesting pattern. When combining all modalities, the
models did not outperform all other individual CNS and ANS
modalities, except the HRV modality. This suggests that while
certain features, when individually, seem to be more sensitive
as cognitive load indicators, when combining all features for
model training, it shows a symptom of slight overfitting. As a
result, while there is a slight performance increase compared to
HRV , this increase is not as substantial. This can be observed
in Figure 4, where despite the slightly better performance
of some modalities, no significant statistical differences were
found between the performance of the different modalities in
this scenario.
Moreover, statistical analyses performed at a level of signif-
icance 1% reveal significant differences between the proposed
ML-based model and the random predictor across various
sensor modalities. This indicates that the proposed models
perform well in distinguishing between well-reviewed and
poorly-reviewed code sections, which is a strong sign of
their efficacy. These findings reinforce the potential of using
biosensors technologies to optimize and improve code review

It is also important to mention and consider the flexibility of
the code review process, depending on the specific application
and goals of the reviews. Different weightings can be given to
the detection of well-reviewed versus poorly-reviewed code
sections. For a conservative approach aimed at rigorously
spotting bad reviews or a more lenient approach focused on
identifying good reviews, the Fβ value can be adjusted. By
varying Fβ from our standard threshold of 0.7 (which places
more importance on detecting bad reviews) to other values,
the model can be tailored to prioritize different outcomes.
This flexibility allows fine-tuning the model according to the
specific needs of the software development process, whether
that means being more conservative in identifying potential
issues or more lenient in streamlining the review process.
Having assessed the impact of individual physiological
modalities, combined with the static software metrics and pro-
grammer proficiency inputs, on the Code Review Evaluation
Model, we also explored the integration of eye tracking as
a behavioral input in our Code Review Evaluation Model.
Specifically, we include the Number of Revisits and Fixa-
tions features from eye tracking as fixed components for our
machine learning model. These features will complement the
cognitive load metrics extracted from other modalities. By

B. Code Review Evaluation Model - Integrating Eye-tracker
as a behavior input
The need for Eye-tracking as a source of developers’ gaze
mapping is evident, i.e., as a localization source, as it plays
a key role in the entire analysis when it comes to spotting
where the reviewers are looking at. Eye-tracking technology
can also offer unique insights into the cognitive processes
and visual patterns of developers during code review tasks.
In this section, we go further by exploring the integration of
Eye-tracking data as a behavioral input in our Code Review
Evaluation Model. Specifically, we incorporate features such
as NRevisits and Fixations from eye tracking, on top of the
cognitive load features from other modalities. The objective
is to assess how Eye-Tracking could augment the machine
learning model’s ability to differentiate between well-reviewed
and poorly-reviewed code sections by considering features that
can be easy to extract and compute, NRevisits and Fixations,
in a software development environment. The idea is driven
by the fact that with the advancements in technology, it is
possible and feasible to record eye-tracker data using standard
webcams, making this approach both cost-effective and widely
accessible to integrate with other low-intrusive cognitive load
biomarkers.
The integration of eye tracking into our Code Review
Evaluation Model presented promising results. It improved
overall performance metrics compared to the model without
eye tracking (see Table III). Across the results, the integrated
model consistently demonstrated higher macro F1-scores, with
improvements in both bad and good reviews F1-scores, in-
dicating higher capability of our models on the assessment
of code review quality. The integration of eye-tracking as
a behavioral input, together with each modality, increased
all performances but particularly increased the performance
regarding identifying good reviews.
Moreover, while the initial performance of individual sen-
sors within the CNS slightly outperformed those within the
ANS in the proposed models, the addition of eye tracking
led to a notable enhancement in the performance of ANS
sensors, particularly in HRV . As shown in Figure 5, there
are significant statistical differences in the macro F1-score
between the previous models that used only HRV features
as cognitive load input and the models that combined HRV
features with Eye-tracking data.
The fact that the F1-score metric for both good and bad code
reviews improved noticeably, suggests the complementary
nature of eye tracking in refining the evaluation of these low-
intrusive physiological metrics. This finding is crucial as it
highlights the potential to achieve comparable results to more
intrusive CNS sensors using non-intrusive wearable devices
suitable for software development environments.

When compared with the random predictor, all the inte-
grated models consistently outperformed across all metrics
(macro F1-score, F1-score for Bad Reviews, and F1-score
for Good Reviews). Statistical analysis using a significance
level of 1% (alpha = 0.01) confirmed significant differences,
highlighting the effectiveness of our approach in enhancing
predictive accuracy compared to random chance.
Eye tracking emerges as a robust and valuable behavioral
input for assessing code review quality. When combined with
physiological sensors such as HRV and pupil data — which
can be captured using low-intrusive devices compatible and
suitable for software development environments — this ap-
proach offers a practical and non-intrusive solution. This inte-
gration not only enhances the accuracy of assessments but also
improves usability by using potential wearable technology. By
combining eye tracking with other physiological signals, the
overall performance of the Code Review Evaluation Model is
significantly improved. This approach highlights the potential
of multimodal methods in refining code review evaluations,

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON SOFTW ARE ENGINEERING 13
suggesting promising directions of adoption of low intrusive
devices for future research and practical implementations in
software engineering.
C. Eye-tracking as Biomarker and Behavior Source in Code
Review Evaluation Models
Eye-tracking technology can offer unique advantages in
the software development environment by providing real-time
insights into a reviewer’s cognitive processes and respective
code review locations at a very fine level of granularity. It
can capture gaze patterns, fixation durations, and pupil size
variability, which can also be indicative of attentional shifts,
information processing, and cognitive load. Therefore, Eye-
tracking can be a significant candidate for improving code
review assessments’ overall accuracy and efficiency of code
review quality assessments.
When we explored Eye-tracking as a primary indicator of
cognitive load and as a behavioral input in the designed model,
combined with software metrics and programmer expertise, we
obtained significant results (see Table II).
The model achieved a macro F1-score of 75.65%, an F1-
score of 84.53% for bad reviews, and 66.78% for good
reviews. These results are higher than other approaches, with
statistical differences observed compared to models based
on low-intrusive HRV data. In contrast, despite presenting
higher performance, there were no statistical differences when
compared to the highest intrusiveness modalities like fMRI and
EEG and the low intrusive pupillography modality (see Figure
6). Additionally, all trained models significantly outperformed
the random predictor at a 1% significance level.

Eye-Tracking shows potential to be a valuable tool in
code review evaluations, as a cognitive load and behavioral
information source. This dual role makes it especially effective
for understanding developers’ focus and attention during code
comprehension and review tasks. However, relying solely
on Eye-tracking can have limitations in scenarios where a
reviewer looks away from the screen or moves outside the
tracking range. In such cases, combining Eye-tracking with
an additional biomarker like HRV from a smartwatch ensures
continuous monitoring. That way, HRV can provide physio-
logical insights even when Eye-Tracking data is momentarily
unavailable, while in other cases, Eye-tracking can remain
effective when HRV might fail due to device issues or signal
noise. This combination ensures that monitoring remains ef-
fective even when one input encounters limitations, creating a
robust, cost-effective practical solution for real-world software
engineering applications.

metrics for Eye-Tracking, such as accuracy, recall, and pre-
cision, complementing the F1-score analysis presented earlier.
While the F1-score remains the primary metric for evaluating
and comparing the Code Review Model’s performance across
modalities, these additional metrics are provided here just to
offer a more comprehensive view of the model’s evaluation.

D. Impact of Software Complexity Metrics on the Performance
of Code Review Evaluation Models
Although the core of this research focuses on the intrusive-
ness level of biosensors and their performance as cognitive
load biomarkers for assessing code review quality, we also
conducted an additional analysis to explore the contribution of
different software complexity metrics to code review quality
assessment when combined with biosensor data. Previously, all
our evaluations consistently used the complete set of software
metrics (LoC, Vg, SonarCC, Halstead) to derive the feature
that represents the complexity of the code under review, while
varying the features obtained from the different biosensors.
In this section, we investigate the complementary role of
traditional software metrics within the code review evaluation
model while keeping the biosensor features and programmers’
proficiency levels fixed.
The results in Table VI illustrate the distinct performance
trends across the different metric combinations. With respect
to our machine learning model (cognitive load and behavior
indicators, and programmer proficiency), when we included
individually the software metrics, like Vg and CC Sonar, the
model presented a macro F1-score around 64-67%. However,
when considering the combination of the software metrics,
such as VgSonarCC (McCabe’s Cyclomatic Complexity Vg
combined with SonnarQube), VgSonarCCHalstead (McCabe’s
Cyclomatic Complexity Vg combined with CC Sonar and Hal-
stead metrics), it significantly enhances the model’s capability
to identify well-reviewed and poorly-reviewed code segments,
as evidenced by higher macro F1-score and higher F1-scores
