E. Review Quality Results
To better understand the actual bugs detected by the re-
viewers in the code regions classified as bad or good reviews
by the models, we computed four key metrics based on
the True Positive Bugs (TP), False Positive Bugs (FP), and
False Negative Bugs (FN) across all code regions. It is worth
noting that, in this case, we used only low-intrusive biosensors
suitable for real-world settings. Specifically, we considered eye
tracking (pupillography and code reading behavior) and HRV ,
which can be measured using bracelets or smartwatches.
The first metric is the Bug Detection Rate (Recall) , which
quantifies the proportion of actual bugs correctly identified
within the predicted review type (e.g., across bad or good
review sections), i.e.,
Bug Detection Rate (Recall) “ TP
TP` FN.
The second metric is the Positive Predictive Value (Preci-
sion), representing the proportion of correctly identified bugs
out of all detected bugs, highlighting the trustworthiness of
bug detections, i.e.,
Positive Predictive Value (Precision)“ TP
TP` FP.
The third metric is the Missed Bug Rate , measuring the
proportion of actual bugs in a section that were not detected
within the predicted review type, i.e.,
Missed Bug Rate “ FN
TP` FN.
Finally, the False Alarm Rate was added to capture the
proportion of bugs reported incorrectly in relation to all bugs
detected within the predicted review type, i.e.,
False Alarm Rate “ FP
TP` FP.
These metrics provide a detailed view of the bug detection
performance for sections classified as either bad or good re-
views, helping to assess how well the system aligns with actual
bug occurrences within those sections. Table VI summarizes
these results.
TABLE VI

REVIEW EVALUATION MODE - P REDICTED REVIEW OUTCOMES IN

Rate Rate Rate Rate
Bad Rev. 18.77% 38.54% 81.23% 61.46%
Good Rev. 59.63% 63.37% 40,37% 36.63%
Table VI highlights the significant difference in actual bug
detection performance between the predicted good and bad
reviews. Sections predicted as bad reviews show notably
weaker performance in actual bug detection by reviewers.
For example, the missed bug rate is 81.23%, indicating that
the majority of bugs are overlooked in these reviews. This
means that the prediction that the code review must be
repeated is clearly justified . Additionally, the false alarm
rate for bad reviews is alarmingly high at 61.46%, reflecting
a significant tendency to incorrectly flag non-buggy sections
as problematic.
In contrast, When the model predicts a section as a good
review, it shows an average bug detection rate of 59.63% and
a precision of 63.37%. This indicates that when the models
predict reviews as good, the actual average bug detection by

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON SOFTW ARE ENGINEERING 15
reviewers is high (although, not perfect), while the likelihood
of false positives remains low. Additionally, the missed bug
rate for good reviews is relatively low at 40.37%, and the
false alarm rate is also low at 36.63%. These results confirm
that the prediction of good reviews is reasonably reliable.
It is worth noting that these values depend on the chosen
threshold of 0.70 (see Section IV-D) to distinguish good
reviews from bad reviews code regions. For example, setting
a higher threshold (e.g., 0.8 or 0.9) would likely amplify the
performance differences between the two classes in terms of
predicted review outcomes and bug detection effectiveness.
These results indicate that low-intrusive biosensors can be
a viable and effective solution for assessing the effectiveness
of complex software development tasks, such as code reviews,
supporting their practical integration into real-world software
development environments.
F . Results summary and implications for Software Engineer-
ing
This paper presents a controlled experiment to evaluate the
predictive power of features derived from data collected by a
comprehensive set of biosensors. Code reviews were selected
as the benchmarking task for comparing the biosensors, as this
task requires a combination of complex cognitive operations,
such as code reading and comprehension, bug finding, and
decision-making (on whether a given construct has a bug
or not). The range of biosensors used in the study includes
highly accurate but intrusive biosensors, such as fMRI and
EEG, which collect direct information from the central nervous
system, as well as nearly non-intrusive sensors, such as HRV
and pupillography, which use signals driven by the autonomic
nervous system. Additionally, we considered code review
behavioral information captured by eye tracking.
The following points summarize the most relevant observa-
tions:
‚ Contrary to expectations grounded in extensive literature
(see, for example, the following 2021 survey [7]), sophis-
ticated and highly intrusive biosensors, such as fMRI and
EEG, do not show significantly higher predictive power
than the simpler and nearly non-intrusive sensors. Our
interpretation of this surprising result is that fMRI and
EEG primarily detect the activation of different brain
regions during code review tasks, but this information
does not necessarily provide the most relevant features
for predicting code review quality.
‚ Simple, nearly non-intrusive sensors, such as HRV and
pupillography, achieved similar performance in predicting
code review quality, making them promising and viable
options for future use in real-world scenarios. HRV can be
captured by non-intrusive bracelets or smartwatches (in
the form of pulse rate variability), while pupillography
can be obtained using simple desktop eye trackers.
‚ Code review behavior (e.g., fixations, number of revis-
its to specific code regions) captured by eye tracking
demonstrates excellent predictive power for code review
quality. Additionally, our results suggest that eye-tracking
features may be less sensitive to intra and inter-subject
variability than features from other biosensors. There is
an extensive literature on the use of eye tracking to
enhance various tasks in software development [76], [77],
but to the best of our knowledge, our results are the first
to effectively benchmark eye-tracking behavior features
against a comprehensive set of biosensors (fMRI, EEG,
HRV , and pupillography).
‚ The ensemble of simple and low-intrusive biosensors
(HRV , pupillography, and eye tracking) provides signifi-
cant results in predicting code review quality. Our results
show that when the models predict a given code review as
bad, the reviewer missed more than 81% of the bugs in
the code, justifying the need for a repeated review, either
by the same reviewer or another one.
‚ The complementarity of low-intrusive sensors is crucial,
particularly in combining modalities such as HRV or
pupillography with eye tracking to analyze code reading
and review behavior.
‚ Since the AI models used to process biosensor features
also consider static code complexity metrics, our results
demonstrate that a single code complexity metric is
insufficient. The best results were achieved when control
flow metrics, such as Vg or cognitive complexity from
SonarQube, were used in conjunction with Halstead data
complexity metrics.
‚ Training AI models with small granularity code regions
(see Section IV-C for details, where the average size
is 9.8 lines of code) to focus on predicting bug detec-
tion effectiveness for each region seems essential for
achieving good results, regardless of the biosensor type.
Additionally, this approach allows for pinpointing the
exact code areas that need a second, more careful review.
In summary, our results show that the combination of nearly
non-intrusive and simple biosensors (e.g., HRV + eye tracking)
has promising potential for real-world software development
scenarios. HRV is already available in bracelets and smart-
watches, and even the eye tracker can be replaced by a
laptop camera, as the resolution required to distinguish gazes
on contiguous lines of code is relatively low. For example,
manufacturers like Tobii are now marketing webcam-based
eye-tracking software as an alternative to desktop eye trackers.
Although the use of code reviewing as the target task
to benchmark all biosensors represents a specific utilization
scenario, the fact that code reviewing is representative of
most tasks related to code development suggests that simple
biosensors can be effectively used to enhance other coding
activities. Monitoring developers’ cognitive load (particularly
cognitive overload), distractions, and fatigue in real time is not
only valuable for ensuring code quality but also for protecting
developers from serious burnout, a major threat in the software
industry.
The intrusiveness of simple sensors has important impli-
cations that must be considered for the widespread adoption
of biosensors in software development. Although wearable
biosensors, such as simple bracelets or smartwatches for col-
lecting PRV data and a desktop eye tracker/cameras may not be
physically intrusive, they can still raise privacy concerns and
create discomfort, particularly the feeling of being watched.

More importantly, any collected data should be anonymized
and strictly used to improve processes and protect developers’
health rather than to evaluate individual employees. By han-
dling this responsibly, organizations can foster an environment
where these tools are perceived as supportive instruments
for enhancing software quality and reliability, rather than as
surveillance mechanisms.
Although concerns about intrusiveness and privacy are valid
and real, perceptions regarding the use of wearable devices,
such as smartwatches, and cameras are evolving. For example,
biometric authentication via smartphone cameras, once a major
privacy concern, is now widely accepted. Naturally, these
broader technological trends have important implications for
the practical adoption of such biosensing technologies in the
software industry.
Table 7 summarizes the potential adoption perspectives
of biosensor technology in the software industry. In short,
the results of this study show that low-intrusive biosensors
provide a practical and effective means to support and enhance
software development tasks, as demonstrated by their ability
to accurately predict bad code reviews in more than 80% of
cases.
VI. T HREATS TO VALIDITY
Although the study provides valuable insights, there are still
limitations that should be discussed as the main threats to the
validity of the presented study.
First of all, given that the data of this study was acquired
in a very controlled environment and inside an MRI scanner,
we will always face limitations in terms of the made-up
setup and the simulation of a natural software development
environment. The very high complexity of the experiments
on studies designed like the presented one is an inherent
limitation. As much as the participants are informed about
all the procedures and task conditions to be performed, in
order to keep them calm and comfortable during the ex-
periment, it is impossible to be close to a real software
environment scenario. However, our study is the first one with
such comprehensive set of simultaneous sophisticated biosen-
sors within the field of software engineering research. This
experimental design incorporates extensive, high-cost, and
high complex data collection methods, capturing physiological
patterns from multiple high-density biosensors, complemented
by programmers’ proficiency and traditional static software
metrics. Furthermore, given the limited number of studies
in this emerging area of software engineering, our research
presents a unique approach to assessing code review quality.
This study places the human element as the key and central
element of the software development process by combining
physiological patterns captured by high-density biosensors,
along with programmers’ proficiency, with the traditional static
software metrics. This comprehensive approach allows for a
more dynamic evaluation of code review quality, bridging
the gap between human cognitive load shift and software
engineering practices.
Regarding the code snippets used in the controlled exper-
iment, although being carefully chosen during the design of
this experiment, in order to represent different characteristics
concerning complexity (simple/complex) and algorithm type
(recursive/iterative), we are aware that the code snippets could
be larger and with more software bugs, being more closer to
real-world software. Nevertheless, for practical reasons, we
could not use extensive programs as the participants would
require a considerable amount of time to inspect the codes and
detect the software bugs, which would make the experiments
unfeasible — particularly with participants lying inside the
MRI scanner for extended periods.
Another limitation concerns the number of participants
in the study. Acknowledging the inherent complexities of
conducting such multimodal studies is essential. These com-
plexities arise due to the inherently complex nature of studies
involving multiple data modalities, the substantial cost of
acquiring such signals, and the challenge of attracting and re-
cruiting volunteers to participate in the experiment (especially
when participants need to lie down inside an MRI scanner).
In the context of our work, the current database, although

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON SOFTW ARE ENGINEERING 17
limited, is the only one with this type of multimodal data
and guaranteed results with statistical significance. Compared
to similar studies published in this area, as presented in the
systematic literature review from Weber et al. [7], the number
of participants in this paper is similar to the median number
of subjects (17) of the other studies reviewed.
Additionally, despite our effort to gather a balanced group of
participants during the screening of participants, unfortunately,
the percentage of female software developers (among students,
researchers and the software industry) is relatively small when
compared to the male percentage, and the group of participants
resulted in not being evenly balanced in gender. Consequently,
this may result in a lack of representative gender diversity in
our findings. Nevertheless, it should be noted that while our
study is based on male participants, this gender homogeneity
does have a positive aspect as it reduces the variability
introduced by gender-related factors. In future work, with
larger datasets, further efforts should be made to guarantee a
more balanced and diverse participant pool, leading to a more
representative analysis and, therefore, findings.
VII. C ONCLUSION
This study presents the results of benchmarking a com-
prehensive range of biosensors from sophisticated tools such
as fMRI and EEG to simple and low-intrusive biosensors
such as HRV , pupillography, and code reading/reviewing be-
havior captured by desktop eye trackers. The high com-
plexity of the controlled experiment designed for this study,
resulting from the simultaneous use of a large number
of complex biosensors, adds unique value to the result-
ing dataset. This dataset is publicly available to ensure
transparency, reproducibility and to encourage further anal-
yses (https://github.com/Julio-CMedeiros/TSE-Benchmarking-
the-accuracy-of-biosensors-bug-finding-effectiveness).
The main objective is to investigate whether low-intrusive
biosensors, compatible with existing software development
setups, can accurately assess programmers’ cognitive load
and other cognitive states. We selected code review as the
benchmark task due to the high complexity of its cognitive
processes, including code comprehension, bug finding, and
decision-making, ensuring that it effectively represents the
diverse cognitive demands of the variety of software de-
velopment tasks. The idea is to use features derived from
signals provided by various biosensors to train a set of AI
models to predict code review quality in terms of bug-finding
effectiveness, allowing a robust comparison of biosensors in
achieving a concrete and very relevant goal of predicting
the quality of code reviews. Given the controlled experiment
conditions, we have prior knowledge of the bugs present in
each code under review, allowing us to assess the reliability
of predictions generated by the different biosensors when
compared with the actual results obtained by the reviewers
who participated in the experiment.
Our results surprisingly show that sophisticated high-
intrusive biosensors such as fMRI and EEG do not show
significantly higher predictive performance in bug-finding
effectiveness when compared to simple and non-intrusive
biosensors. While fMRI and EEG are powerful tools for
understanding the brain mechanisms involved in complex
cognitive tasks such as code review, they do not necessarily
provide the most effective features for predicting bug-finding
performance.
Low-intrusive solutions such as eye tracking, pupillography,
and HRV proved to be viable alternatives to complex and
intrusive biosensors, showing strong potential for real-world
software development applications. For example, these low-
intrusive biosensors reliably detect over 85% of code reviews
as bad and those reviews need to be repeated due to missed
bugs (at least 81%) or are poor-quality reviews because of
their high number of false positive bugs (at least 61%).
A key takeaway is the need of using multiple non-intrusive
biosensors to achieve optimal results, as their features show a
high complementarity. Additionally, the software complexity
metrics used in the prediction models (in conjunction with the
features from the biosensors) also show high complementarity.
Our results show that both control for metrics and data com-
plexity metrics are necessary to provide an accurate assessment
of the bug detection effectiveness of code under review.
Overall, our findings demonstrate that low and non-intrusive
biosensors are highly effective in assessing developers’ cog-
nitive states and predicting the success rate of cognitively
complex tasks such as code reviews, confirming their potential
for future applicability in real-world software development
settings.

VIII. B IOGRAPHY SECTION
J´ulio Medeiros is currently a PhD candidate at the
Center for Informatics and Systems of the University
of Coimbra (CISUC). His research interests include
signal processing, biofeedback, and machine learn-
ing applied to understanding and mitigating human
error in software development tasks. Alongside his
academic work, he currently works as a data scien-
tist in the industry, building AI-driven solutions to
enhance workflows and optimize systems.
Haytham Hijazi received his Ph.D. in Informatics
Engineering (Intelligent Systems) from the Univer-
sity of Coimbra, Portugal, where he is currently an
integrated researcher at the Center for Informatics
and Systems of the University of Coimbra (CISUC).
His research interests include artificial intelligence,
biofeedback, and software engineering, with a focus
on intelligent biofeedback systems for augmenting
content comprehension. In addition to his research,
Dr. Hijazi serves as the Dean of the Faculty of En-
gineering and Information Technology at Palestine
Ahliya University, where he plays a key role in academic leadership and
advancing engineering education.

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON SOFTW ARE ENGINEERING 20
Jo˜ao Duraes is a professor at the ISEC/IPC where
he is responsible for several software engineering-
related courses. He received his PhD in Informatics
Engineering from the University of Coimbra. He has
a long-standing involvement with the Software and
Systems Engineering research group of the Centre
for Informatics and Systems of the University of
Coimbra. His research interests are mainly focused
on software reliability, and his contributions and
published works include novel techniques and mod-
els concerning software fault injection, dependability
benchmarking and security evaluation, and participated on several national and
European research projects in this area.
Ricardo Couceiro has been a research member
of the Center for Informatics and Systems, Uni-
versity of Coimbra since 2006. His research inter-
ests include biomedical signal processing, pattern
recognition, and modeling applied to the analysis of
cardiovascular systems.
Jo˜ao Castelhano is a biomedical engineer with
a PhD in Health Sciences and a researcher at
CIBIT/ICNAS, University of Coimbra. His expertise
lies in multimodal medical imaging data acquisition
and analysis, encompassing EEG, fMRI, MRS, PET,
TMS, ECoG, and fNIRS. His research focuses on
experimental design and the integration of imag-
ing modalities to develop advanced diagnostic and
therapeutic tools, aiming to enhance patient care
and quality of life. He specializes in multimodal
medical and molecular imaging, bridging preclinical
and clinical applications.
C´esar Teixeira is currently an Associate Professor
with Habilitation at the Departments of Informatics
Engineering, Faculty of Sciences and Technology,
University of Coimbra, Portugal. His expertise is on
bio-signal processing, classification, and modeling.
More precisely on EEG/ECG-based epileptic seizure
prediction and ultrasound imaging. From 2009 to
2012, he took a scientific leadership role in the
European project EPILEPSIAE, which have as main
objective the development of a transportable device
for epileptic seizure prediction. He was the Principal
Investigator of portuguese national projects, and has participated in several
other national and international projects. His publication record includes over
150 papers in refereed national and international journals and conferences. He
served as the Vice Director for CISUC. He is an Associate Editor of Research
on Biomedical Engineering.
Henrique Madeira is currently a full professor
with the University of Coimbra, where he has been
involved in research on dependable computing since
1989. He has coordinated or participated in dozens
of research projects funded by the Portuguese Gov-
ernment and by the European Union. His current
funded projects are in the areas of software qual-
ity and software reliability, verification and valida-
tion, human factors in software engineering, and
safety and security evaluation artificial intelligence
in safety-critical functions.
Paulo de Carvalho is a full professor in Informatics
Engineering with the University of Coimbra. His
main research interests include intelligent algorithms
for personal health solutions and clinical decision
support systems. He has authored or coauthored
more than 300 papers in refereed international jour-
nals and conferences. He has been involved in
several organizations and program committees of
international conferences in the digital health and
he is an editorial board member in several scientific
journals. He is the director of the Digital Health
Division of the IFMBE.

Chapter 8
Conclusion
T
his chapter summarizes the main findings and contributions of this the-
sis, emphasizing the multidisciplinary aspect of this work that integrates
software engineering and neurophysiology. It highlights the key outcomes,
the added value of this research, and reflection on the directions for future work.
8.1 Summary of the Main Contributions
This thesis addresses a critical gap in software engineering by introducing an ap-
proach to assess programmers’ cognitive load and improve the quality of software
development processes. By exploring both CNS and ANS perspectives, this research
explored different perspectives and methodologies to offer insights into cognitive load
assessment, and consequently about software quality.
TheworkstartedwithafocusontheCNS,particularlyusingEEG,toexplorethe
neural patterns underlying cognitive load during software development tasks such as
code comprehension and code inspection. The studies showed that EEG frequency
bands are reliable biomarkers of cognitive load, providing real-time, high-temporal-
resolution insights into programmers’ cognitive load, especially when combined with
eye-tracking as a map gaze behavior. Importantly, the research also revealed that
software complexity metrics alone do not fully align with programmers’ perceived
complexity, emphasizing the limitations of traditional approaches. Additionally,
by exploring EEG as a less intrusive and cost-effective alternative to fMRI, this
thesis proposed a reliable ground truth for assessing cognitive load to be used as
a neuroscience reference for software engineering applications. EEG proves to be
a practical solution for evaluating the accuracy of wearable ANS-driven sensors,
offering insights in future validation studies while reducing experimental complexity
and costs.
Following previous insights, the research expanded to ANS measurements, which
included HRV and Eye-tracking with Pupillography data. First, the HRV study
163

164 CHAPTER 8. CONCLUSION
explored the reliability of ultra-short-term HRV features in assessing cognitive load
during software tasks. The work provided recommendations on the smallest time
frame considered to have reliable HRV measurements for cognitive load assessment
scenarios, offering valuable insights for non-intrusive cognitive load monitoring in
software development environments.
In parallel, the pupillography study addressed the existing gaps in pupillogra-
phy frequency domain analysis, where the lack of clear and consensual definitions
for frequency band limits poses challenges for its reliable use and exploration in
software engineering. The work proposed new optimized frequency band limits and
highlighted the use of eye-tracker with pupillography as a potential and practical
sensor for real-world environments, offering an effective solution for cognitive load
assessment while simultaneously mapping gaze behavior.
The final result of this work was the systematic and comprehensive framework
thatevaluatedtheperformanceofbiosensorsinassessingcodereviewquality, provid-
ing insights into which reliable setups are viable for real-world software engineering
tasks. While EEG and fMRI provide precise cognitive load indicators and achieve
good performance in the code review quality prediction model, their cost and com-
plexity make them impractical for widespread industry deployment. In contrast,
HRV and pupillography, particularly when combined with eye-tracking to capture
behavioral gaze metrics, emerged as practical, and low-cost alternatives. This work
not only demonstrates that low-intrusiveness solutions, enhanced with behavioral
insights, can bridge the gap between precision and practicality but also supports the
adoption of such tools in software engineering to optimize bug detection, enhance
software quality, reduce human error, and improve task prioritization, impacting the
effectiveness in development workflows.
8.2 Added Value of Contributions and Future Direc-
tions
The research presented in this thesis contributes to the software engineering field by
bridging the gap between human cognitive load and software quality. By incorporat-
ing neurophysiological measurements into traditional software engineering practices,
this work highlights the importance of understanding and measuring the developers’
cognitive patterns to improve the quality of software development tasks.
One of the core innovations is the development of a multimodal methodology
that compares CNS and ANS signals. This approach is unique in software engi-
neering research by having a complex set of sensors and comparing the different
performances of these different modalities in the prediction of code review quality.
This approach provides a rich and complex methodology by combining cognitive load
indicators, behavioral patterns, programmer profiles and software metrics to assess

8.2. ADDED V ALUE OF CONTRIBUTIONS AND FUTURE DIRECTIONS 165
the quality of code review processes. Unlike traditional software practices relying
on software quality metrics, this work emphasizes the human factor as a critical
component of software quality. Furthermore, it suggests what can be a viable setup
for software development environments. The results open opportunities for person-
alized developer-centric tools that enhance software quality and open the doors for
other applications goals, such as optimizing task allocation, training programs, and
workload management.
The findings also emphasize the viability of real-time feedback systems that could
dynamically monitor developers’ cognitive load. For example, by identifying cog-
nitive bottlenecks, these systems could enable adaptive environments that support
developers during complex tasks, i.e., tools could suggest the programmers take
breaks during periods of high strain, adjust task priorities, or guide reviews to areas
requiring a second look. Such interventions would not only enhance productivity,
but also promote healthier work practices.
Given the promising insights, there is a clear path for future work from this work.
First, validating the proposed methodologies in larger and more diverse datasets
is essential for generalizability. Furthermore, in such studies, including different
programming languages, development tasks, and diverse developer profiles would
strengthen the robustness of the findings. After practical validations, a promising
direction would be the development and implementation of a low-cost and effective
product comprising the machine learning tool together with the recommended non-
intrusive biosensors based on the company’s needs. These practical solutions could
rely on a set of a wearable smartwatch and eye-tracker integrated into monitors.
The tool could be implemented into real-world software development workflows,
presenting minimal interference.
Another relevant future direction work is related to the rise of AI-assisted tools.
With the increase of software programmers relying on AI tools to perform their
daily work, there will be a clear shift on the cognitive demands from writing code
to validating, debugging and tuning the code obtained from AI tools. Future work
should explore the paradigm of AI-assisted tools and how biofeedback approaches
could support software developers, ensuring that AI-assisted programming improves
productivity without compromising software quality.
On another front of research, the integration of this framework with new cogni-
tive load-based software metrics represents a promising direction for future work. By
combining traditional complexity metrics with real-time cognitive load monitoring,
these new metrics could better reflect developers’ actual experiences or refine exist-
ing software metrics to align more closely with human perceptions of complexity and
understandability, improving tools for code understandability, review prioritization,
and software testing guidance. As a result of this thesis, further research is already
in progress, specifically focusing on neuroscience-based guidelines for improving soft-
ware complexity metrics.

166 CHAPTER 8. CONCLUSION
Finally, the research presented in this thesis can also support potential applica-
tionsinotherareas, suchasthefieldofprogrammingeducationandtrainingsettings.
For example, recently Thorgeirsson et al. [149] published a study on the use of EEG
features for cognitive load monitoring to support learners in programming courses,
suggesting that insights from such biosignals may help inform how mental effort
could be optimized and support improved learning outcomes. Future work could
build upon this approach to design adaptive educational applications that provide
dynamic guidance for learners, reducing the cognitive overload and speeding up skill
acquisition for progressive, self-paced learning in the future.

Appendix A
Literature Review Summary
This appendix presents a comprehensive summary of the studies reviewed in Chapter
3 - Literature Review.
Table A.1 provides an overview of key studies exploring the neural mechanisms
underlying software development tasks and the cognitive load in software engineer-
ing contexts. It includes details about the year and authors, tasks and number of
participants, sensors and techniques used, extracted features, methods of analysis,
and reported results, findings, and limitations. This structured summary provides
a quick overview of the current trends, methodologies, and challenges in the field.

Experimental Protocol Study 0
ThedetailedprotocolfortheexperimentalStudy0isprovidedbelow. Thisdocument
outlines the procedures, data collection methods, and participant criteria used in the
experiment.

BASE: Protocol Definition - Study 0 3  
1. Overview 
The experiment included 30 participants and all the subjects had experience in 
the Java programming language. There was a screening process that included an 
interview to assign a programming level skill in Java to each participant as ”Inter- 
mediate”, ”Advanced” or ”Expert”. 
The experimental set up covered a comprehensive set of sensors including EEG 
with a 64-channel cap, ECG, Electrodermal activity (EDA), Impedance cardiogra- 
phy (ICG), Photoplethysmogram (PPG) and eye tracking with pupillography. The 
signals from all these sources were synchronized in a common time base to allow 
consistent cross analysis. 
Experiments involved the compression of 3 small Java programs that had 
different complexities according to software complexity metrics like lines of code 
and cyclo- matic complexity. The programs were carefully designed to keep 
consistency in the programming style and to avoid that math or algorithm pose 
extra difficulties to the participants, not directly related to the code complexity. All 
the participants performed the experiments in the same room, without distractions, 
noise or presence of people unrelated to the experiments. 
The study was approved by the Ethics Committee of the Faculty of Medicine of 
the University of Coimbra, in accordance with the Declaration of Helsinki and all 
experiments were performed in accordance with relevant guidelines and 
regulations. Informed consent (Appendix A) was signed by all participants. 
1.1 Volunteers screening and recruiting 
1.2 Announcement 
Experiments were mainly promoted through e-mail and face to face talks with 
stu- dents and other potential participants including teachers and professional 
software developers. During this phase, potential participants were made aware of 
the overall goal of the experiment: “Assess whether different levels of cognitive 
stress induced during software inspection/reading can be captured and quantified 
using autonomic nervous system activity manifestations captured using wearable 
sensors”, the re- ward associated with the participation (vouchers to use in SONAE 
stores) and the start date of the experiments: May 2018. Due to a delay in the 
vouchers’ purchase process the experiments were delayed to June. 
1.3 Screening interview 
People who show interest in participating in the research were interviewed. 
Through- out this interview subjects were asked some questions regarding 
demographics, con- tacts, profession, programming experience and availability. 
Interviewers did also take notes related to the overall motivation of the person, 
regarding the experi- ment, that could impact the results of the research. All 
information regarding the participants has been managed according to the new 
legislation of data protection, GDPR. 
During the interview information regarding the location: “IBILI P´olo3 – lab 
58” and estimated duration: “2 hours 30 minutes” was disclosed. It was also 
important that the subject was aware that the experiments involved product 
application on hair for the EEG set up and that they are were not eligible for the 
experiment if they had any kind of implanted cardio device. 
Subjects didn’t need any type of preparation before the experiment. 

BASE: Protocol Definition - Study 0 4  
1.4 Screening 
The goal was to have around 30 subjects, balanced in gender and proficiency in 
Java programming. To distinguish the proficiency of the candidates, the following 
criteria was applied: 
• Expert Professional software developers that worked in the field for 
more than 3 year or had more than 15000 lines of Java language 
programmed in the last 3 months or more than 10000 Java lines written 
in their biggest program or teachers that had more than 20000 lines of 
Java language programmed in the last 3 months or more than 15000 Java 
lines written in their biggest program; 
• Advanced Professional software developers that worked in the field for 
more than 1 year or had more than 10000 lines of Java language 
programmed in the last 3 months or more than 5000 Java lines written in 
their biggest program or students and teachers that had more than 10000 
lines of Java language programmed in the last 3 months or more than 
5000 Java lines written in their biggest program; 
• Intermediate Students or teachers that had more than 5000 lines (and less 
than 10000 lines) of Java language programmed in the last 3 months or 
more than 3000 Java lines (and less than 5000 lines) written in their 
biggest program; 
Subjects that did not fulfil these requirements or had a history of heart diseases, 
mental health issues or implanted cardio devices were not eligible for the experi- 
ments. 
1.5 Recruitment 
From the 47 interviewed candidates 30 participants were selected to participate 
in the experiments. Although gender and proficiency balance was established as an 
primary objective due to the insufficient number of ”expert” and female candidates 
it wasn’t possible to achieve it.  The group of participants included 24 men and 
6 women. Regarding the proficiency in Java programming 13 participants were 
classified as ”intermediate”, 12 as ”advanced” and 5 as ”expert” according to the 
responses given during the screening interview. 
2. Experimental set up 
In this experiment four different data recording systems were used to record 
different signals: 
• Neuroscan’s equipment composed by a 64-channel cap that connects to 
an amplifier headbox that is attached to a system unit that communicates 
through USB with a computer that controls the equipment with 
Neuroscan’s Scan 4.4 software. This system records EEG and  ECG 
signals at a 1000 Hz sample frequency; 
• BiosignalsPlux research kit with a Wireless 4-channel hub with a variety 
of sensors that is controlled through bluetooth with Opensignals. ECG 
and EDA sensors were connected to the hub to record these signals at 500 
Hz sample frequency; 
• SMI eye tracker equipment that was positioned under the monitor that 
dis- played the stimulus and allowed to collect pupillography and eye 
movement tracking at 500 Hz and was monitored by the Iviewx software 
through USB; 

BASE: Protocol Definition - Study 0 5  
• Sensatron equipment developed by Philips that was used to measure 
ECG at 250 Hz, ICG at 125 Hz, PPG at 62.5 Hz. This equipment has a 
memory card and a button that starts and ends the recording therefore no 
software was used to control the acquisition of these signals. 
The sensors positioning of the different data recording equipment is displayed in 
Figures 4.1, 4.2, 4.3 and 4.4. In figure 4.4 the 64-channel cap used to record EEG 
is placed on a participant. This process requires special preparation in which the 
scalp of the participant is cleaned with abrasive gel and alcohol to improve the 
conductivity of a different gel applied to each sensor to capture the signal with 
reduced noise. The eye tracker doesn’t require any sensors due to the fact that data 
is recorded directly from the eye movements and changes compared to a reference 
state recorded right before each trial. The room’s light in which the experiments 
were conducted was controlled and constant to reduce inter-subject variations. 
Since all the participants answered surveys after each trial the removal of the 
sensors from the hand was allowed (e.g., the PPG sensor) to facilitate writing and 
were then reapplied before the start of the following trial.  
Figure 4.1: Neuroscan’s (black) and BiosignalsPlux’s sensors (white) positioned 
for ECG data recording. In this image it is also possible to see the Sensatron 
equipment on the diaphragm. 

BASE: Protocol Definition - Study 0 6  
 
Figure 4.2: BiosignalsPlux’s sensors (white) positioned for EDA measuring. 
The index finger’s sensor measures PPG and is connected to the Sensatron 
equipment.  
Figure 4.3: Sensatron’s sensors positioned to measure ECG and ICG with the 
aid of two sensors displayed in Figure 4.4. 


BASE: Protocol Definition - Study 0 7  
 
Figure 4.4: Sensatron’s sensors positioned in the neck to measure ECG and ICG 
with the aid of the sensors from Figure 4.3. In this figure the participant is using a 
cap with sensors that is part of the Neuroscan’s equipment for EEG measuring. 
The overall experimental set up is displayed in figure 4.5. Neuroscan’s equipment 
was connected to a pc mainly responsible for the EEG acquisition which was 
connected to the computer that controls the overall experiment set up and sent its 
triggers through a parallel port. The eye tracker and the BiosignalsPlux hub were 
also connected to their own computers responsible for the data acquisitions which 
communicated with the computer that controlled the set up through a TCP/IP 
connection. Finally, the Sensatron wasn’t directly controlled by any computer. 
Instead, the synchronization of this device with the remaining systems was 
performed in two phases: 
A pre-synchronization step was performed to align the ”bip” sounds (that act like 
triggers) produced by the computer responsible for the stimulus, which were 
captured by the microphones of this device with the remaining triggers; 
The signals captured by this device were then synchronized using the RR 
intervals of the collected ECG and the RR intervals of the ECG collected by the 
other devices. 
The synchronization procedure using the ECG signals is explained in detail in 
[34]. 


BASE: Protocol Definition - Study 0 8  
 
Figure 4.5: Schematic representation of the experimental set up with its 
compo- nents and respective relations. 
The stimulus of the experiment was created with Psychtoolbox-3 software in 
Matlab and displayed by the computer that controls the experimental set up directly 
to the subject. This computer had 2 monitors, one that displayed the stimulus and 
another that was used to control the experiment, and a keyboard that allowed 
the user to skip to the next phase of the protocol if necessary (as explained in the 
next section). 
3. Experimental protocol 
The experiment started with an empty grey screen with a black cross in its center. 
During this phase, the participant focused on the cross in order to abstract himself 
from the environment and to adjust to the experimental environment. This phase 
lasted for 30 seconds and is considered the baseline phase of the experiment where 
the participant didn’t perform any activity. 
In the second phase, a text in natural language was displayed to the subject. In 
order to minimize the effort of the participant related to the reading task, it was 
defined that the text should be written in natural language.For this purpose, the 
selected text was based on Portuguese histories in a tentative to avoid fluctuations 
in measurements due to emotions triggered by the narrative. The duration of this 
step was 60 seconds and to ensure that the participant was reading during the whole 
time of the current step, the text had a higher estimated reading time and the 
participants were informed that, during this phase, they should read the text until 
it stopped displaying on the screen (60 seconds) and should not be concerned with 
reading the whole story. This activity serves as a reference phase for data analysis. 
The third phase was equal to the first one. It had the purpose of abstracting 
the participant from the previous step and avoid interfering with the next one. 
During the fourth phase, a code snippet in Java programming language was dis- 
played to the participants in order to be interpreted and analyzed. The duration of 
this phase was set to a maximum of 10 minutes, but it could be interrupted at any 
time, in case the participant concluded its analysis earlier. This possibility was in- 


BASE: Protocol Definition - Study 0 9  
cluded in the experiment in order to ensure that during this step the participant was 
always focused on the performed task and didn’t tamper the experiment objectives. 
The last phase was equal to first and third and served the purpose of allowing 
data recording after the main stimulus for residual information and avoided ending 
the experiment abruptly. 
This whole process, represented in Figure 4.6, was repeated 3 times.  
Figure 4.6: Schematic representation of the sequence of the main 
phases followed in the experimental protocol. 
3.1 Code snippets and  texts 
In each trial the code snippet displayed to the subject was different from the re- 
maining trials, as well as the code snippet complexity. A short description of the 
code snippets is provided below: 
The first program counts the number of values existing in a given array that fall 
within a given interval using a straightforward loop; 
The second one multiplies two numbers using the basic algorithm where every 
digit from one number is multiplied by every digit from the other number, from 
right to left. The numbers are given as strings and the algorithm includes converting 
the strings to byte arrays; 
The last program seeks occurrences of an integer cubic array inside a larger cubic 
array, trying to find the larger occurrence of the smaller one inside the larger one. 
The complexities of the 3 programs, according to different metrics, are displayed 
in Table 4.1. The first code, due to its overall inferior complexity, had a maximum 
dis- play time during phase four of 5 minutes while the other two codes had a 
maximum time of 10 minutes.  
Code snippet Number of 
lines 
Number of 
parameters 
Cyclomati 
c 
complexity 
1 13 3 3 
2 47 3 4 
3 49 4 15 

BASE: Protocol Definition - Study 0 10  
Table 4.1: Complexity of the 3 programs displayed according to 3 
distinct software engineering metrics. 
The displayed texts were also different in each trial to keep the reader engaged 
throughout the experiment. The texts (extracted from [35], [36] and [37]) and the 
codes displayed to the subjects during the experiments are presented in the 
Appendix C. 
3.2 Cautions 
During the trials participants tried to understand the code without the help of 
the supervisors in the room and without using any auxiliary tools, like pen and 
paper, for example. Participants also tried not move and talk during the trials to 
avoid interfering in the data recording. 
Before the start of the experiment a set of sensors had to be applied to the par- 
ticipant. These sensors were verified before each trial in order to ensure they were 
correctly positioned and acquiring the signals correctly. 
At the beginning of the experiment, that is, before the positioning of the sensors 
and preparation of the participant, all the participants were informed with a 
summary of the informed consent (Appendix A) and were asked to read the consent 
and sign it. In this consent, the experiment details and the participants’ rights were 
fully explained. After the end of the experiment the participants were rewarded 
with  a voucher from the SONAE group, valued at 50 euros, and were asked to 
sign a declaration stating they received the reward (Appendix B). 
3.3 Performance evaluation 
After every trial the participants answered two questionnaires. In the first 
question- naire the participants were asked to explain the general goal of the 
algorithm and what were the steps taken in the algorithm to accomplish its 
objective. The purpose of these questions was to ensure that the participants were 
focused on the activity and that there was mental effort involved during the trial. 
Participants didn’t have to completely understand the algorithms considering that 
the effort displayed during the process was the relevant part of the data recording. 
This assumption is based on the fact that the participant can make an effort to 
comprehend the algorithm and still don’t understand it by the end of the trial. 
Nevertheless, the responses were analysed to make sure that there was a minimum 
comprehension of the displayed codes. This document, as well as the document 
with the responses used as reference for evaluation, are in Appendix D. 
The second questionnaire was based on the NASA’s task load index [38] and had 
4 questions in which participants had to rate the levels of mental effort, task 
comple- tion, temporal demand and frustration, (opposed to the 6 parameters 
presented in 
[38] that also regarded physical effort) from 1 to 6 (in NASA’s version 
participants have a scale from 1 to 20 that was too large for this experiment) 
regarding the trial. In this document the participant chose which parameter was 
thought to be more relevant during the trial from groups of two. These responses 
could be readjusted by the participants after the completion of all trials in case the 
perceived complexity of the first two programs changed at the end of the 
experiment. The purpose of this survey was to access the complexity of the 3 
algorithms in the perspective of each participant and its most prominent factors in 
the subjective workload. These results are later compared with the algorithms 

BASE: Protocol Definition - Study 0 11  
actual complexity according to the complexity measures mentioned before while 
the perceived relevancy of the factors will be used in future studies. The document 
used for this purpose can be found in Appendix D.3. 
Between each trial, it was not defined a limited amount of time to complete the 
survey. Since in these phases the participant was not performing any specific task 
of code inspection and no signals were being recorded, it can be considered as a 
moment of relaxation and rest, which can provide a clearer separation between 
trials (avoiding them influencing each other) and strengthen the conclusions of the 
study. 
The estimated duration of the experiment was 2 hour 
4. Data structure 
For every participant’s trial there is a Matlab file containing its associated infor- 
mation regarding the collected signals making a total of 90 matlab files (3 trials 
for 30 participants). Each file contains a cell array that is organized in 4 columns 
with a varying number of rows that contains a structure array in each cell.  The 
first column has information about the BiosignalsPlux system in the third row and 
the EDA and ECG signals recorded from this equipment in the first and second 
rows, respectively. The second column with 25 rows contains information related 
to the Sensatron system. The recorded microphone data is in row 1 and 2, ECG 
in row 3, ICG in row 18 and PPG in row 21, 22 and 23. This system has other 
sensors implemented that weren’t used in this study that have their information 
in the remaining rows. In the third column, the first row contains the EEG data 
and the second one the information from the Neuroscan’s equipment. Finally, the 
fourth and last column retains the SMI eye tracker recorded information in the first 
row and equipment’s specific settings in the second one. Information related to the 
synchronization triggers are saved in the last row of each column associated with 
the respective system. A simplified representation of this structure is displayed in 
Table 4.2.

Protocol Definition - Study 0.1 3  
1. Overview 
The experiment included 20 participants and all the subjects had experience in the 
Java programming language. There was a screening process that included an  
interview to assign a programming level skill in Java to each participant as 
“Intermediate” , “advanced” and “Expert”. 
The experimental setup covered a comprehensive set of sensors, including an EEG 
(Electroencephalography) with a 64 -channel cap, a Sensatron to obtain ECG 
(Electrocardiography) and PPG (Photoplethysmogram), a ‘watch’ (embrace plus) to 
get the EDA (Electrodermal activity) and BVP (Blood Volume Pulse), and 
Eye-tracking with Pupillography to get the information of the eye (gaze point, pupil 
diameter, et al.). The signals from all these sources were synchronized in a common 
time base to allow consistent cross-analysis. 
Experiments involved the compression of 4 small Java programs that had different 
complexities according to software complexity metrics like lines of code (LoC) and 
cyclomatic complexity(V(g)). The programs were carefully designed to keep the 
programming style consistent and avoid that math or algorithm from posing extra 
difficulties to the participants, not directly related to the code complexity. All the 
participants performed the experiments in the same room, without distractions, noise 
or the presence of people unrelated to the experiments. 
The study was approved by the university. All experiments were performed per 
relevant guidelines and regulations. Informed consent was signed by all participants. 
1.1 Announcement 
Experiments were mainly promoted through e -mail and face-to-face talks with 
students and other potential participants, including PhD and master students who are 
majoring in software engineering. During this phase, potential participants were made 
aware of the overall goal of the experiment: “Assess whether different levels of 
cognitive stress induced during software inspection/reading can be captured and 
quantified using autonomic nervous system activity manifestations captured using 
wearable sensors”. 
1.2 Screening interview 
People who showed interest in participating in the research were interviewed. 
Throughout this interview, subjects were asked some questions regarding 
demographics, contacts, profession, programming experience, and availability. 
Interviewers did also take notes related to the overall motivation of the person, 
regarding the experiment, which could impact the results of the research. All 
information regarding the participants has been managed according to the new 
legislation of data protection, GDPR. 
During the interview, information regarding the location and estimated duration: “2 
hours 30 minutes” was disclosed. It was also important that the subjects were aware 
that the experiments involved product application on hair for the EEG setup and that 
they were not eligible for the experiment if they had any kind of implanted cardio 
device. 
Subjects didn’t need any type of preparation before the experiment. 

Protocol Definition - Study 0.1 4  
1.3 Screening 
The goal was to have around 40 subjects, balanced in gender and proficiency in 
Java programming. To distinguish the proficiency of the candidates, the following 
criteria were applied: 
● Expert Professional software developers that worked in the field for more 
than 3 years or had more than 15000 lines of Java language programmed in 
the last 3 months or more than 10000 Java lines written in their biggest 
program or teachers that had more than 20000 lines of Java language 
programmed in the last 3 months or more than 15000 Java lines written in 
their biggest program; 
● Advanced Professional software developers that worked in the field for  
more than 1 year or had more than 10000 lines of Java language programmed 
in the last 3 months or more than 5000 Java lines written in their biggest 
program or students and teachers that had more than 10000 lines of Java 
language programmed in the last 3 months or more than 5000 Java lines are 
written in their biggest program; 
● Intermediate Students or teachers that had more than 5000 lines (and less 
than 10000 lines) of Java language programmed in the last 3 months or more 
than 3000 Java lines (and less than 5000 lines) written in their biggest 
program; 
 
Subjects that did not fulfill these requirements or had a history of heart diseases, 
mental health issues or implanted cardio devices were not eligible for the  
experiments. 
 
1.4 Recruitment 
From the 67 interviewed candidates, 35 participants were selected to participate 
in the experiments. Although gender and proficiency balance was established as a 
primary objective due to the insufficient number of “expert” and female candidates it 
wasn’t possible to achieve it. The group of participants included 28 men and 7 
women. 
 
2. Experimental setup 
In this experiment, four different data recording systems were used to record 
different signals: 
 
● Neuroscan’s equipment is composed of a 64-channel cap that connects 
to an amplifier headbox that is attached to a system unit that 
communicates through Bluetooth with a computer that controls the 
equipment with Neuroscan’s Scan 4.4 software. This system records 
EEG and ECG signals at a 1000 Hz sample frequency; 

Protocol Definition - Study 0.1 5  
● Tobii Eye Tracker 5L was positioned under the monitor that displayed 
the stimulus and allowed the collection of pupillography and eye 
movement tracking at 120 Hz and was calibrated by the Configuration 
Application software, recording was via Matlab. Image sampling rate 
and gaze frequency:133Hz, non-interlaced gaze at 33Hz. 
(https://help.tobii.com/hc/en-us/articles/360012483818-Specifications-fo 
r-Eye-Tracker-5) 
● Sensatron equipment developed by Philips that was used to measure 
ECG at 250 Hz, ICG at 125 Hz, and PPG at 62.5 Hz. This equipment has 
a memory card and a button that starts and ends the recording therefore 
no software was used to control the acquisition of these signals. 
● EmbracePLUS equipment like a watch that was worn on the 
participant’s hand and connected with the phone care app, then you can 
get the data (BVP—Blood Volume Pulse and EDA—Electrodermal 
Activity at 1 - 4 Hz, PPG at 26 Hz – 208 Hz) from the care portal. 
Figure 4.1 shows the data collection process of EmbracePLUS. 
(https://manuals.empatica.com/ehmp/careportal/user_manual/v2.3e/en 
.pdf) 
 
 
Figure 4.1: The data collection process of EmbracePLUS 

Protocol Definition - Study 0.1 6  
 
Figure 4.2: The sensors’ positioning of the different data recording equipment in 
the real situation. 
The sensors’ positioning of the different data recording equipment is displayed in 
Figures 4.2, 4.3, 4.4 and 4.5. In Figure 4.5 the 64-channel cap used to record EEG is 
placed on a participant. This process requires special preparation in which the 
participant's scalp is cleaned with abrasive gel and alcohol to improve the  
conductivity of a different gel applied to each sensor to capture the signal with 
reduced noise. The eye tracker doesn’t require any sensors because data is recorded 
directly from the eye movements and changes compared to a reference state recorded 
right before each trial. The room’s light in which the experiments were conducted was 
controlled and constant to reduce inter-subject variations. 
For the EmbracePlus, according to the instructions for the device, it can be worn on 
the participant's wrist all the time, and the corresponding data can be obtained later 
according to the task time stamp. 
Since all the participants answered surveys after each trial the removal of the 
sensors from the hand was allowed (e.g., the PPG sensor) to facilitate writing. It was 
then reapplied before the start of the following trial. 
 
Figure 4.3: The index finger’s sensor measures PPG and is connected to the 
Sensatron equipment. 


Protocol Definition - Study 0.1 7  
 
Figure 4.4: Sensatron’s sensors positioned to measure ECG and ICG with the 
aid of two sensors displayed (total has 4 sensors, another two shown in Figure  
4.5). In the real experiment, the yellow small box which include a memory card 
and a button that starts and ends the recording, fixed on the desk displayed in Figure 
4.2. 
Figure 4.5: Sensatron’s sensors positioned in the neck to measure ECG and 
ICG with the aid of the sensors from Figure 4.4. In this figure, the participant is 
using a cap with sensors that are part of the Neuroscan’s equipment for EEG 
measuring. 


Protocol Definition - Study 0.1 8  
3. Experimental protocol 
The experiment started with an empty grey screen with a black cross in its center. 
During this phase, the participant focused on the cross to abstract himself from the 
environment and adjust to the experimental environment. This phase lasted for 30 
seconds and is considered the baseline phase of the experiment, where the participant 
didn’t perform any activity. 
In the second phase, a text in natural language was displayed to the subject. To 
minimize the effort of the participant related to the reading task, it was defined that  
the text should be written in natural language. For this purpose, the selected text was 
based on some fairy tales in a tentative to avoid fluctuations in measurements due to 
emotions triggered by the narrative. The duration of this step was 60 seconds and to 
ensure that the participant was reading during the whole time of the current step, the 
text had a higher estimated reading time and the participants were informed that, 
during this phase, they should read the text until it stopped displaying on the screen 
(60 seconds) and should not be concerned with reading the whole story. This activity 
serves as a reference phase for data analysis. 
The third phase was equal to the first one. It had the purpose of abstracting the 
participant from the previous step and avoiding interfering with the next one. 
During the fourth phase, a code snippet in Java programming language was 
displayed to the participants to be interpreted and analyzed. The duration of this phase 
was set to a maximum of 10 minutes, but it could be interrupted after a minimum of 1 
minute (to ensure data is available), in case the participant concluded its analysis 
earlier. This possibility was included in the experiment to ensure that during this step 
the participant was always focused on the performed task and didn’t tamper with the 
experiment objectives. 
The last phase was equal to the first and third and served the purpose of allowing 
data recording after the main stimulus for residual information and avoided ending the 
experiment abruptly. 
This whole process, represented in Figure 4.6, was repeated 4 times. 
 
Figure 4.6: Schematic representation of the sequence of the main phases 
followed in the experimental protocol. 

Protocol Definition - Study 0.1 9  
3.1 Code snippets and texts 
In each trial, the code snippet displayed to the subject was different from the 
remaining trials, as well as the code snippet complexity. A short description of the 
code snippets is provided below: 
● The first program asks the user for a string and then it analyses the string to see 
if it is a palindrome. It works by generating a reversed string and then 
comparing the original string to the new one; 
● The second one asks the user for a set of integer values which are stored in an 
array. Then, it removes duplicate values from that array. It works by scanning 
the array and, for each element of the array, it searches for further occurrences 
of its value in subsequent elements, removing them if there are any; 
● The third program fills an array with integer values obtained from the user. 
Then it sorts the array using an iterative implementation of the quicksort 
algorithm. This implementation uses an array as a stack to obtain the same 
effect as the recursive version of the algorithm; 
● The last program generates a random cell population into a 10x10 
2-dimensional array. Then it generates the next cell generation using Conway's 
game of life rules, storing it in a second array. 
The time during which the program code is shown to participants is the same for all 
programs: 10 minutes. However, participants are allowed to move to the next program 
before the time is up. The complexities of the 4 programs, according to different 
metrics, are displayed in Table 4.1. It is worth noting that each task consists of a Java 
class having a single “main” method (function). The line with class declaration and  
the import directive are not considered for the metrics shown in Table 4.1., as they are 
not part of the algorithm per se, and the focus of the participant analysis is the main 
method. The number of parameters is always 1 as it is the command line argument 
mandated by the Java language for console applications. These arguments were not 
used at all by any of the programs. 
 
Code snippet Number of lines Number of 
parameters 
Cyclomatic 
complexity 
1 25 1 5 
2 25 1 7 
3 47 1 8 
4 33 1 9 
Table 4.1: Complexity of the 4 programs displayed according to 3 distinct software 
engineering metrics. 
The displayed texts were also different in each trial to keep the reader engaged 
throughout the experiment (We translate the texts into different languages, there are 
four languages: Portuguese, English, Persian, and Mandarin). 
3.2 Cautions 
During the trials, participants tried to understand the code without the help of the 
supervisors in the room and without using any auxiliary tools, like pen and paper, for 
example. Participants also tried not to move and talk during the trials to avoid 
interfering with the data recording. 

Protocol Definition - Study 0.1 10  
Before the start of the experiment, a set of sensors had to be applied to the 
participant. These sensors were verified before each trial to ensure they were correctly 
positioned and acquired the signals correctly. 
At the beginning of the experiment, that is, before the positioning of the sensors  
and preparation of the participant, all the participants were informed with a summary 
of the informed consent and were asked to read the consent and sign it. In this  
consent, the experiment details and the participants’ rights were fully explained. 
Our experiment consists of three trials, and after each trial is finished, participants 
are asked to fill out two questionnaires (Two questions and NASA_TLX) in papers. 
We need to calibrate the eye tracker before the next trial begins in case some errors  
are produced by that (This part has already been included in Matlab). 
3.3 Performance evaluation 
There are two questionnaires for the participants' answers (Appendix A). In the first 
questionnaire, participants should answer them after each trial. There are two parts. 
The first contains two questions, the participants were asked to explain the general 
goal of the algorithm and what were the steps taken in the algorithm to accomplish its 
objective. The purpose of these questions was to ensure that the participants were 
focused on the activity and that there was mental effort involved during the trial. 
Participants didn’t have to completely understand the algorithms considering that the 
effort displayed during the process was the relevant part of the data recording. This 
assumption is based on the fact that the participant can make an effort to comprehend 
the algorithm and still don’t understand it by the end of the trial. Nevertheless, the 
responses were analyzed to make sure that there was a minimum comprehension of 
the displayed codes. This document, as well as the document with the responses, is 
used as a reference for evaluation. 
The second was based on NASA’s task load index [1] and had 3 questions in which 
participants had to rate the levels of mental effort, pressure with time, and task 
accomplishment, (as opposed to the 6 parameters presented in [1] that also regarded 
physical effort) from 1 to 6 (in NASA’s version participants have a scale from 1 to 20 
that was too large for this experiment) regarding the trial. In this document, the 
participant chose which parameter was thought to be more relevant during the trial 
from groups of two. These responses could be readjusted by the participants after the 
completion of all trials in case the perceived complexity of the first two programs 
changed at the end of the experiment. The purpose of this survey was to assess the 
complexity of the 4 algorithms from the perspective of each participant and its most 
prominent factors in the subjective workload. These results are later compared with 
the algorithms’ actual complexity according to the complexity measures mentioned 
before while the perceived relevancy of the factors will be used in future studies. 
The second questionnaire is NASA for the whole experiment and each participant 
is to answer once and choose the most relevant factors they think in this experiment. 
Between each trial, it was not defined a limited amount of time to complete the 
survey. Since in these phases the participant was not performing any specific task of 
code inspection and no signals were being recorded, it can be considered as a moment 
of relaxation and rest, which can provide a clearer separation between trials (avoiding 
them influencing each other) and strengthen the conclusions of the study. 
The estimated duration of the experiment was 2.5 hours. 

Protocol Definition - Study 0.1 11

BASE: Protocol Definition - Study 1 3  
1. Introduction 
The main hypothesis of the BASE project is to research software bugs in a new 
perspective using neuroscience, physiological response and software reliability 
engineering in a tight interdisciplinary approach to understand the brain mechanisms 
involved in error making and error discovery, focusing on software programming and 
code inspection activities, and evaluate the possibilities of using the findings to improve 
software quality through a new comprehensive biofeedback augmented software 
engineering approach. 
To achieve the goal of assessing the coupling between the neural activation patterns 
related to SW bugs and the ANS, two distinct data collection studies will be organised. 
In both cases, experienced programmers (master students and professional 
programmers from SW companies) will participate in a voluntary basis. 
The present document presents the protocol for the first study of the project, which 
focus on the assessment of brain activity and physiological responses during software 
inspection tasks. 
2. Study Objectives 
The main goals of the present study are: 
1. Research the neural network associated to human error making and error discovery 
during software inspection activities, using functional magnetic resonance imaging 
(fMRI) and electroencephalography (EEG), to understand the mental conditions 
that lead to a bug and reveal the neural patterns associated to the “eureka moment” 
of finding a bug. 
2. Establish correlation models between the neuronal activation patterns associated to 
bug scenarios and the physiological response of programmers that can be monitored 
by currently available smart watches and similar wearable devices. Examples of 
such signals that will be used in the current study are the ECG (electrocardiogram), 
PPG (photoplethysmogram) and EDA (electrodermal activity). The main idea is to 
monitor physiologic responses to emotional and cognitive stress and concentration 
states (e.g., variations in the heart rate, breathing rhythm, secretion increasing by 
the sweat glands, etc.), considering also stress/concentration related behavioural 
information that can be inferred from the typical programming environment, such 
as mouse and keyboard activity, eye movement, and facial expression. 
3. Subject enrolment 
The current study will be mainly promoted via e-mail and personal contact with the 
potential participants. During this phase the participants will be informed about the 
objectives of the study and the onset of the experiments. 
It is expected to have a minimum of 35 to 40 volunteers. 
Each volunteer will be interviewed and screened in order to guarantee that he/she fits 
the study objectives as described in the following sections. 
The experiments of this study will be conducted in fMRI lab of the Institute of 
Nuclear Sciences Applied to Health (ICNAS) of the University of Coimbra. 

BASE: Protocol Definition - Study 1 4  
The study group should be balanced for gender. In order to be eligible for data 
collection, the subject shall not be under 18 years old. 
3.1 Interview 
All the subjects showing interest on participating in the current study will be 
interviewed in order to assess their demographic and biometric characteristics (e.g. 
age), professional status, programming experience, availability and motivation. All the 
collected information regarding the interviewed subjects will be managed according to 
the GDPR. 
During the interview, the subjects will be informed about the location and the total 
duration of the experiment. 
3.2 Screening 
The candidates of this study will be screened in order to assess their proficiency level. 
The proficiency level will be accessed using resorting on the score provided by two 
questionnaires: 
1. Programming experience questionnaire: the main objective of this 
questionnaire is to assess the volume of coding of the candidate in the last 3 
years. 
2. Technical questionnaire: the main objective of this questionnaire is to assess 
the coding skills of the candidate. This questionnaire is composed by 10 
questions, scored with 1 point for correct answer and 0 points for incorrect 
answer. The minimum possible score is 0 points and the maximum score is 
10 points. 
Candidates will be distributed into 4 main categories according to the results abtained 
in the abovementioned questionnaires: 
1. Not eligible for the experiments: Candidates with a score below 4 points 
2. Low-proficiency participants: Candidates with a score between 4 and 5. 
3. Mid-proficiency participants: Candidates with a score between 6 and 7. 
4. High-proficiency subjects: Candidates with a score between 8 and 10. 
 
Participants that do not fulfill the aforementioned are not eligible for the experiments. 
Participants with cardiac implanted devices, metallic prosthesis/implantations and/or 
with known mental conditions are not eligible for the experiments. 

BASE: Protocol Definition - Study 1 5  
4. Experimental setup 
The current study includes the simultaneous recording of several signals and images 
to access the neuronal and cardiovascular activity of the subjects. These are presented 
in the table below, as well as the corresponding recording equipment.
